# Valence: information for institutional reviewers

Valence is an independent, free project made for any learner. It is not affiliated with any school and there is no vendor relationship: no contract, no account manager, and no support agreement. An institution that chooses to point students to Valence does its own review. This page gives each reviewing department the facts it needs.

Each section states only what is true of the current version. The current version has the molecule explorer, the periodic table, fourteen labs (the standardization of a base by titration, reactions in aqueous solution, flame tests, calorimetry, the Group I cations, Le Chatelier's principle, the clock reaction, the iodination of acetone, red cabbage pH, measurement and density, freezing point depression, types of reactions, polarity and mixing, and endothermic and exothermic) and one mechanism timeline: hydrochloric acid and sodium hydrogen carbonate, step by step. What the labs show about hazards, and what they do not, is under Lab safety at the end of this page.

## IT and security

- **What it is:** a static website (HTML, CSS, JavaScript). There is no server-side code, database, or API.
- **Install:** none. It has been tested in Chromium (the engine behind Chrome and Edge), where scripted checks are run against the production build. Firefox and Safari have not been tested yet.
- **Accounts:** none. There is no sign-in of any kind.
- **Network:** the app requests only its own files, from the server it was loaded from. Fonts and all data are bundled; nothing is loaded from a third-party CDN and nothing is sent anywhere. Parts of the app (the molecule pages, the 3D viewer, the periodic table, each of the five labs, the mechanism timeline and its 3D scene) and some font files are fetched from those same files the first time they are needed.
- **Storage:** preferences and learning progress are stored in the browser's `localStorage` under keys beginning `valence:`. Nothing is stored anywhere else. The titration lab stores one entry, `valence:lab:titration`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the seed of the run, the mode, what the learner did at the bench and what they typed (the state of the glassware, each trial's additions, readings and molarities), and the learner's own average and relative deviation. Results (passed or not, percent error, causes) are worked out from that each time it is read and are not stored. The entry holds nothing that identifies the learner. The file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the seed, and the values the lab hides from the learner follow from the seed for anyone who reads the source code. An entry that cannot be read, or that was written by another version of the entry's format, is set aside under a key beginning `valence:backup:` and is not deleted; the lab's page then starts fresh and says, on that visit and until the page is reloaded or a new run is started, that a saved run was kept and that the export includes it. With the lab open in two tabs of one browser, each tab takes the record the other saves at the moment it is saved, so neither goes on from an old one; nothing is sent between them but the browser's own notice that the stored entry changed. If the browser refuses to store progress, the lab page and Settings say that progress will last for that session only. The aqueous reactions lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:aqueous`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for each of the ten stations whether its tube has been mixed, which tools have been used, the observation as it was last checked and the equation last checked in each of the three forms. It has no seed, because the stations are fixed, and it holds nothing that identifies the learner. Whether an entry is correct is worked out each time the record is read and is not stored. What a learner has chosen or built at a station and not yet checked is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored at all: a reload loses it. A stored entry that does not fit is set aside and reported as the titration lab's is, and two tabs take each other's saved record the same way. The flame test lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:flame`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, what the loop carries now (the names of the solutions it was dipped in since it was last cleaned), the answer last given to the closing question and, for each of the six stations, what the loop carried when it was last held in the flame there, which of the flame and the strip's lines and bands have been looked at since, and the three answers last checked with what the loop carried when they were made. It has no seed, and it holds nothing that identifies the learner. Whether an observation counts and whether it is correct are worked out each time the record is read and are not stored. What a learner has chosen and not yet checked is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The calorimetry lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:calorimetry`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the seed of the lab, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its three parts what the learner did to the cup, as counts of the logger's readings (when the lid was put on, when the stirrer was started, whether the logger was started, when the change was started, and how many readings have been taken), which readings of the graph have been looked at, the worksheet's entries as they were made, and whether the worksheet has been checked. It holds nothing that identifies the learner. The masses and temperatures of a run, which metal the unknown piece is, every reading of the logger, whether an entry follows and whether a run counts are worked out from that each time it is read and are not stored. As with the titration lab, the file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the seed, and what the lab hides from the learner follows from the seed for anyone who reads the source code. What a learner has typed and not yet entered is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The Group I cations lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:cations`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the seed of the lab, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its two parts the list of what the learner did in it, in order: each reagent added, the heating, each use of the centrifuge and of litmus paper, each look at a part of a tube, what was recorded as seen, each tile put in a place of the flow chart or taken out, each check, each pouring off, and the ions named. It holds nothing that identifies the learner, and nothing that says which ions a sample holds: what each tube holds and shows, whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, and whether a run counts are worked out from the seed and that list each time it is read, and are not stored. As with the titration lab, the file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the seed, and which ions each unknown holds follows from the seed for anyone who reads the source code. What a learner has chosen and not yet recorded is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The Le Chatelier's principle lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:le-chatelier`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its four systems the list of what the learner did in it, in order: each prediction, each press of the button that makes a change, each look at a part of a tube, what was recorded as seen, each answer, and the check. It has no seed, because nothing in the lab is drawn by chance, and it holds nothing that identifies the learner. What each tube shows, whether what was recorded is what the tube showed, whether an answer is right and whether a run counts are worked out from that list each time it is read, and are not stored. What a learner has chosen and not yet recorded is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The clock reaction lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:clock-reaction`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds a seed, which is a random number that fixes the room's temperature, the baths' and the small scatter in each time; the mode; whether the lab is in progress or completed; for every attempt at each of its eight runs, what was done to the flasks and how the watch was stopped, with what it read and, for a watch stopped by hand, how many milliseconds after the liquid changed the press came; and for each working of its two notebooks, what was entered, which places on the graph's line were looked at, and whether it was checked. That delay is a measure of how quickly the learner pressed a button on this page; it is kept so that the time recorded can be worked out again, the Record tab shows it, and it identifies nobody. A run whose watch is still running is not stored. The time of each run, whether a run counts, whether an entry follows and whether a part is done are worked out each time the record is read, and are not stored. What is typed and not yet entered is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The iodination of acetone lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:iodination-of-acetone`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds what the clock reaction lab's entry holds, for four runs and one notebook: a seed, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, how the watch of every attempt at each run was stopped, with what it read and, for a watch stopped by hand, how many milliseconds after the colour had gone the press came, and what was entered in each working of the notebook. A run whose watch is still running is not stored, and how much colour was drawn at any moment is not stored. The red cabbage pH lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:red-cabbage-ph`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds a seed, which fixes the pH of each household thing within its range; the mode; whether the lab is in progress or completed; for the rack of thirteen buffers, whether the indicator is in and whether the rack has been looked at, the three answers and whether they have been checked; for each of the fourteen household things, whether the indicator is in its tube and whether the tube has been looked at, the estimate made by eye and whether the thing was called acid, neutral or basic, how the meter was last read in it (with a rinsed probe, or with one last in which other thing), whether Guided has warned there of a probe not rinsed, and whether it has been checked; which thing the probe was last in while it has not been rinsed; which things count, in the order they came to; the two answers of the part on what a unit of pH means, and whether they have been checked; and how many times each of these was checked. It holds no pH and no colour, and nothing that identifies the learner: what a tube shows, what the meter reads, whether an answer is right and whether a thing counts are worked out from the seed and the record each time it is read, and are not stored. As with the titration lab, the file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the seed, and what the lab hides from the learner follows from the seed for anyone who reads the source code. A tube chosen and not yet recorded is kept by the page while it is open and is not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The measurement and density lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:measurement-and-density`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds a seed, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its three parts where the eye is against the liquid in the cylinder (above it, level with it or below it), which steps of the bench have been taken (the weighing, the pouring, and the pieces slid into the water or dropped in), each reading and each worked-out number as it was typed, each level with where the eye was when it was read, the name given to the unknown, and whether the notebook has been checked. It holds nothing that identifies the learner. What each instrument shows, which liquid or metal an unknown is, whether a reading is right, whether an entry follows and whether a run counts are worked out from the seed and the record each time it is read, and are not stored. The file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the seed, as the titration lab's does. What is typed and not yet entered, and whether a closer look is open, are kept by the page while it is open and are not stored. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way, and two tabs take each other's saved record. The freezing point depression lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:freezing-point-depression`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds a seed, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its three parts what was done to the cup, as counts of the logger's readings (when the lid went on, when the stirrer was started, when the solute went in, when the cup was strained and weighed), which solute a run of part 3 dissolved, which readings of the graph were looked at, and what was entered in the notebook. No reading of the logger or of the balance is stored, and nothing that is judged: each is worked out from the seed when the record is read. The types of reactions lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:types-of-reactions`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its five stations the list of things the learner did in it, in order: that the station's one action was done, each part of a vessel that was looked at, what was recorded as seen of each vessel, the kind of reaction that was said, each equation that was built, as the tiles on each side with the number and the state given to each, the order given for the three metals, and that the station was checked. It has no seed, since nothing in the lab is drawn by chance. What a vessel shows, whether an answer is right and whether a run counts are worked out from the lab's record each time the entry is read, and are not stored. The polarity and mixing lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:polarity-and-mixing`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for each of its seventeen tubes how many times it has been set up again, which liquid there is more of in a pair or that the liquid and the solid are in, whether the tube has been shaken, has stood and has been looked at, what was recorded of it (one layer or two and which liquid is on top, or whether the solid is gone), and whether it was checked; and for its third part the family each liquid was sorted into, the two answers, and whether the part was checked. It has no seed, since nothing in the lab is drawn by chance. What a tube shows, a liquid's family, whether an answer is right and whether a tube counts are worked out from the lab's record each time the entry is read, and are not stored. The endothermic and exothermic lab stores one entry in the same way, `valence:lab:endothermic-and-exothermic`, and nothing before the learner presses Start. It holds a seed drawn in the browser, from which each run's starting temperature and mass of salt are worked out, the mode, whether the lab is in progress or completed, and for every run of each of its two salts what was done to the cup as counts of the logger's steps (when the lid went on, when the stirrer was started, whether the logger was started, when the salt went in and how far the logger has run), which readings were looked at, the six entries of its notebook and whether the notebook was checked; and for its third part the salt answered to each of the two questions and whether the answers were checked. The logger's readings, whether an entry follows, whether a run counts and whether an answer is right are worked out from the seed and the lab's record each time the entry is read, and are not stored. The mechanism timeline stores one entry, `valence:mechanism:hcl-nahco3`, and nothing until the learner does something on its page: opening the page stores nothing, and neither does following a link to one of its chapters. The entry holds the mode, whether the timeline is in progress or completed, where on the timeline the learner last was (a number from 0 to 1), and the first answer given to each of its four questions, as the name of the answer chosen. Whether an answer was right is worked out each time the record is read and is not stored. The entry holds nothing that identifies the learner. It is written at each action. While the timeline plays it is written when it enters a chapter and when playing stops, the page being left among the ways it stops, and not at every frame. "Start again" on the timeline's page writes a fresh run over it, after asking. An entry that does not fit is set aside and reported in the same way.
- **Inline script:** besides its script files, the page carries one short script written into `index.html` by the build. It reads the stored theme and reduced-motion preferences and applies them before the page is first drawn, so that the dark theme does not flash light on every load. It requests nothing and stores nothing. A host whose Content Security Policy forbids inline scripts can still serve Valence: the app applies the same preferences once it has loaded, a moment later.
- **Shared computers:** on a shared browser profile, the next person can see the previous learner's saved progress. Settings has a "Clear my progress" button, and clearing site data in the browser has the same effect.
- **Size of the molecule library:** when the library went from 31 to 101 entries (2026-10-09), the list of molecules that the first page loads (names, formulas, topics) grew from 3.9 kB to 12.9 kB, and the list of sources, which the first page also loads, by 72 entries (154 to 226). Measured in Chromium on the Home page, the scripts of the first load went from 545 kB to 572 kB before compression, which is 168 kB to 171 kB as a server sends them with gzip. The structures themselves, 105 files, are not in the first load: they are in one file of 202 kB (32 kB with gzip; it was 44 kB and 7 kB), fetched when a molecule's page or the mechanism timeline is first opened. The library page lists from the small list and no longer fetches them.
- **Self-hosting:** run `npm ci && npm run build`, then serve the `dist/` folder from any static web server. No rewrite rules are needed, and it works from a sub-path.
- **Source code:** public under the MIT licence.

## Legal and privacy

- **Personal data:** Valence collects none. It has no accounts, no forms that ask for personal information, no analytics, and no cookies.
- **Data leaving the device:** none. Progress never leaves the learner's browser unless the learner uses "Export my progress" to download their own file.
- **Education records:** because nothing is collected or transmitted, Valence creates and holds no records about any student. Whether and how an institution uses Valence under FERPA or similar rules is the institution's determination; this page states the facts for that review and is not legal advice.
- **Children's privacy (COPPA) and GDPR:** the same facts apply. No personal information is collected from anyone, of any age, in any location.
- **Third-party data:** chemistry data is cited to its sources on the About page, which acknowledges PubChem (National Library of Medicine), CIAAW and NIST, and names the publishers the titration lab's constants, primer and hazard information come from. The source repository also keeps copies of the pages the periodic table was built from, as a research record; one of them, a Wikipedia page, is under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike licence and is attributed beside the copy. The copies are not part of the built app. For the titration lab the repository keeps four copies (two Wikipedia revisions, attributed beside them, and one data file each from NOAA and PubChem). Every other page the lab cites, the safety data sheets among them, was read and is not copied: the research record says what each was read to print, and where. For the aqueous reactions lab the repository keeps one copy, a data file from PubChem; its other 30 sources and its 25 safety data sheets were read and are not kept. For the mechanism timeline the repository keeps no copy of any source. For the Group I cations lab it keeps none of that lab's own: one of the lab's 22 sources, the table of standard atomic weights, is the copy the periodic table's record keeps, and the other 21 and the 49 documents read for its hazards were read and are not kept.
- **No quotation in the titration lab's text.** The lab's primer, its hazard notes and its messages are written in Valence's own words. Two things are stored as their sources print them, because they are data: the digits of a cited figure, and a hazard statement with its code. A test holds the primer, the hazard notes and the research record's rows for the safety data sheets to repeating no run of six words of the pages they rest on. That test needs a fingerprint of those pages which is not kept in the repository, because stretches of the pages could be rebuilt from it. So the test runs only on a machine where the fingerprint has been made, and is reported as skipped everywhere else, the automated build on the code host included. It was run when the words were written; nothing re-runs it for every change. The aqueous reactions lab has a test of the same kind over its primer, its stations' notes and hints and its two cautions, which runs, and is skipped, on the same terms; its reagents' hazard notes are not under that test. The sentences written into that lab's screens (headings, button names, refusals) are Valence's own and rest on no page. The mechanism timeline has a test of the same kind over its chapters' text, its questions with their answers and feedback, its notes and what it says of each energy, on the same terms. The pages it is compared against are the paper its acid constant is from, two abstracts that were read as a check, and a listing of constants; none of them is kept. The tables of 1982 are a scan with no text to compare against. The flame test lab has a test of the same kind over its primer, its stations' notes, what they say the strip leaves out, the reasons stored with the model's own choices and the research record's rows, on the same terms; it was run, with the fingerprint, on 2026-10-09. The six bottles' hazard notes are not under that test. A script that is not in the repository compared the six with the text of the documents they rest on when one of them was changed on 2026-10-08, and found no run of six words repeated. The calorimetry lab has a test of the same kind over its primer, with what each paragraph says it rests on, its parts' notes, the notes on its two enthalpies and on water, the working of each value that is worked out, the reasons stored with the model's own choices and the research record's rows, on the same terms; it was run, with the fingerprint, on 2026-10-09, on the machine the lab was built on. The hazard notes of that lab's reagents are not under that test. The Group I cations lab has a test of the same kind over its primer, with what each paragraph says it rests on, its record's notes and rules and its research record's rows, and a second over every sentence its page can say, on the same terms; both were run, with the fingerprint, on the machine the lab was built on, the second again on 2026-10-10 after four sentences were added to the page. The hazard notes of its bottles are not under either. The Le Chatelier's principle lab has the same two tests: one over its primer, with what each paragraph says it rests on, its record's notes and rules, the working of each value that is worked out, the reasons stored with the model's own choices and its research record's rows, and one over every sentence its page can say. Both were run, with the fingerprint, on 2026-10-10 and again on 2026-10-11 on the machine the lab was built on, the second after the page's screens added a system's short name and the two words beside a bath. The hazard notes of its bottles are not under either. The clock reaction lab's text is held in the same way by two tests: one over its primer, its record's notes, the working of each value that is worked out and the reasons stored with the model's own choices, and one over every sentence its page can say. The fingerprint they compare against is not in the repository, and where it is absent they are skipped. Both were run with it, rebuilt from the 47 pages saved for the lab, on 2026-10-11 on the machine the lab was built on, after the screens added two sentences to the page's words. The iodination of acetone lab's text is held in the same way by two tests, over its primer and record and over every sentence its page can say, against a fingerprint of the 27 documents its record and its hazard record list, which is not in the repository. Both were run with it on 2026-10-11 on the machine the screens were built on, after the screens added three sentences to the page's words and changed one, and passed.
- **Licence:** MIT.

## Accessibility services

Valence has not had an accessibility audit, and this page makes no claim of conformance to WCAG or any other standard. This section lists what was built, how it was checked, and what has not been checked.

**How it was checked.** Scripts drove the production build in headless Chromium 153 (the engine behind Chrome and Edge) on Linux, using the keyboard only where the check is about the keyboard. Screenshots were taken in the light and dark themes at 1366×768 and 400×800 (the periodic table also at 2523×1290) and looked at by the AI coding assistant that built the screens; no person has reviewed them yet. The chemistry text (descriptions, names, measurements) comes from functions with automated tests.

**What was checked that way:**

- **Keyboard operation of the explorer.** With no mouse: reach the library from the menu, open a molecule, switch between the four views, turn and zoom the 3D model with the camera buttons, open the text description, select atoms and bonds from the Atoms and bonds list, open the "More about" and "Sources" parts of the cards, measure a distance and an angle, clear the selection, change the valence display level, switch between the neutral and zwitterion forms, and jump to another molecule with the search dialog (Ctrl+K, ⌘K, or the Search button in the menu). Focus moves to the page heading after each navigation (the periodic table has one exception, described next), returns to where it was when the search dialog is closed with Escape, and has a visible indicator on every stop in the molecule page's tab order.
- **The library's filter (added 2026-10-09, checked at 820×1180 and 1366×768, light theme).** The library page lists 101 molecules, so it has a search field labelled "Filter molecules" above the list. Typing a name or a formula narrows the list as it is typed; a status line under the field gives the count ("11 of 101 molecules") or says that nothing matches; a Clear button appears once something is typed, and Escape clears the field too. Both leave focus in the field. From the field, with the keyboard only: "caff" was typed, Tab pressed twice (Clear, then the one molecule left) and Enter opened it. The script put focus in the field itself; that Tab reaches the field from the top of the page was not tried, though it is an ordinary input in the page's order. A group with no match is left out, heading and all. Whether a screen reader reads the status line as it changes was not tested. On an element's card, a list of more than twelve molecules is folded under its count ("71 molecules of the library contain carbon"); it was opened with Enter on the card of carbon at 820×1180, with focus put on it by the script.
- **Keyboard operation of the periodic table.** With no mouse: reach the table from the library page, from Home, or from the search dialog; move between the elements; open an element and read its card; change the colouring and the highlight; open the list of all elements; jump to another element or to a molecule with the search dialog; and follow the link on a molecule's atom card to that element. The table is drawn as two grids, the main table and the two rows below it, which together are a single stop in the tab order. Inside them the arrow keys move to the nearest element in that direction, across the gap between the two grids; Home and End go to the ends of a row, and Ctrl+Home and Ctrl+End to the first and last element of the table; Enter or Space opens the element. An arrow key at an edge does nothing, and does not scroll the page. When focus leaves the grids, the stop stays on the selected element, or on the cell that last had focus when none is selected. Colouring and highlighting are two ordinary drop-down lists, and each chemical family in the legend is a toggle button. Every stop on the page has a visible focus indicator.
- **The table's exception to the focus rule.** Opening an element from the table, or from the list of all elements, leaves focus on the cell or link that was chosen, so the arrow keys can go on from there; a status line announces "Selected: Fluorine. Its details are below the table." The highlighted set is announced in a second status line, so that choosing an element does not read the highlight out again. A "Clear selection" link follows the table, and the element's card follows that. Reaching an element any other way (the search dialog, the link on an atom card, or the browser's Back button from another page) moves focus to the page heading as everywhere else. Opening elements one after another does not add to the browser history, so Back leaves the table in one step.
- **Names and alternatives in the periodic table.** Each cell's name gives the element's name, symbol, atomic number and chemical family, the value and unit when the table is coloured by a property ("no value listed" when there is none), and whether it is highlighted. The columns and rows of the main table have group and period headers. The cells of lanthanum and actinium are also described by a sentence saying that cerium to lutetium, or thorium to lawrencium, come next and are drawn below the main table. Below the table, "All 118 elements as a list" gives every element as a plain link in atomic-number order with the same value beside it, so a trend can be read without the grid or its colours. The shell diagram on an element's card is hidden from assistive technology and its electron counts are given as text. Every link, button, drop-down list and disclosure on the page has a text name.
- **Text alternatives.** The 3D model and the 2D Lewis drawing are each exposed as one image with a name ("3D ball and stick model of Water") and a description: the formula, the number of atoms and bonds, each central atom's geometry, and where the lone pairs are. The same description can be read on the page under "Text description". Every atom and bond is in the Atoms and bonds list by name, and the current selection and any measurement are written in a status line.
- **Reduced motion.** With the operating system set to reduce motion, or with Reduced motion set to On in Settings, the 3D model stops as soon as a drag is released; with motion allowed it coasts briefly. Nothing in the explorer moves by itself in either case. On the periodic table's page nothing moves. The only transition is the brief fade of the focus ring on the legend's buttons, as on buttons elsewhere, and it is off when motion is reduced.
- **Theme at load.** The light or dark theme (the learner's choice in Settings, or the operating system's) is in place before the page is first drawn. With the system set to light and the dark theme chosen, and the reverse, every frame Chromium painted from the first was in the chosen theme, on Home, Settings and the periodic table at 1366×768; so it was with nothing stored, with a stored value that was corrupt, and with storage blocked, where the system's theme is used. With storage that could be read but refused every write, the stored theme was in place in every frame sampled on Settings, in both directions, and Settings said that nothing is being saved. The browser is told which theme is in use (`color-scheme`), which is what it draws its own parts by; the drop-down lists and the scrollbars themselves were not looked at.
- **Colour is not the only signal.** A selected atom or bond has a tick in the list and a ring in the model, and is named in the status line; the current view and form have a tick. Elements are coloured in the model, and each atom's element is also in its name ("Oxygen 1") and on its card. In the periodic table the chemical family is named in the legend and in each cell's name as well as coloured; a highlighted set has a solid border and the rest lose their fill and take a dashed border, with no fading, so the text keeps its full contrast; a property's value is printed in the cell as well as shaded; an element with no value is hatched and named "no value listed"; the selected element has a double border.
- **Fit.** At 1366×768 and at 400×800 every one of the 118 elements was opened and the page never scrolled sideways. When the table does not fit at its smallest cell size (44 pixels, or 56 while a property's values are shown), it scrolls sideways inside its own box. At 400×800 every cell reached with the arrow keys was wholly inside that box and the window, with its focus outline. The name and value of the focused or pointed-at element are written in a strip under the box, which does not scroll sideways with the table; it follows whichever was used last, naming the focused element after a key press and the pointed-at element after the pointer moves. When a cell takes focus from the keyboard, the page scrolls as far as it must, at once and without animation, to bring that strip into the window with the cell: at 400×800 and at 360×640 the strip was in the window and named the focused element after Tab into the table and after every key press that followed, with and without a property's values shown. A click or a tap scrolls nothing: at 400×800 and at 1366×768 a cell half cut off by the edge of the box or of the window opened on the first click. The first key pressed after such a click (Enter, Space, an arrow or a letter) brings the cell and its outline wholly into view. A modifier pressed on its own (Shift, Control, Alt or the Meta key) scrolls nothing, so that a Shift+click opens the cell under the pointer; Shift alone does make Chromium draw the outline, which is then cut by the edge until the next key. An element opened from the search, the list or its address has its cell scrolled into view.
- **Names.** Every button, link, field and menu on the molecule page, with the search dialog open, has a text name.
- **Without WebGL.** If the browser cannot start the 3D model, the page says so, and the 2D Lewis view, the list, the cards and the text description still work.

**What has not been checked:**

- No person has tested Valence with a screen reader. The roles, names and descriptions above were checked by script, not by listening to them.
- No browser other than Chromium: not Firefox, not Safari, and no phone or tablet browser. Touch has not been tested.
- No person with a colour-vision deficiency has looked at the periodic table. Its ten family colours were chosen by a script that simulates three kinds of colour blindness and keeps the colours as far apart as it can; that is a simulation, not a test with people. Two pairs of families (transition metals and noble gases; post-transition metals and noble gases) are the closest even for full colour vision.
- Text contrast has not been measured on the rendered pages, with one exception: the text of the periodic table's cells against their fill, measured by script in Chromium at 1366×768 and 400×800. It is at least 9.64:1 on the family colours in the light theme and 7.01:1 in the dark theme, and at least 7.96:1 (light) and 6.56:1 (dark) on the property shading.
- Browser zoom and enlarged text have not been tested. Forced-colour (high contrast) mode was tried for the periodic table only, in Chromium's emulation and not with a Windows contrast theme: the borders that mark selection, highlight and focus remain when the colours are removed, and so does the hatching of an element with no value. The family colours and the property shading do not: there the family is given only by the names in the legend and in each cell's name, and a property only by the printed values.

**Known limits:**

- Atoms cannot be reached one by one with the keyboard inside the 3D model or the Lewis drawing. The Atoms and bonds list beside them is the keyboard path and selects the same things.
- On a canvas, dragging turns the model and the mouse wheel zooms it instead of scrolling the page.
- Some names contain subscript characters ("Oxygen (O₂)"), which a screen reader may read oddly.
- How a screen reader announces the periodic table is not known: whether it says the group and period headers, and what it says on crossing from the main table to the two rows below it, were not heard.
- An element's card is below the table. On most screens it is out of view when an element is chosen; the status line says where it is, and so does the readout in the table's empty corner.
- In a cell, an element's name is shown only on a wide screen, and a value with more digits than fit in the cell is printed there rounded to the digits that fit, not cut short: at 1366×768 and below that is 13 ionization energies and hydrogen's density, in type 9 pixels high. The selected cell, whose border is wider, prints one digit fewer at some widths. The legend says that cells round. The full name and value are in the cell's accessible name, in the readout (above the transition metals, or under the table where the table scrolls sideways), in the list of all elements, and on the element's card. The readout itself is hidden from assistive technology because it repeats the cell's name.
- A cell is laid out for the type sizes above. With a minimum font size set in the browser (Chromium's setting was tried, at 400 and 1366 pixels wide), nothing was cut at 12 pixels. At 16 pixels the three lines of a cell no longer fit its height and the lower part of each value's digits is cut off; the two values too wide for the cell even when rounded, the densities of hydrogen and helium, are cut as well, and the mark that would show it falls in the part of the line that is cut off. At 20 pixels the values are pushed out of the cells and are not seen at all. The places listed in the item above still give the value whole.
- Once an element is selected, the table is re-entered at that element, not at the cell that last had focus.
- Where the table scrolls sideways, the readout strip under it is not always in the window. From the keyboard it is brought there only if the window is tall enough for the table and the strip together (566 pixels at 400 wide, and up to 604 while a property's values are shown); in a shorter window (400×500 was tried) the focused cell is kept in view and the strip is cut off or below the window's edge while a cell in the top two rows has focus. With a mouse or a touch screen nothing scrolls: at 400×800 with the page at its top the strip is below the window's edge, and the learner has to scroll down about 300 pixels to have it in view with the whole table. Until then the cell shows only the symbol and atomic number. The name is still in the cell's accessible name, for assistive technology, and once the element is opened in the status line, which is under the strip and so no nearer.
- The search dialog lists 159 entries when nothing has been typed.

### The titration lab: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The lab was built and checked by the AI coding assistant that wrote it. Its scripts drove the lab's page in headless Chromium 153 on Linux with the keyboard alone (Tab, Shift+Tab, Enter, Space, the arrow keys, Escape and typing; no pointer, and focus was never set by the script), on the development server and on the production build, in the light and dark themes. Before the page was whole, the parts of the bench were driven the same way one at a time, outside the app. The scripts are not part of the repository. No person took part in these checks. How the lab looks (colour, spacing, proportion, how fast the stream runs, how pink the flask is) is for the project's author to judge by eye and is not reported here.

**What was checked that way:**

- **A whole lab by keyboard.** One complete Guided lab, with three trials that count and the learner's average and relative deviation entered, and one Challenge trial from Start to its result. Every control of the lab is a button, a radio button, a text field, a disclosure or a link, reached with Tab and worked with Enter, Space or, for the two modes, the arrow keys. No action needs a pointer, a held key or timing. A key held down adds one drop or one half-drop. The stream is a toggle: it is closed by the same key on the same button, or by Escape wherever focus is, and "Run in 1 mL" and "Run in 5 mL" deliver that volume and close by themselves.
- **The page's three tabs.** The lab's page is in three tabs at every width: Primer, Lab (the Guided checklist, the bench and the notebook) and Record (the table of the run's trials). The tab list takes one stop in the tab order; the arrow keys move between the tabs and wrap round, and Enter or Space shows the one focus is on. Each tab names its panel. A panel that is not shown is hidden and inert: Tab reached nothing in it, and it is kept in the page, so that what was typed in a field, the bench's message line and a warning that is showing were all still there after a visit to another tab. The selected tab is marked by an underline and a heavier weight as well as by its state. The bar with the tabs stays at the top of the window while a panel scrolls, and what takes focus in a panel comes to rest below the bar, not behind it. A tab that was left with its panel scrolled under the bar came back at the same place: with the stopcock in view on the Lab tab, a visit to the Primer tab and back left the stopcock's button where it had been, at 1024×768, at 820×1180 by touch and at 1366×768, by pointer and by keyboard. The page opens on the Primer tab when there is no run and on the Lab tab when there is one; which tab is shown, and where each was left, is not stored and is not in the address.
- **A stream is not left running unseen.** With the stopcock open, choosing another tab closes it: nothing was delivered while the other tab was shown, and back on the Lab tab the message line read "Stopcock closed: it was closed when you left the Lab tab."
- **The primer, one topic at a time.** The Primer tab shows one of the twelve topics as a card, with "Topic 7 of 12" over its heading, and under it buttons named for the topics either side ("Previous: Why phenolphthalein", "Next: The calculation"); at the first topic there is no Previous and at the last no Next. A list of the twelve topics, in a navigation region named "Topics", marks the one shown (with a bar and a heavier weight, and for a screen reader as current) and puts the words "Read first" on the four the primer says to read before the bench. Where the primer is wide enough the list is beside the card; where it is not, it is behind a button named "Topics" that says whether the list is out. Choosing a topic by any of these moves focus to the card's heading.
- **Focus.** A Guided warning takes focus when it appears, and after either of its two buttons focus returns to the control that raised it. When a trial ends, focus moves to its result in the notebook; "Start the next trial" moves it to the bench; Start shows the Lab tab and moves focus to its panel, which is named "Lab", with the tab bar at the top of the window and the checklist's line and the top of the shelf under it, whether Start was pressed above the tabs or on the Lab tab, by keyboard, pointer or touch. All of these are inside the Lab tab, and none of them happened while another tab was shown. At 1366×768 and at 2523×1290 a warning was wholly in view once it had focus.
- **Controls that cannot be used yet.** A bench button that cannot be used yet stays in the tab order and is marked unavailable. Pressing it says why in the bench's message line, in words that name what is missing and nothing else. The message line is a status region, which a screen reader speaks when its text changes, so a sentence that is already showing there is said the second time with "Again:" before it: a second drop of indicator, a second swirl that finds the same, or a second press refused for the same reason then changes the text as the first did. The line is emptied when the notebook or a recorded reading has moved the trial on. A field that cannot be used yet is disabled, with its reason shown under it and tied to it. The two exceptions are under Known limits.
- **Steps done, and entries made.** (Added on 2026-10-06, at the author's request after using the lab on a tablet.) A step of the shelf that is done once is one button: it is named for the action until the step is done, and for what is so afterwards ("Burette conditioned", "Pipette wet with HCl", "HCl pipetted into the flask"), with a tick in front that is hidden from assistive technology, so the words are the button's name. Such a button is marked unavailable, stays in the tab order and says why when pressed. The fill's button reads "Burette filled. Refill" once the burette is filled, and still refills it whenever a fill is allowed; once the burette has run out to its last mark it reads "Fill burette" again, with no tick. A field whose entry has been made (the HCl molarity, a burette reading, the NaOH molarity, the learner's average and relative deviation) is disabled and shows the entry. Where that entry can still be made again, the button beside the field reads "Change" and is described by the field's label, because the Tab key passes over a disabled field. Change puts focus in the field with the old entry selected, "Cancel", or the Escape key anywhere in the field's form, puts the field back, and the old entry stands until a new one is recorded. A held Enter key counts as one press. When Enter records an entry, focus moves from the field, which is then disabled, to that button; it did so after a Guided warning too. Where no entry can be made, a trial that is over among them, the field has its one reason under it and nothing else. These were driven by script in headless Chromium on the production build, in the dark theme: 72 checks, all passing. The script put focus on a control and then used the keyboard; two buttons were pressed by pointer. The Escape key, the held Enter key and the fill's name at the last mark were added after a check by a reviewer who had not built these, and were driven the same way in both themes.
- **Text for the drawings.** The drawing of the burette and flask and the enlarged view of the scale are each one image with a name and a description, and each description can also be read on the page. "The meniscus in words" gives the two graduations the meniscus lies between and which fifth of the gap it is in, and never the reading itself; it is offered to every learner. In the scripted lab every burette reading was taken from those words alone, and every one was accepted. The description of the stand gives the level to the whole millilitre, the stopcock's position, an air bubble in the tip, and what is in the flask and its colour. In every state the scripts reached, the words agreed with the state of the drawing.
- **Names.** Every button, link, field, disclosure, group and table on the lab's page has a text name, before Start, with the bench shown and with a warning showing.
- **Colour is not the only signal.** The flask's colour is named in a status line ("colourless", "faint pink", "pink", "dark pink (overshot)"), which also says what the last addition did: "pink fades at once", "pink fades slowly" or "pink persists". A finished step has a tick and words, a step of the checklist has its state in words, and a trial's result is words. From 2026-10-06 some of this text is also coloured, as an added cue: a value read off the bench or the notebook, the label of a field that is waiting for an entry, a tick and a result that passed and counts, a result that did not pass or has no usable titre, the flask's status words while the flask is pink, and a sentence that says why something cannot be done now. Each colour is on words or a mark that say the same; automated tests hold, for the bench and for the notebook, that no coloured element is without them, and the same test files hold the words themselves.
- **The contrast of the coloured text.** Measured by script in headless Chromium on the production build, in both themes: for every element that carried one of those colours, in fourteen states of a run on the Lab and Record tabs, the colour the browser computed for it against the background colours computed for the elements behind it, laid over each other. No such element was drawn at less than full opacity. The least ratio found for each kind, light theme then dark: a value, 5.66:1 and 10.67:1; a waiting field's label, 5.00:1 and 12.96:1; a tick or a good result, 5.16:1 and 8.69:1; a failed result, 4.77:1 and 6.21:1; the flask's words while pink, 5.93:1 and 6.93:1, and overshot, 9.32:1 and 5.32:1; a reason, 5.12:1 and 8.92:1. These are figures for the colours as a browser computed them. They are not a measurement of any screen, and nothing else on the page was measured.
- **Reduced motion.** With the operating system set to reduce motion, an addition drew no plume and no falling drop, an open stopcock animated nothing, and the status line read the same as with motion allowed.
- **Reload.** After a reload before a titration, during a running stream and after an overshoot, the stored record was byte for byte what it had been, the stopcock was closed, and the last addition's plume was not played again; the status line still gave its cue. The lab's entry is read once when the page loads, nothing is written before Start, and an action that is refused writes nothing.
- **Sizes.** At ten window sizes from 768×1024 to 2523×1290 the page did not scroll sideways, no button was under 32 pixels tall or outside its tile, and both drawings were at least 416 pixels tall.
- **Narrow windows.** In a window narrower than 768 CSS pixels the bench is not shown: the Lab tab has a notice that says so in its place, the Primer tab the primer, and the Record tab a read-only record of any saved run. The page then has no mode choice, Start or Restart, and nothing on it changes a run; its buttons are the tabs and the primer's own. A 1366-pixel screen zoomed to 200% is 683 CSS pixels wide, so it gets this page too, and the notice says that zooming out brings the bench back. Making the window narrow while the stream runs closes the stopcock. The page was tried at 767 and at 768 pixels; browser zoom itself was not used.
- **The meniscus band.** In the enlarged view the darker band of the meniscus against the liquid under it measured 4.12:1 in the light theme and 5.44:1 in the dark theme, from a screenshot of the drawing at its smallest size, 188 pixels wide.
- **Not saving.** With storage that refuses writes, the lab page says that progress will last for that session only.

**What has not been checked:**

- **No screen reader was used.** What is announced on a drop, during a stream, when the cue changes, when a warning appears, when a trial ends, when a tab is chosen or when the primer's topic changes has not been heard by anyone. The roles, names, states and descriptions were read by script.
- **No person's reading of the drawn meniscus is on record.** The whole lab rests on reading a level to 0.01 mL from a drawing. In every check the readings were taken by script from the text alternative, and the geometry of the drawing is held by automated tests. Whether a person can read the drawing to within 0.02 mL has not been recorded, and no learner has tried.
- **Chromium only.** No other browser, no phone, no tablet and no touch screen, not even an emulated one. The tabs were asked for by the project's author after opening the page on a tablet; the scripts tried them at a tablet's window sizes (1024×768 and 768×1024) in desktop Chromium, by keyboard, not on a tablet.
- **The checklist written for a person at the keyboard was run by script instead.** Three things in it were exercised only by automated tests or outside the app, not on the lab's page: the ending in which a rinse thins the pink away at the burette's last mark; a stream opened again while Guided's hint is showing; and a hidden browser tab closing the stream.
- Text contrast on the lab's page has been measured for the coloured text listed above and for nothing else: not the grey secondary text, not the faint text of a button that cannot be used yet, and not a button while the pointer is over it. Browser zoom, enlarged text and forced-colour (high contrast) mode have not been tried. No person with a colour-vision deficiency has looked at the pinks or at the other colours; the words above carry what they show.

**Known limits:**

- **The bench needs a window at least 768 CSS pixels wide.** A learner who needs 200% zoom on a 1366-pixel screen, or who has only a phone, can read the primer and a saved run's record and cannot do the lab there. With no run, such a window opens on the Primer tab, and the notice that says why there is no bench is on the Lab tab.
- The record of a run is on its own tab, so the table of trials and the bench are not on screen together. The notebook, with the result of the last trial, is on the Lab tab with the bench.
- In the primer, the sentences that state a rule of the simulation and not of a real bench are set off by a rule down their side and a tint. Nothing is added for a screen reader: each such sentence says so itself, in words such as "In this lab".
- While a stream or a measured pour is running, the other stopcock buttons cannot be used, and pressing one says nothing new: the running button reads "Close stopcock". A Record or Check button whose field cannot be used yet has its reason beside it all the time and does nothing when pressed.
- Two buttons on the page can read "Change" at the same time, one beside the HCl molarity and one beside a burette reading, while a trial is open. They are told apart by their descriptions, the label of the field each belongs to, and by where they are; their names are the same.
- A field whose entry has been made is disabled, so the Tab key does not stop on it. Whether a screen reader reads out a disabled field and the entry in it has not been heard.
- Moving focus off the running button with Tab leaves the stopcock open. The message under the stand says, when the stopcock opens, that the same button or Escape closes it.
- When a trial ends by itself during a stream (an overshoot, or the burette's last mark), focus moves from the stopcock button to the result in the notebook.
- Each new trial starts with "The meniscus in words" and any open Hazards disclosure closed, so a learner who reads the burette from the words opens that disclosure once in each trial.
- During a stream, additions come every 0.4 seconds and the status line changes only when the flask's colour or the cue changes. Whether a screen reader announces a change in time to act on it is not known.
- "Discard this flask" acts at once, with no confirmation; Restart asks first.
- The Record tab of a completed lab shows its three trials as passed, and does not show the line that says the lab is complete or the base's true molarity: those are in the notebook, on the Lab tab, which a narrow window does not show.
- Formulas in the primer use subscripts and superscripts, which a screen reader may read oddly.

### The aqueous reactions lab: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The lab's screens were built and checked on 2026-10-07 by the AI coding assistant that wrote them. A script drove the production build in headless Chromium 153 on Linux: 271 checks, all passing at the last run. The script put focus on a control and then used the keyboard (Enter, Space, Tab, Escape, the arrow keys and typing); once, at one station, it walked the Tab key from the station's heading to the end of the tab. One tab was chosen by pointer. The script is not part of the repository. No person took part, and how the lab looks (colour, spacing, proportion, the drawn tube) is for the project's author to judge by eye and is not reported here.

**A second look, by a reviewer that did not build it.** Later on 2026-10-07 another AI coding assistant, which had not written the lab, reviewed it and drove the production build again with scripts of its own, in the same headless Chromium on Linux. It ran one whole lab in Challenge to completion four times: by keyboard at 1024×768 in the light theme and at 1366×768 in the dark theme, and by tap at 820×1180 in each theme, in a browser context that emulates a touch screen. Each run made mistakes on the way: an observation that is not the tube's at every station, a check before the tools were used, a state that is not right, an equation that does not balance, the two sides exchanged, a formula that does not belong, "No reaction" where something reacts, an equation at the station where nothing does, a spectator left in a net ionic equation and a compound not split. Each run made 209 checks, all passing at the last run. It also tried reloads, Restart, stored records that do not fit, two tabs on the lab, the app's own reduced-motion setting and the two panels. What it found and changed is in `docs/research/phase-4/build-record.md`, and the lists below are as the lab stands after it. No person took part in this either, and the scripts are not part of the repository.

**What was checked, by the one script or the other:**

- **A whole lab by keyboard.** One complete Guided lab: at each of the ten stations the tube mixed, every tool used, the observation chosen and checked, and the three equations built and checked, to the line that says the lab is complete, in the light theme at 1024×768. Then a Challenge run of two stations, each with an observation that is not the tube's and an equation with a state that is not right, put right afterwards. Every control is a button, a radio button, a checkbox, a disclosure or a link. Nothing is typed but a sum in the calculator, nothing is dragged that the arrow keys do not also move, and nothing depends on timing or on a held key.
- **Tab order.** At one station with an equation part built, the Tab key went from the station's heading through 47 stops, which were every control of the station that could be seen, in the order they are read, and none that was hidden. A group of radio buttons took one stop.
- **Focus.** Start moves focus to the Lab tab's panel. Mix leaves focus on its button, which then reads "Mixed". A tile of an equation builder's bank keeps focus when it is pressed, and its substance goes into the active slot. Enter or Space on a full slot moves focus to that term's coefficient; taking the term out with its button moves focus to the slot it leaves, and Backspace or Delete on a slot leaves focus on that slot. A check that finds an entry correct leaves focus on the button, which then reads "Change"; Escape while a change is open puts the entry back with focus on "Change". Previous, Next and the list of stations move focus to the heading of the station shown. Closing a floating panel moves focus to the button that opened it.
- **One station at a time.** One station was on the page at a time at every step. A station left with an equation half built still had it on coming back.
- **Controls that cannot be used yet.** A button that cannot be used stays in the tab order and is marked unavailable; pressing it says why in the message line of its card, which is a status region. Tried: Mix on a mixed tube, a check of observations with nothing chosen, a check of an equation with nothing on a side, a coefficient below 1, a substance added twice to one side, and every button of a completed lab. A sentence that is already showing is said the second time with "Again:" before it. A question that a tool answers is disabled until one of its tools has been used, with the tool to use shown under it; a check made while such a question still waits says to use the tool named under it. On a station of a completed lab the reason no entry is taken is said once, at the station's head, and that sentence is the description of the button of each of the station's four entries. "Smell the tube directly" is always unavailable, and a press gives the stored caution.
- **Challenge gives nothing away before the learner records.** At every station, before the mix, after it and after the tools were used, the text and the attributes of the station on the page were searched for the station's hints, for the lines that say what a correct observation holds, for its notes and for its three stored equations, with and without their state symbols. None was there. After an observation was recorded, the hints and the expected observation were there, as the mode's own sentence says, and the notes and the stored equations still were not. The notes came when the station was done.
- **Feedback gives no answer.** After an observation that is not the tube's, the line named the question to look at again and not the answer, and that question carried the words "Look again". After each kind of equation that was tried (an ion in a molecular equation, a substance that does not belong, a state, "No reaction" where there is one), the line said what kind of thing was wrong; automated tests hold that these sentences contain no formula.
- **Words for the drawing.** The drawn tube is one image with a name, described by a line that says what to do with it. Three buttons are laid over it, one on each section of what is in the tube: the surface of the liquid, the liquid, and the bottom. A mouse pointer over a section, keyboard focus on it or a tap shows beside it, in words, what is seen there: the solid and its colour or that there is none, the colour of the liquid, bubbles or none. The same words go to a status line for a screen reader, and a list beside the picture says in words which parts have been looked at. Each of the three questions about what is seen opens once its part has been looked at. Automated tests hold that the drawing and the words agree with each other and with the lab's record at all ten stations. This was changed on 2026-10-07, after the checks above were run, and was run by script in headless Chromium by mouse, by keyboard and by emulated touch at stations 1, 5, 7 and 10; nobody has heard it in a screen reader.
- **Colour is not the only signal.** The colour of the liquid and of the solid are named under the drawing. What a reagent looks like in its bottle is said in words on its tile, and the colour drawn in the bottle of a coloured solution adds nothing to them (added later on 2026-10-07, and looked at by script in both themes). A station's progress, a correct entry and one that is not, a tool that has been used and each cell of the solubility table are words, with a mark beside some of them. The colours of the titration lab's page (a value, a field that waits, a tick, a result that is not good, a reason) are used here for the same things, as an added cue.
- **Reduced motion.** With the operating system set to reduce motion, the bubbles were drawn and did not move. With motion allowed they rise three times, which takes under five seconds, and then rest. With the app's own setting on and the operating system allowing motion, nothing on the page ran for longer than a hundredth of a millisecond after a mix, a panel opened or closed, a change of tab, a term added or a change of station.
- **Reload, Restart and other tabs.** A reload in the middle of a station left the stored record byte for byte what it was and came back on the Lab tab at that station, with the tube mixed, the observation as it was last checked and the equation as it was last checked, each with its feedback. A completed lab came back complete. Restart asked first, and a refusal changed nothing. A stored record of another version, one that is not JSON, one whose status its entries do not give and one with a formula that is not on its station's palette were each set aside with the notice and kept, and Start then worked. With the lab open in two tabs, each tab took what the other saved, a Restart included. What had been chosen or built and not checked stayed through a look at another tab of the page, at another station, and a change of mode.
- **The two panels.** The solubility table and the calculator each opened as a panel that is not modal, moved 16 pixels at a press of an arrow key on its grip, and closed with Escape. Each was dragged by its grip, with a pointer and by touch, past the edges of the window and stayed inside it; each stayed inside when the window was made smaller under it; and the Tab key left each at either end. The solubility table is a table with a caption, a header for each column and each row, and a word in every cell. One cell has a footnote's sign beside its word, with words for a screen reader in place of the sign, and a line under the table that says what it means. A third panel, the ions of the bench, was added later on 2026-10-07 and checked by another script in the same browser: it opened from its button by keyboard at 1024×768 and by tap at 820×1180, moved 16 pixels at a press of an arrow key, was dragged by its grip with a pointer and by touch and stayed inside the window, and closed with Escape, with its close button and with the button that opened it. It is two tables, each with a caption and a header for each column and each row; a colour in it is a word. In a window 768 pixels high this panel is taller than the window allows and scrolls inside itself. A wheel or a finger scrolls it; by keyboard, Page Down does once focus is in the panel, and focus is not moved into a panel when it opens, so the panel is many presses of Tab from its button (53 at a station whose tube is not mixed), as the other two panels are.
- **Sizes.** At 768×1024, 820×1180, 1024×768, 1366×768 and 1920×1080 the page did not scroll sideways, and every button and every choice of the observation form and of an equation builder was at least 44 pixels each way. The controls that are drawn smaller (the buttons that open the panels, the "Hazards" and "Sources" disclosures, the "Stations" button, Previous and Next, and a panel's grip and close button) each have a hit area of 44 pixels each way laid over them, which draws nothing; taps in that area outside the drawn control were tried and worked. Where two such controls are close, their areas meet: under the panels' buttons, where the "Stations" button is 12 pixels below, the two areas share 2 pixels. In a window narrower than 768 pixels the Lab tab shows the notice in place of the stations, as the titration lab's does, and the page has no mode choice, Start or Restart. At 820 pixels wide the list of stations was behind a button named "Stations", which listed them when pressed and closed on Escape; at 1024 pixels it was beside the station.
- **Both themes** were loaded, and the browser's console was empty throughout. The page requested nothing but its own files.

**What has not been checked:**

- **No screen reader was used.** What is announced when a tube is mixed, when a tool is used, when feedback comes, when a term is added to an equation or when the station changes has not been heard by anyone. The roles, names and states were read by script.
- **Chromium only, and no real device.** No other browser, no phone and no tablet. The taps were made by script in a desktop Chromium that emulates a touch screen at 820×1180; no finger has touched the page, and the sizes of the controls and of their hit areas were measured, not felt.
- **Contrast was not measured on this lab's page.** It uses the titration lab's colours for the same roles, on the same backgrounds as far as was seen, but nothing was measured here: not the coloured text, not the grey text, not the faint text of a button that cannot be used, and not the colours inside the drawn tube, where a white solid is told from the white card by its outline and by the words the bottom of the tube shows when it is looked at.
- Browser zoom, enlarged text and forced-colour (high contrast) mode have not been tried. No person with a colour-vision deficiency has looked at the page.
- Nobody has run the lab by hand, with a keyboard or otherwise.

**Known limits:**

- **The stations need a window at least 768 CSS pixels wide**, as the titration bench does; a narrower window has the primer and the record of a saved run.
- What is chosen or built and not yet checked is lost on a reload, and on a reload in the middle of the second of two stations begun the page opens on the first of them.
- Not every control is drawn 44 pixels tall: the "Hazards" and "Sources" disclosures are drawn 20 pixels tall and the buttons that open the floating panels 28. On a lab's page each has a hit area of 44 pixels all the same (see Sizes, above). The mode's choice, over the tabs, is one line that says the mode in force ("Mode: Guided"), drawn 20 pixels tall with a hit area laid over it in the same way (measured on the flame test lab's page on 2026-10-09 that area was 40 pixels tall, not 44: the bar of tabs under it lies over its foot); it opens to two radio buttons, each with what its mode does. It was made so later on 2026-10-07, for both labs, and tried by script by keyboard at 1024×768 and by tap at 820×1180. "Restart lab" is as the titration lab has it and was not changed.
- **A station is a long page.** At 820×1180 a station with its tube mixed is a little under three screens long, with three equations built and none yet checked as well; a complete ionic equation of seven terms is about a third of a screen while it is open, and one line once it is correct. Building it takes 19 presses at station 1, and its net ionic equation 7. (The equation builder was rebuilt on 2026-10-07 as a row of slots filled from a bank of tiles. Before that the same station was about four screens long, the open builder a screen, and the two equations 26 and 10 presses.)
- A window made narrower than 768 pixels and then wide again shows the stations afresh: what was chosen or built and not checked is lost, as on a reload.
- An equation builder has no undo; a term is taken out with its button, with Backspace or Delete on its slot, or by dragging it out of the row. A tile can be dragged with a mouse or a finger, and nothing in a builder can be done by dragging only.
- Three builders are on a station at once, each with its own "Check" button; the three buttons have the same name and are told apart by the heading of the builder each is in, which is each button's description. The "Change" buttons of entries that are correct are the same.
- A formula is drawn with real subscripts and superscripts, which a screen reader may read as plain digits: "Fe3+". The names of a term's group and of its "Remove" button use the raised and lowered characters of plain text, which a screen reader may read oddly.
- A new term of an equation is written as dissolved, (aq), until the learner changes it.
- Each station starts with its "Hazards" disclosures closed.

### The flame test lab: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The lab's screens were built and checked on 2026-10-08 and 2026-10-09 by the AI coding assistant that wrote them. Scripts drove the production build in headless Chromium 153 on Linux: 308 checks in five scripts, all passing at the last run. One script used the keyboard alone (Tab, Shift+Tab, Enter, Space, Escape and the arrow keys), one tapped in a browser context that emulates a touch screen, and the others used a mouse pointer. The scripts are not part of the repository. On 2026-10-09 a second AI coding assistant, which had not built the lab, reviewed it in the same way, changed it where it found fault, and ran scripts of its own after its last change: 4,019 checks passing, at four window sizes and in both themes, with four that did not pass, all of the labs' shared head of the page and given under the known limits below. What it found and changed is in `docs/research/flame/build-record.md`. No person took part in either, and how the lab looks (colour, spacing, proportion, the drawn flame and the strip) is for the project's author to judge by eye and is not reported here.

**What was checked that way:**

- **A whole lab by keyboard.** One complete Guided lab at 1024×768 in the light theme: at each of the six stations the loop dipped without cleaning, the warning answered, the loop cleaned and dipped, held in the flame, the flame and every line and band looked at, the three questions answered and checked; then the closing question, answered wrongly and then rightly, to the line that says the lab is complete; then the Record tab. At the first station a check was tried with nothing chosen and then with a wrong colour. At the second the warning was answered with "Dip it anyway", the observation made that way was recorded, and the station was done again. Every control is a button, a radio button, a disclosure or a link. Nothing is typed, nothing is dragged, and nothing depends on timing or on a held key.
- **Two stations by tap, in Challenge,** at 820×1180: the first with the loop as it is picked up, handled and not cleaned, observed, recorded, told that it does not count, and done again with a clean loop; the second cleanly, with a wrong colour first.
- **Eight whole labs in Challenge, at the review:** by keyboard alone at 1024×768 and 820×1180 in the light theme and at 1366×768 in the dark; by tap at 820×1180 in both themes; by mouse at 768×1024 and 1366×768 in the light theme and at 1024×768 in the dark. In each, the handled loop was dipped as it was at the first station and its observation told that it does not count, a loop that still carried the last salt was dipped at the second, a wrong colour, a wrong range and a line for a band were recorded, and the closing question was answered wrongly and then rightly. At every station the callouts, the names of the buttons before and after a look, the list under the strip and the Record tab were compared with sentences worked out again from the lab's record, and each was announced in a status region whose text changed.
- **Looking.** A mouse pointer over the flame or over a line or a band, keyboard focus on it, or a tap shows a callout that says in words what is seen there, and the same words go to a status line for a screen reader. The callout went when the pointer left, at Escape, and on a tap elsewhere. At the review a pointer was moved up from each line and band toward its callout a pixel at a time, at 768, 1024 and 1366 pixels: the callout went before it was reached, in a gap of 10 pixels between the strip and the callout; the gap was made part of the button, and after that the callout stayed while the pointer travelled onto it and along it. Every callout of the strip was inside its dark card. A look opened the question it belongs to: the flame the first, and the last of the strip's lines and bands the other two.
- **Focus.** Start moves focus to the Lab tab's panel. The warning takes focus when it is raised, and again when the same press raises it again. "Go back and clean it" puts focus on "Clean the loop"; "Dip it anyway" puts it on "Dip the loop". Clean, Dip, Hold and "Look through the spectroscope" keep focus when pressed. A check that finds the observation correct leaves focus on the button, which then reads "Change". The "Clean the loop" button under an observation that does not count cleans the loop and puts focus on "Dip the loop". Previous, Next and the list move focus to the heading of the entry shown.
- **Tab order.** At one station with everything showing, the Tab key went from the heading through 14 stops at the barium station, in the order they are read: the bottle's hazards, Clean, Dip, Hold, the flame, the spectroscope's button, each line and band from the violet end, the hints' disclosure, the three questions (one stop each), the check, and the disclosure under it. (Thirteen before the hints were put behind a disclosure, at the review.) Moving through the pictures by keyboard looks at each part as focus reaches it.
- **Controls that cannot be used yet.** A button that cannot be used stays in the tab order and is marked unavailable; pressing it says why in its card's message line, which is a status region. Tried: Hold before the loop carries the station's solution, a check with nothing chosen, and Clean on a completed lab. A question whose look has not been made is disabled, with what to look at first as text under it. The closing question is there and disabled, with the reason under it, until the six stations are done.
- **Challenge gives nothing away before an observation that counts.** Automated tests search the Lab tab's markup, at every station and at six steps from a fresh station to one recorded with a loop that was not clean and begun again, for what a correct observation holds, the station's notes, what the strip leaves out, the record's names for its lines and bands, the formula of the molecule that gives a band, the words "atom" and "molecule", the hints and the link to the metal's card. None is there, in the text or in an attribute. The script that tapped through two stations searched the page for the same at six points on the way. After an observation that counted, right or wrong, the hints and the expected observation were there; the notes came when the station was done. At the review the live page was searched for the same at every station of the eight Challenge labs, fresh, dipped, held, with everything looked at and after an observation that did not count: in its text, in every attribute and in what its form controls held. None was there, and the only sentences on how bright anything is were the callouts of lines and bands the learner had looked at.
