A change to Guided in the middle of a station marked what Challenge had recorded, and the station counted once those entries were put right. Restart asked first.
- **Challenge, at one station (the builder).** A wrong observation was taken with no mark and the words "Not checked"; "No reaction" was said, and no equation was asked for; the check then marked each entry, said that the run does not count and why, and focus was on that. "Work the station again" asked first, set the station up as run 2 and put focus on its heading. Worked right, the equation showed "Recorded." and no mark, in a row with one empty place on each side whatever the equation is; the check then counted the station and showed its notes, with focus on them. The Record tab listed both runs.
- **A whole lab in Guided by the keyboard alone (the review),** at 1280×800 in the dark theme, by Tab, Shift+Tab, the arrows, Space, Enter and Delete, with nothing focused by script. Every part of a picture was looked at by moving focus to it. A wrong observation, a wrong kind and "No reaction" of a tube that reacts were refused, each with its sentence and with focus left on the button; equations were refused for a species that does not belong, a state, the balance and a species missing; a tile was put in the place of another; a place was emptied by Delete; a hint was opened by Enter. Focus was on "Change" after an entry was taken, on a term's controls after Enter on its place, on the notes when a station came to count, and on the station's heading after Next and after "Work the station again".
- **Names and roles (the review),** read from the browser's accessibility tree at three stages: a picture is an image with a name and a description; its parts are buttons named for what is looked at and whether it has been; a tile is its formula and its name; a place says its number, its side, what it holds and whether it is chosen; a term has a group with its number in front, four states each named in full, and its way out.
- **Contrast (the review),** measured in the page for every piece of text of the Lab tab at eleven stages in both themes: the least is 4.7 to 1 in the light theme and 6.9 in the dark. The words of a button that cannot be used now, which still takes focus and says why when pressed, are 3.7 and 4.9. Every picture stands on a white card in both themes: the ink that edges each piece of metal, bubble, light and powder is 10.4 to 1 against the card, 8.9 against the colourless liquid, 9.3 against the pale yellow and 4.4 against the paler blue; the blue of the first station and the paler blue it fades to differ by 3.8; the dashed outline of a part not yet looked at is 3.4 against the card.
- **Stored records that do not fit (the review).** Six kinds were put in the browser's store: text that is not a record, another shape, an entry made in an order the lab cannot make, a status its runs do not give, a later version, and an entry that changes nothing. Each was set aside under a second key, with a notice, and the lab started fresh; a sound record reopened at its station.
- **What a picture says, and when.** A part is looked at by a pointer over it, by focus or by a tap; it is outlined, and what is seen there is said under the drawing in a line that assistive technology is told has changed. Once what is seen of every vessel of a station is recorded, the picture is drawn without the room under it and those words hang under the drawing, over what stands there, for as long as the part is looked at (the review). Until then the words of a part are nowhere on the page: an automated test searches the markup of each station for them. The whole of each picture in words is behind a closed disclosure, and those words are tested for every picture the lab can show.
- **Nothing is told by colour alone, and nothing moves.** A coated piece of metal is drawn under a grain as well as in another colour. Bubbles are none, three small ones, fourteen larger ones or seven of a middling size, at rest. The three metals of one station are named over their tubes. A solid in a liquid is drawn as specks with an edge, and the liquid as cloudy. The light of the burning strip is a star with rays, drawn at rest. That a tube is warm is said in words, once the tube has been felt. With reduced motion asked for, no element of the page had a transition or an animation longer than a millisecond; with it not asked for, 23 did, all of them the frame's buttons. The lab has no animation of its own.
- **How large each thing to press is.** Over ten stages of four stations at 768×1024: no button, choice, tile or place of a station was under 44 px either way, and the least part of any picture was 49 px each way (the tongs, in the second picture of the burned strip). The three tubes that stand in a row are drawn 148 or 149 px wide there, and the part of each 70 by 52 px. The controls that carry the frame's own 44 px area are smaller to the eye: a disclosure's line, the list's button, Previous and Next.
- **That buttons stay where they are.** At the station with the widest equation, at 768×1024: the bank of tiles and the button that raises a number each stood at one place through four tiles and eight presses that took a number to 9, and the button that records the equation moved once, by 52 px, as the row took its second line. The tiles and the line of a term's controls stand over the row of places for that reason (the review put the controls there; as built the row kept the height of two lines from the start).
- **Every disclosure's hit area at the foot of a station, point by point.** At both sizes, with Sources, the bench, each of the nine hazards and the sources inside each open in turn, 22 states at each size: a script asked the page what answers at points 4 px by 2 px apart over each control's own box, and no point answered for another control.
- **The same on a station itself (the review),** at twelve stages at both sizes, with the picture in words closed and open and each hint open, over each control's box and over the hit area laid on a disclosure's line. With Guided's hint open under an open question, the lower part of the button that records answered for the hint, at four stages at each size; an open hint now stands 16 px lower, and no point of any control answers for another.
- **How long a station's page is.** At 768×1024 with the menu open, 976 px of the window are under the tab bar. From a station's head to the foot of its last card before the one that closes it, as the review left it: the three stations with one tube are 467 px before anything is done, 613 to 795 px with what is seen open, 810 or 828 px with the kind open, 821 to 891 px with the equation open or built, and 830 to 956 px done; the station of the burning strip, which has two pictures, is 938 px at the most while it is worked and 972 px done; the station of three tubes is 645 px before, 841 px with what is seen open, 1,048 px with its kinds open, 1,129 px at the most with an equation built, 945 px at the order and 1,059 px done. As built the first four reached 1,071 and 1,137 px and the last 1,442 px. Design spec, section 24.6, has every figure, and those at 1280×800 as built.
- **Focus,** where the builder measured it: on the station's heading after "Work the station again"; on the notes when a station comes to count, in every run; on how the run came out when it does not. The review's run by the keyboard, above, has where it stands after Next and after an entry is recorded or refused.
- **Both themes, two sizes.** Forty-seven pictures of the page were taken in each of the six runs. The assistant that built the page looked at four sheets that hold the picture of every station before and after its action and the notes of the last station, at both sizes in both themes, and at three whole pages of the build as it is and six of builds before it. The other pictures were taken and not looked at. The review retook the pictures on the build as it left it, and looked at two of the four sheets (768 px in the dark theme, 1280 px in the light), at the pictures of every station before and after at 768 px in the light theme one by one and larger, and at ten whole pages in both themes and at both sizes. In one of them it found what the builder's unseen pictures also showed: under each of the three tubes of a station that has been checked, the sentence on what was found ran out of its column into the next. Each line there now has its own tick or cross, and the sentence is a screen reader's.
- **The aqueous reactions lab, after its builder was shared.** The markup of its Lab tab at each of its ten stations in six states, and of its Record tab in each, was written out before and after and is the same byte for byte; its page tests pass untouched. In the browser, at its first station, one equation was built by taps and one by the keyboard alone, and each was found correct and stored. After the review's change to the shared builder, a test holds that lab's order of row, controls and tiles, and the three equations of its first station were worked to their end at 768×1024, the molecular one by emulated touch and the two ionic ones by the keyboard alone: each was found correct and stored, with no error.
- **By automated tests,** which run without a browser: where every part of every picture lies, that each is a finger wide and tall at the size it is drawn and that none lies over another; the bubbles; a value to draw every colour word the record stores; what a station shows at each stage; the row of places in each mode; that Challenge shows no kind, equation, note, hint, mark or source of an answer before the check; the notes, the finished equations and the catalyst over its arrow; the record; and where the page reopens.

**What was not checked:**

- No person has run the lab, by hand, by keyboard or by touch. No screen reader was used: that a look, a refusal and a run's outcome are announced is known from the markup, from the accessibility tree and from where focus went, and not from hearing them.
- One browser, headless Chromium on Linux. No real device, no real tablet, no real touch screen.
- The page with the text enlarged, at 200% zoom, in a high-contrast mode, or with the window between the sizes named, or with the menu closed.
- The keyboard at more than one size, and touch at more than one.
- No chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes, the primer or the page's sentences.

**Known limits of the types of reactions lab's page:**

- **The station of three tubes is longer than a screen from its kinds on,** on an upright tablet with the menu open: by up to 153 px, though its kinds, equations and order are asked one at a time. The other four stations are inside a screen at every stage.
- **The three tubes in a row are small** on an upright tablet with the menu open: 148 px wide each. What a part of one shows is said under it in a line that breaks three or four times.
- **Once what is seen is recorded, the words of a part hang over what is under the picture** for as long as the part is looked at: the recorded answer and its "Change". A press goes through them.
- **A white solid spread through a liquid and bubbles in a liquid are both drawn as white circles with a dark edge.** The solid's are small, many and evenly set in a cloudy liquid; the bubbles are few, larger and scattered. The words of the look say which.
- **A button that cannot be used now is drawn at half strength:** its words are 3.7 to 1 in the light theme. It still takes focus, and says why when pressed.
- **Looking is seeing.** A learner who cannot see a drawing has what each part shows said when the part takes focus, and the whole of a picture in words behind a disclosure; that is then reading a sentence and choosing the words that match it.
- **Warmth is said only once the tube is felt,** and the part that feels it is a hand drawn beside the tube.
- **The builder has a place that is outlined, and a tile goes there at one press.** A tile cannot be picked up first and a place pressed after. Dragging works and is never needed.
- **The row of an equation takes a second line, or a third,** in a narrow card as it is filled, and the button that records, under it, then moves down. The tiles and a term's controls are over the row and do not.
- **What is chosen or built and not yet recorded is dropped** when the page is left or reloaded, and when the mode is changed.
- **"Work the station again" asks with the browser's own box,** as Restart does.
- **Previous and Next are at the foot of a station,** so they stand at another height from one stage to the next, as in every lab that has the paged list.
- Below 768 pixels of width the Lab tab shows a notice in place of the bench, as in every lab.

### The polarity and mixing lab: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The lab's screens were built on 2026-10-12 and checked that day by the AI coding assistant that wrote them, by script, in headless Chromium on Linux, on the production build. Nobody sat at a keyboard. The page is built on the pieces of the labs before it: the frame and its tabs, the form of choices, the hazards' tile, the way a picture is explored, and the shared tube's drawing, which gained one thing for this lab, a second liquid lying on the first. What is new is the table the tubes are chosen from. The lab's one deep review was held the same day, by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium on the production build and by reading; what it ran is under "The review" below, and what follows has the page as the review left it.

**What the page is.** Three parts, chosen with three buttons. In the first two a tube is chosen from a table: each place is a button named by what is in its tube and how the tube stands ("Distilled water with vinegar: Not begun"), in a table with a heading for each row and each column. A tube is one card: its picture, with a button over each layer; three or four step buttons beside it, each of which says whether it is pressed once its step is done; and what is seen, as choices, under the picture on a tablet and beside it where the card is 46rem wide. The third part is seven groups of two choices.

**What was run, by script:**

- **Three whole labs,** from Start to the line that says the lab is complete: in Guided by the pointer at 768×1024 in the light theme and at 1280×800 in the dark, and in Challenge by emulated touch at 768×1024 in the dark theme. Every look was a press on a part of the picture and every answer a press on its choice. The browser reported no error. A reload then opened the Lab tab on the complete lab.
- **The line between two liquids.** Two of the bench's pairs are two liquids with no colour, drawn as two bands of one grey. The line between two bands was read from the pixels of the page drawn at four times its size: rgb(63, 63, 70), against rgb(232, 237, 242) where a liquid has no colour, 8.9 to 1, and against rgb(251, 243, 189) where it is pale yellow, 9.3 to 1. The tube's card is white in both themes, and the figures were the same in each at both sizes. No other contrast was measured.
- **No layer is told by its colour alone.** Each layer has a bracket and a word in the picture ("Top layer", "Bottom layer") once it has been looked at, and not before, so that the labels do not say how many layers there are; and a look says where it is, whether it is the thicker or the thinner of the two, and then its colour. Which liquid there is more of is said over the question that asks which is on top. Tests hold that for every pair with more of either liquid.
- **Sizes.** No button, choice, place of a table, link or disclosure's hit area was under 44 px either way at either size; the least part of a picture was 67 by 56 px, the thinner of two layers where it is underneath.
- **Hit areas, point by point.** With each disclosure open in turn (the hint, the tube in words, the sources, the bench, its hazards and their sources, and all four at once), the second question's hint, the table open over a tube, both tables, and part 3 with its hint, 29 states over the two sizes, every point of every control's box, and of the hit area laid over a disclosure's line, was asked which control answers there. None answered for another.
- **Nothing moves under the hand.** The three step buttons stood at one place through every stage of each pair's tube in the two runs by the pointer. In the run by touch a button read 1 px lower just after it was tapped; that is taken to be the frame's pressed look and was not looked into. A place of the table stood where it was when the sentence for a place off the bench came up under the table, and the other choice of a liquid stood where it was when Guided refused one.
- **Lengths.** At 768×1024 with the menu open, 959 px of the window are under the bar that holds the tabs once it is at the top. From the three buttons that choose a part to the foot of a tube's last box the page was 644 px until the tube had stood, 762 to 810 px with its first question open, 862 to 930 px with its second, 771 to 818 px once the tube counts, and 939 px at the sixth tube of a part, with the part's notes; the sources and the bench are 92 px under that. A tube tried after its part is done is as long as the first six while it is under way, and up to 962 px once it counts. At 1280×800, with 735 px under the bar, a tube with a question open was 810 to 930 px as first built, longer than that window; with what is seen beside the picture it is 567 px at every stage and 671 to 694 px once it counts, part 3 is 580 px with its questions open, and the sixth tube of a part that counts is 816 px with the part's notes.
- **Motion.** With reduced motion asked for, no element of the Lab tab had a transition or an animation over a millisecond. The lab's own stylesheet has none in any case, which a test holds.
- **Both themes, two sizes.** A hundred pictures were taken, twenty-five at each of 768×1024 and 1280×800 in each of the light and the dark theme. The builder looked at every kind of tube among them, both tables, part 3, the Record tab and a page with every disclosure open, and not at the rest.
- **By automated tests,** which run without a browser: the acceptance list of the design spec (section 25.5); the description in words of every tube at every stage; the markup of the page at each stage in both modes; that nothing outside a closed disclosure says what a tube shows before it is looked at; that Challenge shows no mark, no note and no source of an outcome before a check; and that no liquid's family is on the page of the first two parts.

**The review, on 2026-10-12,** by script, by an AI reviewer that had built none of the lab:

- **A whole lab by the keyboard alone,** in Guided at 768×1024 in the light theme: 1,693 presses of Tab, Shift+Tab, Enter, Space and the arrow keys, through all nine pairs, all eight solids in liquids and the three places off the bench, with a wrong number of layers, the wrong liquid on top, a wrong answer of a solid, a wrong family and a wrong answer in part 3, to the line that says the lab is complete, then the Record tab, a reload and Restart. After each press that did something, focus was on a control, on the heading of the tube or the part chosen, or on the box that says how a tube came out, and never on the page itself; every control the Tab key reached was inside the window.
- **Challenge by emulated touch,** at 768×1024 in the dark theme: wrong answers recorded, checked and set up again in a fresh tube; an answer changed; the mode changed over a wrong answer; part 3 with two of seven wrong, checked and answered again; a seventh tube; a reload. Before each check the card, the Record tab and the list of sources held no mark, no note and no source of an outcome.
- **What it found there and changed.** "Answer again" in part 3 left focus on the page itself: it now goes to the part's heading. What the last look said stayed in the line a screen reader is given after the tube was shaken or had stood: a step now clears it. A step done and a step that cannot be taken yet were both announced only as unavailable: a step's button now says whether it is pressed. A tube tried after its part was done had the part's notes under it and was 954 and 1,074 px with its questions open at 768×1024: they now stand under a tube that counts. A sentence told a learner at a filled tube to set it up again in a fresh tube, which the page does not offer there: it no longer does.
- **Contrast of the text,** from the computed colours of every text on eighteen states of the page (the tables, a tube at each stage with each question open and refused, a tube that counts, part 3 at each stage, the Record tab, the primer). In the light theme 4.5 to 1 or more: the least is the small grey text on the primer's grey box, 4.5; the grey 12 px text on white is 4.7, the cross 4.8, the colour of a question still to answer 5.0, a refusal 5.1, the tick 5.2 to 5.6, and a bracket's word 10.4. In the dark theme 6.2 to 1 or more. The one text under that is a step that cannot be taken yet, drawn at half strength: 3.7 to 1 in the light theme and 4.9 in the dark.
- **What is pressed, again,** on the same states at both sizes in both themes, with each disclosure open in turn and the window made as tall as the page so that every control was inside it: 44 px or more each way but for the links of an open list of sources, which are 16 to 32 px tall in this lab as in every lab; and no point of any control's box answered for another control.
- **Motion, again:** none over a millisecond on any of those states with reduced motion asked for.
- **Names and roles,** read from the browser's own tree: the table has a caption, a heading for each row and column and a named button in each place; a place off the bench is a button that says it is unavailable and takes focus; a tube is a region named by its heading, its picture an image with a name, its parts buttons, and its questions groups of radio buttons named by the question.
- **Pictures.** Nineteen pictures of a tube, of every kind, before a look and after one, were looked at, and the page at both sizes in both themes.
- **The shared tube in other labs.** The tube that the Group I cations lab, the Le Chatelier's principle lab and the types of reactions lab draw at Start was written out as markup before the review's change to the drawing and after it, and is the same byte for byte.

**What was not checked.** No person has used the lab. No screen reader has been used on it: what one is given was read from the page's markup and from the browser's tree of names and roles, and nobody has listened to it. It has been run in one browser, headless Chromium on Linux, on no real device. No chemistry instructor has read it.

**Known limits of the polarity and mixing lab's page:**

- **Looking is seeing.** A learner who cannot see the drawing has what each part shows said when the part takes focus, and the whole tube in words behind a disclosure; that is then reading a sentence and choosing the words that match it.
- **The table is a table of buttons.** A screen reader meets each place as a button with the tube's whole name, and the row's and the column's headings as the table gives them; nobody has listened to it.
- **The words of a part hang over the bracket and the word beside it** while the part is looked at, and for the lowest part they reach a few pixels under the picture.
- **The parts of a picture show how many layers there are before a look.** A part not yet looked at has a dashed edge, and each layer is a part with a button named for where it is ("Top layer", "Bottom layer"), from the moment the tube has stood. The brackets and their words come only with the look.
- **In a window 1280 px wide the table is behind "Choose a tube to try",** as on a tablet, so that what is seen can stand beside the picture; the two stand side by side with the tube from about 1,490 px.
- **Two lengths are still over the window:** at 768×1024 a tube that counts after its part is done, up to 962 px where 959 px are in the window; and at 1280×800 the sixth tube of a part once it counts, 816 px with the part's notes, where 735 px are.
- **The links of an open list of sources are 16 to 32 px tall,** as in every lab.
- **A tube under way cannot be emptied and begun again;** it can once it has been checked and does not count. Which liquid there is more of cannot be changed once the tube is filled.
- **A button that cannot be used now is drawn at half strength,** as in every lab: 3.7 to 1 in the light theme. It still takes focus, and says why when pressed.
- **What is chosen in a form and not yet recorded is dropped** when the page is left or reloaded, and when the mode is changed. Which tube was shown is not stored: a reload opens the tube that is under way, or the table. Which parts of a picture were looked at is not stored either: a tube come back to while it is under way is looked at again.
- Below 768 pixels of width the Lab tab shows a notice in place of the bench, as in every lab.

### The endothermic and exothermic lab: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The lab's screens were built on 2026-10-12 and checked that day by the AI coding assistant that wrote them, by script, in headless Chromium on Linux, on the production build. Nobody sat at a keyboard. The page is built on the pieces of the labs before it and draws nothing of its own: the frame and its tabs, the paged list of parts, the foam cup's drawing, the logger's graph that is explored, the worksheet with its typed entries and its choice boxes, the hazards' tile and Guided's warning. The lab's one deep review, by a reviewer who built none of it, has not yet been held.

**What the page is.** Three parts, one at a time. The first two are a run of a salt each, as a column of cards: the bench; the cup, with four step buttons; the graph, with "Run on" over it and a button over each reading; a notebook of six entries, three typed and three chosen from two boxes each; and how the run came out. The third is two questions, each answered by choosing one of two salts.

**What was run, by script:**

- **Eight whole labs,** from Start to the line that says the lab is complete: in Guided and in Challenge, each at 768×1024 and at 1280×800 in the light theme and in the dark. Guided was worked by the pointer, with the graph walked by the arrow keys and each typed entry made with Enter; Challenge at 768×1024 by emulated touch, with the graph walked by the two buttons under it. Every temperature was typed from a point's callout, and every choice made from the two temperatures typed. In Guided the salt was first added with the lid off and the stirrer still, a temperature a tenth of a degree off was typed, and a wrong choice and a wrong answer were given: each was refused with its sentence and nothing was stored. In Challenge part 3 was answered wrongly, checked, marked, and answered again. The browser reported no error.
- **Where focus goes.** The warning took focus when it was raised, and "Go back" put focus on the step that was skipped. As the salt went in, focus went to the line the cup's card folds under. When a notebook was checked, focus was on how the run came out; when part 3 was checked, on the line that says how it stands; and "Answer again" put it on the part's heading.
- **Nothing moves under the hand.** "Run on" stood at one place in the window through every press of it, in sixteen runs of a salt at the two sizes, measured before each press and after the last.
- **Sizes and hit areas, point by point.** On six states of the page (a cup with Guided's warning up, a notebook just opened, a notebook with a choice waiting, a finished run, part 3 open, part 3 done), at both sizes, with each disclosure open in turn, every point of every control's box and of the hit area laid over a disclosure's line was asked which control answers there. As first built, the open hint under an entry that is chosen lay over the foot of the "Enter" button above it; the hint was given room, and none then answered for another. The least room between a laid area and the next control was 4 px. No button, field or disclosure's area was under 44 px either way. The columns over the graph's readings meet by design, and from the ninth reading are narrower than a finger where the picture is narrow: the two buttons under the graph step from reading to reading.
- **Lengths.** At 768×1024 with the menu open, 959 px of the window are under the bar that holds the tabs. The cup's card is 328 px and the graph's 584 px, 924 px with the room between them: inside one screen. Once the salt is in, the bench and the cup fold to 90 px each. The whole page is 1,127 px at Start, 1,722 px with the logger started, 1,613 px as the salt goes in, 1,855 px as the notebook opens, 2,385 px with the last entry waiting and 1,853 px when the run is finished. At 1280×800, where 735 px are under the bar, the cup and the graph together are 882 px.
- **A reload** in the middle of a notebook opened the Lab tab on that part, with its bench and its cup folded and its three entries made.
- **Motion.** With reduced motion asked for, no element of the Lab tab had a transition or an animation over a millisecond with the stirrer going; without it, the stirrer's four bobs and the buttons' own transitions.
- **Colour is not the only signal.** A reading that has been looked at is a filled point and one that has not an open one; the line where the salt went in is dashed; a step that is done has a tick; an entry that follows has a tick and the word, and one that does not a cross and a sentence. The two salts are drawn alike.
- **Both themes, two sizes.** Seventy-two pictures were taken, eighteen at each size in each theme. The builder looked at the two graphs with a point looked at in each theme, a cup and graph before the salt, a graph while the temperature moves, Guided's warning, a notebook part worked, and part 3, and not at the rest.
- **By automated tests,** which run without a browser: the acceptance list of the design spec (section 26.5) over seeds 1 to 100; the description in words of the cup and of the graph at each stage; the markup of the page at each stage in both modes; that no reading is printed beside the graph; and that, at every step of a careful run in Challenge up to the check, nothing on the part says which way the salt's temperature goes outside the notebook's own choices and the graph in words, which is closed.

**What was not checked.** No contrast was measured for this lab: it draws no colour of its own, and the figures for the pieces it is built on are under the calorimetry lab and the freezing point depression lab above. No whole lab was worked by the keyboard alone: the graph and the typed entries were, and the Tab key was followed from a part's heading through the cup's steps to "Run on", but the choice boxes were pressed by the pointer. No person has used the lab. No screen reader has been used on it. It has been run in one browser, headless Chromium on Linux, on no real device. No chemistry instructor has read it.

**Known limits of the endothermic and exothermic lab's page:**

- **Looking at the graph is seeing.** A learner who cannot see it has each reading said when its point takes focus, and the whole graph in words behind a disclosure; that description names the two readings the notebook's first entries ask for.
- **A pointer that rests where the page moves under it** looks at whatever reading comes under it, as in the two labs that draw this graph.
- **A run's page is two screens of an upright tablet,** and two and a half while the last entry waits; at 1280×800 the cup and the graph before the salt are 147 px over one screen.
- **The cup's drawing is folded away as the salt goes in,** so the emptied weighing boat is seen only by opening the cup's line; the cup's words say that the salt is in.
- **The links of an open list of sources are the height of their text,** as in every lab.
- **A button that cannot be used now is drawn at half strength,** as in every lab. It still takes focus, and says why when pressed.
- **What is typed or chosen and not yet entered is dropped** when the page is left or reloaded. Which part was shown is not stored: a reload opens the first part that is begun and not done.
- Below 768 pixels of width the Lab tab shows a notice in place of the bench, as in every lab.

### The mechanism timeline: accessibility

As above, there has been no audit and this is no claim of conformance.

**How it was checked, and by whom.** The timeline's page was built and checked on 2026-10-08 by the AI coding assistant that wrote it. A script drove the production build in headless Chromium 153 on Linux: 200 checks, all passing at the last run. It worked the page by keyboard at 1024×768 in the light theme, and by tap at 820×1180 in each theme, in a browser context that emulates a touch screen; it looked at the dark theme at 1024×768, and ran once each with the operating system set to reduce motion and with WebGL taken away. WebGL was taken away by a script that made the browser hand out no WebGL context; no browser that really lacks it was used. A second, shorter script loaded the page at seven sizes of window. The scripts are not part of the repository. No person took part, no reviewer that did not build the page has looked at it yet, and how the page looks (colour, spacing, the drawn scene and beaker, the chart) is for the project's author to judge by eye and is not reported here.

**What was checked that way:**

- **The whole timeline by keyboard.** With focus on Next, Enter went through the seven chapters, each at its start, to the end of the track, where the run was stored as completed and one line said so. Focus stayed on Next throughout. Going back and to the end again did not say it a second time. The Tab key alone, from the page's heading, reached the mode, the energy panel's button, the periodic table link, Play, Previous, Next, the slider, the scene's words, the chapter's longer text, the chapter's link, and in a step each name in the list of what is in the scene.
- **Play.** From the start, Play moved the timeline by itself to the end in about a minute, never backwards and with no stop in Guided; Pause stopped it; a move of the slider by hand stopped it; at the end the button offered to play again from the start.
- **The same frame both ways.** One place inside step 2 was reached going forwards and again coming back from step 3, by the arrow keys on the slider. The scene's words were the same, and the picture of the scene was the same pixel for pixel. The same was found in the tap run, where the slider was set back to the place by script. The picture was not blank in either theme.
- **The camera.** A drag with a mouse turned the scene. The turned view held while the timeline was still, and the chapter's own framing was back, pixel for pixel, once the timeline had moved a step and back. In one run of five the picture taken a little after the drag differed from the one taken just after it; the cause was not found and it did not happen again.
- **Naming what is in the scene.** A mouse pointer moved over the scene brought up the names of at least three molecules or ions, each with its formula, and the callout went when the pointer left. Keyboard focus on a name in the list beside the scene put that callout on its molecule, inside the picture; Tab moved it to the next name and Escape put it away. A tap on a molecule in the scene named it, a tap elsewhere put the callout away, and a tap on a name in the list brought it up.
- **Controls that cannot be used yet.** Previous at the start, Next at the end, and Play and Next while a question waits each stayed in the tab order, were marked unavailable and not disabled, and said why when pressed, in a status line under the buttons. A sentence already showing was said the second time with "Again:" before it.
- **Challenge, by keyboard and by tap.** With the mode set to Challenge, no step's name was on the page ahead of its question. Next stopped at the start of step 1 with the question, which was also in the status line that announces chapters. One answer that is not right was pressed and one that is: each was told so in words with a mark before them, with the answer that was chosen shown, and the chapter's own text followed. By keyboard, focus went to the chapter's heading, since the answer's button was gone. The stored record held which answer was chosen and not whether it was right. The slider went past a question that had not been answered. Play went on by itself and stopped at the next question. Back in Guided the question was gone.
- **A reload in the middle** came back to the same place on the timeline, in the same mode, with the answers given and the feedback under the heading, and left the stored record as it was.
- **Links to chapters.** Each of the seven chapters' links opened at that chapter's start and stored nothing. A chapter's own link, followed from the middle of the chapter, went to its start without moving focus to the top of the page; the learner's first action after that removed the link from the address.
- **The energy panel** opened by keyboard and by tap as a panel that is not modal, stayed open while the timeline moved, and moved its cursor with it. It named the step the timeline was in, and its table, a table with a caption and a header for each column and row, held the four steps' own values. Escape closed it. It lay inside the window at both sizes.
- **Colour is not the only signal.** A bond that is forming or breaking is drawn as dashes, thinner the less of it there is. Free energy is the heavier, whole curve and enthalpy the thinner, broken one, and each has its name written at its end. The chapter the timeline is in has a heavy edge and a bold number on the track, and the step it is in has a line of words in the energy table. A right answer and one that is not are words with a mark. Which species take part in a chapter is said by a heading over their names. These were read in the page's markup by automated tests and seen in the screenshots; no measurement of contrast was made.
- **Reduced motion.** With the operating system set to reduce motion, Play was not on the page and one line said why. Nothing moved in more than a second of waiting, Next put the scene at the next chapter at once with nothing gliding afterwards, and the browser reported no animation running.
- **Without WebGL** the frame said that the 3D model could not start and held the scene in words in its place, once; the beaker was still drawn at the start; the chapter, the list, the timeline, Play and the energy panel worked; the words changed as the timeline played; and the 3D code was not fetched.
- **A stored record that does not fit** (one with a position off the track) was set aside with the notice, the page started at the beginning, and the notice went once a new run was saved. Other kinds of unreadable record were not tried on this page; the storage code that sets them aside is the labs' and has its own automated tests.
- **Sizes.** At 400×800, 768×1024, 820×1180, 1024×768, 1180×820, 1366×768 and 1920×1080 the page did not scroll sideways. With the energy panel open, every button, link, slider and disclosure of the page and the panel had a hit area of at least 44 pixels each way, except the two radio buttons of the mode's choice, which are the labs' own control, and the links of the breadcrumb. Several are drawn smaller, with the hit area laid over them as on the labs' pages.
- **The rest of the app.** Home, the list of labs, each lab and the Learn page were loaded and fetched neither the 3D code nor the timeline's own code. The search opened the timeline. A molecule page was still drawn in 3D after the explorer's 3D shapes were moved to a file the two scenes share.
- **Both themes** were loaded, and the browser's console was empty throughout. The page requested nothing but its own files.

**The review of the phase, the same day.** An AI coding assistant that had not built the timeline reviewed the whole phase on 2026-10-08, changed what it found, and ran its own scripts against the production build as it stood after those changes, in headless Chromium 153 on Linux with WebGL drawn in software: 371 checks, all passing at the last run of each script. The scripts are not part of the repository. No person took part. What it found and changed is the last section of `docs/research/phase-5/build-record.md`. What it checked:

- **The whole timeline in Challenge, by keyboard at 1024×768 and by tap at 820×1180,** with two answers that are not right and two that are, to the end, and then "Start again": the button asked first, a refusal changed nothing, and an acceptance put the timeline at its start with nothing answered, still in Challenge, with focus still on the button and one line that said so. A question answered wrongly in the first run was asked again in the second and answered rightly.
- **That nothing answers a question before it is asked.** Just before each of the four questions, with the energy panel closed and again with it open and every disclosure on the page open, the script read the whole document, text and attributes, less the question's own card, for the names of the steps and for the words each right answer turns on. It found none. The picture at each of those four places was looked at as a screenshot.
- **Guided:** Play from the start to the end, through all seven chapters with no stop, and slower than the minute it is paced for, the drawing being done in software; the slider forwards and then backwards over 51 places, with the same words at each both ways; the same picture, pixel for pixel, at four places reached from either side; and at the start, the middle and the end of every chapter, that the heading, the slider's words, the announcement, the list and the scene's description name the same chapter and the same things, and that each name in the list, focused, has its callout in the scene.
- **Saving.** Five seconds of playing wrote the stored run three times at most, not three hundred; Pause wrote where it stopped; leaving the page while it played kept the place; a whole play wrote it seven times.
- **The camera, by mouse and by touch.** A drag turned the scene, the wheel zoomed it without scrolling the page, the turned view held while the timeline was still, and the chapter's framing was back, pixel for pixel, once the timeline moved, also in "Before mixing", where the chapter's own camera stands still. With touch events sent by script: a one-finger drag that starts on the scene turns it and does not scroll the page; one that starts under the scene scrolls the page; a pinch zooms; a tap on a molecule names it, a tap elsewhere puts the name away, and a tap on a name in the list brings it up.
- **Reduced motion, by the app's own setting** on the Settings page: no Play, one line that says why, the whole timeline finished in Challenge with Next and the four answers, nothing moving by itself, and no animation running.
- **Without WebGL,** again by a script that made the browser hand out no WebGL context: the beaker at both ends, a question asked with the scene in words in the scene's place and nothing of the answer in them, the words following the timeline, Play, and the end reached; the 3D code was not fetched.
- **Links to each of the seven chapters;** a link opened over a stored run, which showed the link's place, left the stored run alone, and took the place at the learner's first action; a reload in the middle of a Challenge run, which came back to the place, the mode, the answer and its feedback, and did not ask again.
- **Stored records that do not fit:** another version, text that is not JSON, a position off the track, an answer to no question, and a field too many. Each was set aside under a backup key with the notice, and the notice went when a new run was saved.
- **Two tabs.** A move in one was taken by the other. A tab that was playing stopped and took the place the other tab saved, and wrote nothing over it afterwards. "Start again" in one put the other at the start, and "Clear my progress" in Settings left the open timeline at its start with nothing stored.
- **A window made narrower with the page open,** from 1366 pixels to 820 and to 400: the page did not scroll sideways after a fault found this way was put right. Every button, link, slider and disclosure of the page and of the open panel had a hit area of at least 44 pixels each way at 400×800, 768×1024, 820×1180, 1024×768 and 1366×768, with the mode's choice closed, but the links of the breadcrumb.
- **The energy panel** at 1024×768, 1366×768 and 820×1180: where it opened and what it covered, that its cursor moved with the timeline while it stayed open, and that every figure it printed, with everything in it open, was one of the ten the record has for the four steps and their totals.
- **The rest of the app.** Home, the list of labs, each lab, Learn, Settings and About fetched neither the 3D code nor the timeline's; two molecule pages were drawn in 3D and turned by a drag, and a click still chose an atom; one station of the aqueous reactions lab was worked to the end by keyboard; and the titration lab's primer and bench loaded. The console was empty throughout, and nothing was requested but the app's own files.
- **Screenshots of the scene** at the start, the middle and the end of every chapter, at 1024×768, at 820×1180 and, in the dark theme, at 1366×768, were looked at by the reviewing assistant for a species cut by the edge, one thing drawn over another, and whether the charges and the arrows could be read. That is one assistant's reading of pictures; how the scene looks is still for the project's author to judge.

**What has not been checked:**

- **No screen reader was used.** What is announced when the timeline enters a chapter, when a question comes, when an answer is told right or not, or when the end is reached has not been heard by anyone. The roles, names and states were read by script.
- **Chromium only, and no real device.** No other browser, no phone and no tablet. The taps were made by script in a desktop Chromium that emulates a touch screen. Turning the scene with a finger and a pinch to zoom were tried only as touch events sent by script to that browser (at the review, below), never with a finger on glass.
- **Contrast was not measured:** not the grey text, not the faint text of a button that cannot be used, not the lines of the chart, and not the scene, where an electron pair's arrow is yellow with a dark edge and a callout is black on white in both themes.
- Browser zoom, enlarged text and forced-colour (high contrast) mode have not been tried. No person with a colour-vision deficiency has looked at the page.
- The Page Up and Page Down keys on the slider were not tried; Home, End and the arrow keys were.
- Nobody has gone through the timeline by hand, with a keyboard or otherwise.

**Known limits:**

- **The 3D scene needs WebGL 2.** Without it the scene is given in words, which is a full account of what is where and what is changing but is not a picture.
- **The camera needs a pointer.** There is no keyboard control for turning or zooming the scene, and nothing on the page depends on doing so. A turn or a zoom lasts until the timeline next moves, so a step cannot be watched playing from the learner's own angle.
- A drag on the scene on a touch screen turns the scene and does not scroll the page, as in the molecule explorer.
- **The scene is one image to assistive technology,** with a name and with the scene's words as its description. The callouts in it are for the eye; the list beside the scene gives the same names. The description changes at every position and is not announced as it changes; only the chapter is.
- The feedback on an answered question is in a status region under the chapter's heading, so it is likely to be announced again each time the learner comes back into that step.
- **The track's marks show only a step's number.** A chapter's share of the track is too narrow for its name. The name is the heading beside the scene, the real timescale of the step the timeline is in is one line under the track, and a screen reader is given all seven names, and the four timescales, in the list.
- One press of an arrow key moves the slider by a two-hundredth of the track. Play has one speed.
- **In Challenge the first answer to a question is the one kept,** until the learner presses "Start again" beside the mode, which asks first and then puts the timeline at its start with nothing answered. It erases the place and the answers of this timeline only.
- While a question waits, the scene still shows every molecule and ion that is in it, and the list still names them. What is kept back is the step's name, its text, its real time, and which of the species it is about; before step 1 the solid is out of the picture and is named as one unit, not as its two ions; and until every question is answered the energy panel gives its figures without its notes, its working or its sources.
- The energy panel floats over the page. In a window 820 pixels wide and 1180 tall it opened under the timeline and covered neither the scene nor the slider; it covered the right-hand part of the chapter's words, or of a question's answers, and of the list under them, until it was moved or closed. In a shorter or narrower window it covers part of the scene or the slider until it is moved: the slider at 768×1024, and both at 400×800. At 1024×768 it opens over the chapter's words and over the right edge of the scene, by about 30 pixels.
- **What looks on can be cut by the edge of the picture.** The camera frames the species a chapter is about, whole. A molecule or an ion that only looks on may be half inside the picture or outside it, and the list beside the scene still names it. In step 1 the solid comes into the picture from above and is not in it when the step begins; in step 4 the carbon dioxide leaves the picture before the step ends.
- A formula in a callout or in the list is written with the raised and lowered characters of plain text, which a screen reader may read oddly.

## Finance and procurement

- **Cost:** free. There are no paid tiers.
- **Licence:** MIT, which permits use, copying, modification, and redistribution, including by institutions.
- **Contract and vendor:** none. There is no company behind Valence, no service-level agreement, and no guaranteed support.
- **Hosting cost if self-hosted:** that of serving a few megabytes of static files.

## Faculty and curriculum

**What exists.** The molecule explorer, the periodic table, fourteen labs (the standardization of a base by titration, described under "The titration lab" below; reactions in aqueous solution, under "The aqueous reactions lab" after it; flame tests, under "The flame test lab" after that; calorimetry, under "The calorimetry lab" after that; the Group I cations, under "The Group I cations lab" after that; Le Chatelier's principle, under "The Le Chatelier's principle lab" after that; the clock reaction, under "The clock reaction lab" after that; the iodination of acetone, under "The iodination of acetone lab" after that; red cabbage pH, under "The red cabbage pH lab" after that; measurement and density, under "The measurement and density lab" after that; freezing point depression, under "The freezing point depression lab" after that; types of reactions, under "The types of reactions lab" after that; polarity and mixing, under "The polarity and mixing lab" after that; and endothermic and exothermic, under "The endothermic and exothermic lab" after that) and one mechanism timeline, under "The mechanism timeline" after those.

**What the explorer shows.** A library of 101 molecules and ions in six groups: basics (25), a VSEPR showcase (13), organic functional groups (24), biomolecules (14), everyday molecules (22: caffeine, aspirin, vanillin and others a person knows by name or by smell) and the species of the mechanism timeline (3). Glycine, alanine, serine and cysteine each have a neutral and a zwitterion form, so there are 105 structures. Thirty-one of the entries are the first library; the other 70 were added on 2026-10-09 (see "The molecules added on 2026-10-09" below). The library page has a field that narrows the list by name or formula as it is typed. For each one:

- Four views: ball and stick, space filling (van der Waals radii), wireframe, and a 2D Lewis structure with lone pairs and formal charges.
- An atom card: symbol, atomic number, standard atomic weight, electronegativity, valence electrons, formal charge, lone pairs, oxidation state, hybridization and VSEPR geometry, with electron configuration, ionization energies and radii one step further in.
- A bond card: length, order, σ and π composition, and polarity as the Pauling electronegativity difference, labelled "a rough guide".
- A measure tool: two atoms give a distance, three an angle, four a dihedral.
- Three valence display levels: a Bohr diagram of the selected atom, labelled "a simplified picture"; lone pairs as dots; and hybrid orbital lobes for sp, sp² and sp³ atoms, with molecular orbital diagrams for O₂ and N₂ only. The O₂ diagram points out that the Lewis structure shows every electron paired while the diagram shows two unpaired.

**Where each kind of value in the explorer comes from.** The cards list their sources, and the About page lists all of them.

| Value | Source |
| --- | --- |
| Standard atomic weight | IUPAC CIAAW abridged standard atomic weights, 2024 |
| Electron configuration, ionization energies | NIST Atomic Spectra Database |
| Electronegativity (Pauling) | PubChem periodic table |
| Covalent, van der Waals and ionic radii | Cordero et al. 2008; Mantina et al. 2009; Shannon 1976 |
| Geometry of 19 basics and VSEPR molecules | Experimental gas-phase structures from NIST CCCBDB, each citing the original paper: the 13 of the first library, and six added later (hydrogen, carbon monoxide, ozone, sulfur tetrafluoride, chlorine trifluoride, xenon difluoride) |
| Geometry of the other 86 structures | PubChem3D computed conformers (MMFF94s force field). These are not measurements, and each page says so. |
| 2D layouts for the Lewis view | PubChem 2D records, except six authored drawings: sulfur dioxide (PubChem draws it as a straight line), ammonia, hydronium and phosphorus trichloride (PubChem draws a flat Y; here the lone pair is on top), and carbon dioxide and xenon difluoride (drawn straight and horizontal). The layout of a molecule added on 2026-10-09 is turned as a whole, by a multiple of 30°, where PubChem draws a long molecule on end or on a slant, so that it lies along the frame and can be read on a tablet. |
| The sentence that says what an everyday molecule is | Written from the molecule's PubChem record, in Valence's own words; `docs/research/phase-2/notes.json` records where in the record each was read |
| Molecular orbital order for O₂ and N₂ | OpenStax Chemistry 2e, section 8.4 |
| Formal charge and lone pairs | Authored with the Lewis structure; checked by the validator |
| Element colours in the models | Jmol CPK colours |
| Oxidation state, steric number, VSEPR geometry, hybridization, bond order, σ and π counts | Computed in the app from the stored structure by tested functions |
| Lengths, angles and dihedrals | Measured from the stored coordinates. For the 19 experimental structures a bond card shows the cited value; automated tests hold the coordinates to the cited lengths within 0.01 Å and the cited angles within 1°. |

The computed values are checked against a table of expected values written from the research: every atom of every structure. Oxidation states are tested to sum to each species' charge, and every stored formal charge is checked by the validator.

**Conventions chosen.** Where textbooks differ, Valence follows a general chemistry text (OpenStax Chemistry 2e) and notes the convention on the card where it applies:

- **Hybridization** comes from the steric number. The O–H and ester oxygens of acids and esters are sp³ and bent; their card adds that organic chemistry describes them as sp², because one lone pair is delocalised into the neighbouring C=O. The oxygen of a phenol or of an aryl ether is treated the same way, and its card names the aromatic ring. A nitrogen with a lone pair next to a double bond of carbon is the one exception: the nitrogen of an amide or of urea, and the N–H of a ring such as that of serotonin or of indigo, is shown as sp² and trigonal planar, as its model is.
- **Terminal atoms.** Hydrogen reads "none (1s)". A terminal atom with a double or triple bond shows a value (a carbonyl oxygen is sp²). Singly bonded halogens and O⁻ read "not assigned (terminal atom)" and get no VSEPR geometry.
- **Expanded octets.** Steric numbers 5 and 6 read "sp³d (textbook label)" and "sp³d² (textbook label)", with the note that real d-orbital involvement is negligible. No lobes are drawn for them.
- **Sulfur dioxide** is drawn in the octet-obeying resonance form: one S=O, one S–O, formal charges +1 on sulfur and −1 on one oxygen. Its bond cards say this is one of two equivalent structures.
- **Sulfate, phosphate, sulfuric acid, phosphoric acid and sulfur trioxide** are drawn the same way, in the octet-obeying form: single bonds from sulfur or phosphorus to every oxygen (one S=O in sulfur trioxide), a formal charge of +2 on sulfur or +1 on phosphorus, and −1 on each oxygen that carries no hydrogen. Many texts, and PubChem's records, draw S=O and P=O double bonds instead, which lowers the formal charges and gives the central atom more than eight electrons. Oxidation states, geometry and hybridization are the same in both drawings. The page of each of the five says that both drawings are in use.
- **Resonance elsewhere.** The carboxylate of each zwitterion and of the acetate ion, the two unprotonated C–O bonds of bicarbonate, the two O–O bonds of ozone and the two N–O bonds of nitric acid to the oxygens that carry no hydrogen are drawn as one structure of two, and read as order 1.5. The three bonds of nitrate, of carbonate and of sulfur trioxide are drawn as one structure of three, and read as order 1.33. The bonds of a benzene ring are shown as aromatic, order 1.5, wherever one occurs; a ring that holds nitrogen is not (see the known limits).
- **Oxidation state** gives each bond's electrons to the more electronegative atom (Pauling) and splits them between atoms of the same element. Every atom of an uncharged element (H₂, O₂, N₂, Cl₂, ozone) is 0.
- **Geometry.** Nineteen small molecules use experimental gas-phase geometry. The rest use one computed conformer; ethanol, glucose and ethyl acetate carry the note "one conformer of many", and so does every molecule added later for which PubChem lists more than one conformer.

**Known limits.**

- The covalent, van der Waals and ionic radii come from paywalled papers and were checked only against secondary transcriptions that agree with each other.
- The xenon electronegativity (2.6) has no traced primary source. The atom card says so under the value.
- PubChem 3D structures are computed conformers, not measurements. The bicarbonate conformer shows its two equivalent C–O bonds at different lengths; its page says so. The 19 molecules added to the basics and VSEPR groups that use a computed conformer (hydrogen fluoride, chlorine, hydrogen sulfide, the acids, the ions and others) were not compared with measured structures; the first 13 of those groups are all experimental.
- **Rings that hold nitrogen are not marked aromatic.** The pyridine ring of nicotine, the rings of adenine, thymine, caffeine and theobromine, and the five-membered ring of serotonin and of indigo are stored as PubChem draws them, with their double bonds where they are drawn. "Order 1.5" is the textbook statement for a benzene ring and not for these, so the library says nothing of their aromaticity.
- **Three molecules that were asked for are not in the library.** Beryllium chloride and bromine pentafluoride: beryllium and bromine are not among the 11 elements of the explorer's element table, and adding an element takes values from sources that were not read for them (the radii above). Without bromine pentafluoride the library has no square pyramidal molecule. The triiodide ion: NIST's database has no experimental geometry for it, and iodine is not in the element table either.
- **The sentence under an everyday molecule says only what its PubChem record says.** Geosmin "has an earthy smell" (nothing read says it is the smell of rain); indigo is "a dye" (nothing read says it is blue). The record whose structure is used for indigo (CID 5318432) has no description; the sentence rests on PubChem's main record for indigo (CID 10215), which draws a tautomer, and says so.
- **The zwitterion note on serine and cysteine** ("the form found in water and in the solid") is the sentence the spec gives every zwitterion. It was not read again for these two.
- The hydrogen peroxide H–O–O angle of 94.8° is the 1962 measurement NIST lists; later measurements give about 100–102°. Its page says so.
- Hybrid lobes are a simplified shape drawn along each bond and lone-pair direction. The lone pair of an amide or urea nitrogen is described in a note and not drawn as a lobe.
- The molecular orbital diagrams show the order of the levels, not their energies.
- The chemistry has been checked against the cited sources by script and by a second pass over the research. It has not been reviewed by a chemistry instructor.

### The molecules added on 2026-10-09

Seventy entries (72 structures) were added to the first 31, and the Everyday group with them.

- **Where each came from.** PubChem's record by its CID: the 3D conformer and the 2D layout were fetched once from PubChem's public REST service, one request at a time with a pause of five seconds, and are kept under `docs/research/phase-2/sdf/`. PubChem has no 3D conformer for hydrogen, chlorine trifluoride and xenon difluoride. Its conformer of sulfur tetrafluoride is tetrahedral, which that molecule is not; its conformer of ozone has two O–O bonds 0.2 Å apart, where the real molecule's are equal; and its conformer of carbon monoxide has a bond 0.07 Å shorter than the measured one. Those six are built from the lengths and angles that NIST's CCCBDB prints on its experimental geometry page for each, read on the same day; the pages are not kept, and `geometry.json` holds each figure as printed, with the paper the page cites.
- **How the chemistry was checked.** A table of expected values (formal charge, lone pairs, oxidation state, steric number, geometry and hybridization of every atom; order, aromatic flag and resonance note of every bond) was written by a script that shares no code with the app, and read through by the AI coding assistant that wrote it: every atom that is not a hydrogen, except, in the last 22 structures, the methyl, CH₂ and aromatic C–H carbons, which all read alike. The build stops if the Lewis structure it builds differs from that table, and the tests hold what the app derives to it. Twenty-nine named cases written by hand cover the ones a course asks about: ozone, carbon monoxide, hydrogen cyanide, nitrate, carbonate, sulfate, phosphate, the two acids, sulfur trioxide, the three new shapes, phenol, nicotine, serotonin and others. **This was one pass.** The first 33 structures had a second, independent pass that fetched the sources again; these have not, and no chemistry instructor has reviewed them.
- **What had to change in the model.** Four things, each with tests: an atom of an uncharged element is given oxidation state 0 (the rule for like atoms left each oxygen of ozone its formal charge); lone pairs are placed on atoms of five electron domains (seesaw, T-shaped, linear); the note on an oxygen or nitrogen whose lone pair is delocalised names what it is next to, a C=O, a C=C or an aromatic ring; and the validator, which refuses a bond to hydrogen shorter than 0.80 Å as a sign of a 2D drawing, lets the bond of H₂ be 0.741 Å.
- **What was looked at.** In headless Chromium, by the AI coding assistant, at 820×1180 and at 1366×768 in the light theme: the library page and its filter; and 30 of the added structures opened as a 3D model and as a Lewis structure, with the atom and bond cards read for up to three atoms and one bond of each (every one added to the VSEPR group, every ion, the four largest, and ozone, carbon monoxide, nitric acid, caffeine, indigo, adenine, cysteine, phenol, toluene, hydrogen and the serine zwitterion). The values on the cards were the expected ones and the browser's console was clean. Palmitic acid (50 atoms), capsaicin (49), sucrose (45) and aspartame (39) were looked at as Lewis structures on the tablet's frame and none is a tangle; in aspartame one hydrogen of the ring is drawn close beside an oxygen, as PubChem's layout has it.
- **What was not checked.** The other 42 added structures were not opened in a browser; the tests draw the layout of every one. Nothing was looked at in the dark theme, at a phone's width, or with a screen reader. No person has looked at any of it.

### The periodic table

**What it shows.** All 118 elements in the 18-column layout, with cerium to lutetium and thorium to lawrencium in two rows below. The table can be coloured by chemical family or by one of five properties (electronegativity, covalent radius, first ionization energy, melting point, density), shaded on a linear scale from the lowest to the highest value, with each value printed in its cell. A group, a period, a block or a family can be highlighted, and each group, period and block has a short description of what its members share. Choosing an element shows a card with its atomic mass, group, period, block, family, electron configuration, a shell diagram with the electrons per shell, electronegativity, covalent and van der Waals radii, first ionization energy, electron affinity, common oxidation states, state at 25 °C, melting point, boiling point, density, year discovered, links to the library molecules that contain it, an emission spectrum for the 97 elements that have one (and for each of the other 21 a sentence that says what NIST lists for it), and its sources. Each atom card in the molecule explorer links to its element.

**Where each kind of value comes from.** Unlike the explorer's values, which were curated by hand, the table is built by a script from saved copies of its sources, with nothing fetched while the app runs. Every number is shown in the digits its source prints, with one exception: a cell of the table too narrow for a value prints it rounded, and the legend says so; the element's card always gives the value in full. The research record, including the saved copies, is in `docs/research/periodic-table/`.

| Value | Source | Elements |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Symbol, name, chemical family, electron configuration, state at 25 °C, oxidation states, year discovered | PubChem periodic table, downloaded 2026-10-03 | 118 (oxidation states 117) |
| Standard atomic weight | IUPAC CIAAW abridged standard atomic weights, 2024 | 84 |
| Mass of one named isotope, for elements with no standard atomic weight | PubChem periodic table | 34 |
| First ionization energy | NIST Atomic Spectra Database, with NIST's marking where a value is not measured | 1 to 108 |
| Electronegativity (Pauling scale) | PubChem periodic table | 95 |
| Covalent radius | Cordero et al. 2008, as carried on PubChem's element pages | 1 to 96 |
| Van der Waals radius | PubChem periodic table (from Los Alamos National Laboratory, which names no study) | 99 |
| Electron affinity | PubChem periodic table (from a 1990 compilation) | 57 |
| Melting point, boiling point, density | PubChem periodic table (from Jefferson Lab) | 103, 93, 96 |
| Emission lines of the neutral atom, 400 to 700 nm | NIST Atomic Spectra Database, lines query, saved as returned on 2026-10-08 (`docs/research/emission-spectra/`) | 97 |
| Group, period, block, electrons per shell | Worked out in the app from the atomic number and the configuration by tested functions | 118 |
| Descriptions of groups, periods and blocks; notes in the legends | Written for Valence from OpenStax Chemistry 2e, the IUPAC Red Book, and the IUPAC and Royal Society of Chemistry periodic tables. Beside each sentence the source code stores what it rests on: the passage itself where it is from OpenStax (licensed CC BY 4.0), and otherwise where the passage is and what it establishes. | |

**Cross-check.** A script compares the saved sources with each other and writes a report (`docs/research/periodic-table/cross-check.md`). Its outcome decided which source is shown:

- **Atomic weight.** PubChem's column agrees with CIAAW for 80 of 84 elements; argon and iron are rounding ties; lithium (7.0 against 6.94) and sulfur (32.07 against 32.06) differ. CIAAW's values are shown.
- **First ionization energy.** PubChem's column agrees with NIST for 76 of the 102 elements both list, and differs for 26, nine of them by 0.1 eV or more. NIST's values are shown.
- **Electron configuration.** PubChem agrees with NIST's ground state for 107 of 108 elements. Lawrencium differs; PubChem's is shown, with a note giving NIST's. NIST has no entry for elements 109 to 118.
- **Electronegativity, melting point, boiling point, density, van der Waals radius.** Compared once, with Wikipedia's transcriptions of handbooks and not with a primary source. The values that stood far from the other tabulations are shown as PubChem has them, each with a note on the card.
- **Electron affinity, covalent radius, oxidation states, year discovered.** Not compared value by value with an independent source.

The report lists 41 values as unresolved: disagreements no decision settles, and values PubChem gives as plain facts that are predictions, estimates or untraced. A test fails if any of them has no note on its card.

**Notes.** A value that needs a caution carries a sentence beside it on the card, with its own source: 107 notes on 57 elements. Among them: helium's melting point (it is solid only under pressure); carbon's and arsenic's melting and boiling points (both elements turn from solid straight to gas at ordinary pressure); the density of each gas (it is for the gas at 0 °C and atmospheric pressure, which PubChem does not state and which was worked out from the values); the years for aluminum, calcium, fluorine and silicon, which are not the years those elements were first isolated; the oxidation states of krypton, xenon and radon (PubChem's list shows only 0, although compounds such as XeF₄ exist); the melting points, states and masses of the heaviest elements that are estimates or predictions; and the eight covalent radii that PubChem's pages give with no uncertainty, among them helium's and francium's, the two ends of that colour scale.

**Conventions chosen.**

- **Group 3 and the f block.** Lanthanum and actinium are in group 3 and the d block; the f block is cerium to lutetium and thorium to lawrencium, 14 in each row, with no group number. This is how the Royal Society of Chemistry's table is drawn and how OpenStax Chemistry 2e assigns the blocks. PubChem puts lutetium and lawrencium in group 3; a provisional report of an IUPAC task group in 2021 leaned the same way, and IUPAC has not ruled. The page says so in a note on group 3. Helium is in group 18 and in the s block.
- **Chemical families.** PubChem's ten categories are used as given, under the heading "Chemical family (PubChem's grouping)". Four notes below the table, which the legend points to, say that fluorine, chlorine, bromine, iodine and the noble gases helium to radon are nonmetals too, that sources differ on which elements are metalloids, that group 12 is not always counted among the transition metals, and that elements 109 to 118 are placed in their families by position. Polonium and astatine carry a note that PubChem's own pages class them differently.
- **Atomic mass has two kinds.** An element with a standard atomic weight shows it. The other 34 show the mass of one named isotope, with a sentence that the element has no standard atomic weight, and "estimate" from lawrencium on. Bismuth, thorium, protactinium and uranium have a standard atomic weight.
- **"No value listed".** A value its source leaves empty is shown with those words, never as zero and never as "not measured", because an empty cell in these sources does not always mean that.
- **Predicted and expected.** A configuration PubChem marks as predicted or calculated keeps that word, on the card and on the shell diagram; a state it words as expected is shown as expected.
- **Radius.** The table is coloured by covalent radius, which shows the trend a general chemistry course teaches. PubChem's own "atomic radius" column is a van der Waals radius from mixed sources; it is on the card under that name and is not used for colouring.
- **Electron affinity** is given as the energy released when an atom gains an electron, so a positive value means energy is released; the card says that some textbooks use the opposite sign.
- **Oxidation states** are headed "Common oxidation states (PubChem's list, not complete)". **The state** is headed "State at room temperature (25 °C)", because that is what PubChem's column holds; it is not the thermodynamic standard state. **The year** is headed "Year discovered (as PubChem lists it)".
- **Names** are spelled as PubChem spells them (aluminum, cesium). The search also finds "aluminium" and "caesium".
- **The 11 elements the explorer also describes.** Atomic weight, first ionization energy and covalent radius come from the same sources in both places. The van der Waals radii of hydrogen and fluorine differ; the element card shows the table's value and gives the explorer's value and its source in one sentence.

**Emission spectra.** Added on 2026-10-08. NIST's database was asked, once for each element, for the lines of the neutral atom with observed wavelengths in air from 400 to 700 nm, and what came back is kept (`docs/research/emission-spectra/sources/`). The build picks the lines from those copies by one rule: of the lines NIST gives a numeric relative intensity, the six with the largest and every line that ties with the sixth, or all of them where there are six or fewer. 95 elements have a spectrum by the rule. Hydrogen and sodium keep the lines chosen by hand earlier. The other 21 have one sentence and no strip: francium (NIST lists 87 lines in the range and no intensity for any), astatine and fermium (lines, but none of the neutral atom in the range), and mendelevium to oganesson (no lines at all). Every spectrum carries the sentence that says which lines are shown and one note on what the strip leaves out: the light of ions and molecules, and how strong each line is.

**Known limits of the periodic table.**

- The emission spectra show what NIST's database rates, not what an element is best known for. NIST's own help page describes its relative intensities as qualitative, dependent on the light source and without a common scale. Caesium shows three red lines, because NIST gives no intensity to its lines in the blue. No line outside 400 to 700 nm is shown. Ties are not cut, so plutonium shows 49 lines. The marks NIST puts on a line (blended, wide, observed in absorption) are not used and not shown.
- Until 2026-10-08 xenon showed six lines chosen by hand, with a sentence that called them the six strongest by NIST's relative intensity. The response saved that day showed they were the six with the largest transition probability, a different column of NIST's table, and that two of them had no relative intensity. The owner decided the same day that the rule applies to xenon like every other element; its lines were replaced, on the periodic table and in the molecule explorer, and the research record was corrected (`docs/research/emission-spectra/README.md`, `docs/research/phase-2/README.md`).

- No chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed the data, the notes or the descriptions. They were checked by script, and by AI reviewers against the cited pages.
- Only atomic weight, first ionization energy and the electron configurations of elements 1 to 108 were checked against a primary source. Everything else PubChem compiles from Jefferson Lab, Los Alamos National Laboratory and journal papers was taken on PubChem's attribution; the original papers (Cordero 2008, Allred 1961, Myers 1990) were not read.
- The electron affinities are from a 1990 compilation and some are dated: fluorine is 3.339 eV here, and about 3.40 eV in current compilations.
- The van der Waals radii are not one consistent set, and about half match none of the published sets they were compared with. They do not show a clean trend.
- Francium's electronegativity (0.7), the low end of that colour scale, and lawrencium's (1.3) have no source on PubChem's own pages. Both carry a note.
- Helium's covalent radius (28 pm) and francium's (260 pm), the two ends of that colour scale, are printed on PubChem's pages with no uncertainty, as are six others (neon, promethium, astatine, radon, actinium, protactinium); the other 88 radii have one. Why is not known, because the paper the radii come from was not read. Each of the eight carries a note asking the reader to treat it as an estimate, and the legend shows it for both ends.
- For the heaviest elements most values are predictions or are missing. Elements 109 to 118 have no ionization energy, and none from fermium on has a van der Waals radius.
- The scales are linear. For density that puts all 11 gases together at the pale end; for melting point the two ends (helium, carbon) are not ordinary-pressure melting points. The legends say so.
- The descriptions of the groups, periods and blocks are short and general. Nothing checks automatically that each sentence says no more than the passage stored or located beside it.
- The pages the descriptions cite were read on 2026-10-03 and are not saved. Whether PubChem's table has changed since that date has not been rechecked.
- The colours of the table are a design choice, not chemistry data.

### The titration lab

**What it teaches.** Standardizing a sodium hydroxide solution of about 0.1 M against 25.00 mL of hydrochloric acid whose molarity is on the bottle's label, with phenolphthalein as the indicator, on a drawn bench. The learner records the label's molarity, conditions the burette, fills it, clears the air bubble from its tip, reads the meniscus to 0.01 mL, rinses a pipette with the acid and delivers the aliquot, adds 2 or 3 drops of indicator, titrates to a faint pink that lasts, reads the burette again, and calculates the base's molarity as the acid's molarity × 25.00 mL ÷ the titre. Trials are repeated until three count. The learner then enters their own average and relative deviation and sees the notebook's beside them.

**The primer.** Twelve cards, written for a reader with no chemistry background: acids, bases and neutralisation; molarity; what a titration is; why sodium hydroxide has to be standardized; equivalence point and endpoint; why phenolphthalein; titrating to the endpoint; the calculation; reading the burette; conditioning, rinsing and the tip bubble; how closely the trials agree; safety. The first card names four to read before going to the bench. The cards are shown one at a time, with a list of the twelve and buttons to the one before and the one after, and those four are marked "Read first".
