So that a topic is quick to find inside a card, each paragraph has a short bold label at its head, each term is bold where it is defined, and each sentence that states a rule of the simulation and not of a real bench, and says so itself, is set off by a rule down its side. These were added on 2026-10-06 over the wording the author approved, which a test holds unchanged, byte for byte. Each card lists its sources, and the sources of the model's constants are under the card. The primer quotes none of its sources: beside each paragraph the source code stores where the passage it rests on is, and what that passage establishes, in Valence's own words.

**How a run is made.** Pressing Start draws a seed, which is shown in the notebook. The seed fixes the molarity on the acid bottle's label (0.0850 to 0.1050 M, to four decimal places, and exact), the molarity of the base, which the learner is not shown (0.09500 to 0.10500 M), and for each trial what the pipette delivers (25.00 mL give or take up to 0.03 mL; the learner calculates with 25.00) and where a filled burette's meniscus starts (between 0.00 and 2.00 mL). Nothing else varies. The same seed and the same actions always give the same readings and the same answer. Restart asks first and then draws a new seed.

**The bench.** The burette is graduated every 0.1 mL and is read from an enlarged view of the scale, with the eye level with the meniscus. The stopcock gives a half-drop (0.025 mL), a drop (0.05 mL), a stream (0.25 mL every 0.4 seconds) and two measured pours, of 1 mL and of 5 mL. It stays shut until the burette is filled, the flask holds the acid and 2 or 3 drops of indicator, and an initial reading is recorded. After each addition the bench says, in words as well as in the drawing, whether the pink where the drop landed fades at once, fades slowly or persists. The final reading can be recorded only after "Swirl and wait 30 s" has shown the pink to last; the 30 seconds are simulated and there is no real wait. The flask wall can be rinsed down with distilled water, 10 mL at a time and at most three times in a trial. A flask can be discarded and the trial begun again. The bench never states how much has been delivered. The description of the burette and flask gives the burette's level to the whole millilitre and no finer, and the status line and the message under the stand give no level of their own. The level is read in the enlarged view, and that view's text alternative, which every learner can open, gives the two 0.1 mL marks the meniscus lies between and which fifth of the gap it is in. The only readings printed as numbers are the ones the learner typed, and the titre worked from them. Additions are exact, though, so a learner who counts them can work out the titre without reading the burette; that is accepted.

**Two modes.** Guided shows a checklist of the steps. It warns before the burette is filled without conditioning, before an initial reading is taken with the bubble still in the tip (and of an unconditioned burette once more there), and before the acid is pipetted with an unrinsed pipette; the learner can go back or go on. It does not record a burette reading that is more than 0.02 mL from the meniscus, or an acid molarity that is not the label's, and asks for it again. Within 1.00 mL of the flask's equivalence point it shows a hint to slow down, and closes a running stream the first time the hint shows. Once both readings are in, it sets out the calculation with the learner's own numbers and stops before the answer. It never shows the expected titre, or a molarity the learner did not type. Challenge shows none of these: skipped steps, misread burettes and a miscopied label carry into the result and are named afterwards. The mode is chosen before Start and can be changed between trials, not during one. No trial is labelled with a mode.

**How answers are checked.**

- **The form of what is typed.** A burette reading is typed to exactly two decimal places and a molarity to exactly four. Anything else is refused, in both modes, and so is a comma as the decimal mark.
- **The arithmetic.** The base's molarity is accepted only when it is the learner's own recorded acid molarity × 25.00 mL ÷ their own titre, rounded to the fourth decimal place. At an exact tie, where the fifth decimal place is a 5 with nothing after it, either neighbour is accepted, because rounding a half up and rounding a half to even are both taught. A value cut off after the fourth place instead of rounded is refused, with a sentence that says to round. The check uses only numbers the learner typed, so it gives nothing away, and a refusal marks nothing.
- **The grade.** A trial passes when the molarity is within 1.0% of the base's true molarity, the edge included. A trial that does not pass shows its percent error, with its sign, and names what went wrong. A trial that passes shows no percent error.
- **Invalid trials.** A trial is invalid, and is repeated, when more than 0.10 mL of base (two drops) was added past the flask's own equivalence point; when base has run into the flask and the burette is at its 50.00 mL mark with the flask not pink; or when the learner discards the flask.
- **Three trials must count.** A trial that passes counts only when both typed burette readings were within 0.02 mL of the meniscus, the recorded acid molarity is the label's, and no technique error is named in it (burette not conditioned, tip bubble, pipette not rinsed).
- **The percent error.** The percent error of a failed trial lets a learner work out the base's true molarity. That is why a trial counts only on those three conditions. A result reached by typing a reading the burette does not show is marked "Passed, not counted", and so is one that is close only because two mistakes cancelled or because what was left of a bubble was small. Its feedback says why.
- **Completion.** When three trials count, the base's true molarity is shown, to five decimal places, and no further trial is run. The learner enters their own average and relative deviation over those three; the notebook then shows its own beside them. Neither is marked right or wrong.
- **Relative deviation** is the mean absolute deviation of the three molarities divided by their mean, in parts per thousand. The notebook works it from the average rounded to four decimal places and shows it to one decimal place. Molarities are typed to four decimal places, and one unit in the fourth place is about one part per thousand of the result, so the relative deviation of three careful trials can take only a few values and reflects rounding as much as technique.

**Technique errors are larger than at a bench, and named.** In the model, a burette filled without conditioning holds base diluted by 2.0%. An air bubble of 0.60 mL forms in the tip when the burette is filled from empty; left there, it escapes during the first 5 mL delivered, so the reading falls 0.60 mL more than went into the flask. The first aliquot drawn with a pipette still wet with water is diluted by 2.0%. Each size was chosen so that the error, on its own, fails a trial; the automated tests hold that for seeds 1 to 100. The sizes are parameters of the model, stored with the reason for each, and are not measurements. The feedback after a trial names each error, says which way it moves the result, and says that the lab makes it cost more than a real bench does. The primer says so too. No source was read for what these errors cost at a real bench.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately:

- A burette filled without conditioning stays 2.0% dilute through every refill, until it is conditioned. A real one is less diluted at each refill.
- The 0.60 mL bubble is more air than a real burette's tip holds, and it leaves evenly over the first 5 mL where a real one leaves at once. Both statements about a real burette are the project's own; no source was read for either.
- A wet pipette spoils only the first aliquot drawn with it, because drawing that aliquot wets the pipette with acid. A bubble that has escaped does not come back at a refill; conditioning empties the burette, so the fill after it traps a new one.
- The stopcock will not open until the flask holds 2 or 3 drops of indicator, and no indicator can be added once base has run in.
- The distilled water is free of carbon dioxide, and a flask takes at most 30 mL of it in a trial. A rinse is 10 mL, which can thin a very faint pink until it no longer shows; the bench and the primer say what to do then.
- The swirl and wait cannot fail: the model's flask is closed, so a pink that persists when it is added is never found faded 30 seconds later. The wait stands for the 30 seconds of a real bench, and the primer says so.
- Refilling the burette before each trial is taught and not forced. A trial run on what is left in the burette is an ordinary trial unless the burette runs out.
- A bubble run out into a flask that is then discarded, and a pipette wetted by an aliquot that is then discarded, leave the trials after them sound. So three trials can count although "Clear the tip" and "Rinse pipette with HCl" were never pressed. Conditioning cannot be got round in this way.

**What is not modelled.** Carbon dioxide taken up from the air during a trial (the acid in the flask starts with the carbon dioxide that water holds in equilibrium with outdoor air, the flask is then closed, and the base is free of carbonate); the slow fading of phenolphthalein's pink in strong base; activity corrections (the pH is that of an ideal solution, and no pH is shown to the learner); any temperature but 25 °C; the ethanol in the indicator solution; and parallax, which the primer explains.

**Conventions chosen.**

- The known solution is hydrochloric acid whose molarity is on its label and is taken as exact. The primer says that the acid was itself standardized.
- Molarities are written to four decimal places in every case. That is this lab's convention, not a significant-figure rule, and the primer says so.
- The endpoint is the first faint pink that lasts the 30-second swirl and wait.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The model's constants are stored with their sources, are listed on the Primer tab under the card and on the About page, and are never adjusted to make a test pass.

| Constant | Stored value | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| K_w, water's ion product, at 25 °C | 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ | IAPWS release R11-24 (2024). The release gives the constant with the ions in moles per kilogram of water; the stored note converts it to moles per litre with the density of water from the NIST Chemistry WebBook and rounds to two figures. |
| Phenolphthalein's two acid constants (pK) | 9.05 and 9.50 | Tamura and others, Analytical Sciences, 1996: measured in 0.2 M phosphate buffers at 25 ± 2 °C, and used unchanged. Only the form that has lost both protons is pink. |
| Phenolphthalein's molar mass | 318.3 g/mol | PubChem, compound 4764 |
| Strength of the indicator solution | 0.5 g per 100 mL | A supplier's catalogue listing for a 0.5% (w/v) solution in 50% (v/v) alcohol, read on 2026-10-04 and not kept. The listing prints the basis, weight per volume. What is assumed is that this lab's bottle is that kind of solution. The endpoint test passes for any strength from 0.1% to 1%. |
| Volume of a drop of indicator | 0.05 mL | The metric drop, 20 to the millilitre, as Wikipedia's page on the unit gives it. The drop of an alcohol and water solution was not measured. |
| Henry's constant for carbon dioxide in water at 25 °C | 0.034 mol/(kg·bar) | NIST Chemistry WebBook |
| Carbon dioxide in outdoor air | 425.62 ppm, the global mean for 2025 | NOAA Global Monitoring Laboratory |
| Carbonic acid's two acid constants (pK) | 6.35 and 10.33 | A handbook (Perrin, 1982) that was not read: the figures were taken from Wikipedia's page on carbonic acid, which cites it. Reading the first as the constant of dissolved carbon dioxide is the project's own reading. The database of the U.S. Geological Survey's PHREEQC program gives 6.352 and 10.329 at 25 °C, which bears both out. |

One number of the model is tuned and has no source: the concentration of the pink form at which the flask counts as faintly pink (1 × 10⁻⁶ mol/L), set so that a careful titration first shows a lasting pink within one drop past the equivalence point. The seeded ranges, the sizes of the additions, the sizes of the technique errors and the tolerances (0.02 mL for a reading, 1.0% for the grade, 0.10 mL for an overshoot) are the lab's own choices, each stored with its reason. How pink the flask is drawn, how fast the stream runs and how long a plume takes to fade are presentation, chosen by eye; no endpoint and no grade depends on them. The primer's statements rest on OpenStax Chemistry 2e, David Harvey's Analytical Chemistry 2.1, and pages on burette and pipette technique; each card lists what it rests on.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The pH solver against exact solutions; the titration curve against the closed-form answer; the endpoint for seeds 1 to 100 and at every corner of the seeded ranges; grading at the edge of 1.0%; the typed molarity over every label and titre; which trials count; each technique error; the water rinses; how a trial can end; the two modes; and that a lab reloaded after every action ends in the same state as one never reloaded.
- **By review before any screen was built.** Three AI reviewers that had not written the code, between them, checked every stored constant against its source, fetched afresh; re-derived the model with an implementation of their own; wrote down which way each technique error moves the result before reading the lab's answer; read every sentence a learner sees that existed then; and tried to complete a lab without three honest titrations. Later checks of the same kind followed the changes made after that review. The findings and what was done about each are in the research record, `docs/research/phase-3/gate-a.md`.
- **By the project's author.** The author read and approved the primer and the hazard notes as they stood on 2026-10-05, before the hazard statements were moved to a supplier's safety data sheets. The author asked for that move, and for the safety card's sentence on how long to rinse.

**Known limits of the titration lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the primer, the messages, the model or the hazard text.
- Eight points of the primer rest on no source that was read, and nobody has judged them one by one: that the faint pink of a real endpoint fades because the open flask takes up carbon dioxide, and that this is why the rule gives a time; that a sodium hydroxide solution which has stood is standardized again; touching a hanging part-drop to the flask wall; the definition of relative deviation; which way an escaping tip bubble moves the reading; the statements about a real bench given above; that a 10 mL rinse can thin a very faint pink until it no longer shows; and that a single drop's pink clears at once again after the stream's has begun to linger.
- Sentences written after the reviews have been read by no independent reader: those written to fix the last review's findings, and the labels, headings and button names written into the lab's screens. That includes everything written on 2026-10-06 when the page was put into tabs: the forty labels at the head of the primer's paragraphs, the choice of which terms are bold and which sentences are set off, the names of the tabs and of the primer's buttons, and the sentences shown where there is no run or where a stopcock was closed on leaving the Lab tab.
- The acceptance tests run seeds 1 to 100, and the reviewers' attacks ran seeds 1 to 400. Other seeds were not searched.
- The test for pink uses floating-point arithmetic, and only one JavaScript engine has run it. Everything else that decides a reading, an overshoot or a grade is whole-number arithmetic.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The aqueous reactions lab

**What it teaches.** Ten fixed stations, each a test tube in which two reagents are mixed. The learner mixes the tube, observes it (by eye at every station, and at three stations with tools: wafting and red litmus paper, touch and a thermometer, a lit splint), records what was observed from fixed choices, and writes the reaction three ways: as a molecular equation, a complete ionic equation and a net ionic equation. Five stations give a precipitate, one gives no reaction, and the other four give ammonia, carbon dioxide, heat alone, and hydrogen with heat. The amounts are fixed; the learner does not choose them.

**The primer.** Eleven cards for a reader with no chemistry background, shown one at a time: ions in solution; solubility and the solubility table; precipitates; the three forms of an equation; working a station, step by step; spectator ions; gases and heat as signs of a reaction; when nothing happens; the limiting reagent; observing safely; safety. Five are marked to read first. The worked example of the equations card is a reaction that is at none of the stations, and so is the one that the card of worked steps takes from two bottles to three equations in eight labelled steps; automated tests hold every equation on both cards to balance in atoms and in charge. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A station.** The tube's card shows the two reagents with what each looks like in its bottle, their amounts and their hazards, and after "Mix" a large drawing of the tube to explore: pointing at, focusing or tapping the surface of the liquid, the liquid or the bottom of the tube says in words what is seen there, and each of the three questions about what is seen opens once its part has been looked at. The tools each say what they show when used; touching the tube of station 8 says that it is hot and gives a caution, the thermometer there gives the rise and says that the tube is hot, and asking to smell a tube directly is refused with a caution on why. The observation is one choice for each thing the station's tools can show; its first question asks what solid is in the tube once any reaction is over, so that where a powder or a ribbon dissolves the answer is plainly "no solid". A station's notes, which say where the lab's account is simpler than a real tube and at three stations name the equation that is checked, are shown once the station is done and not before. Each equation is built from a palette of formulas offered for that station, which includes some that do not belong: a formula is chosen, placed as a reactant or a product, and given a coefficient and a state; "No reaction" can be chosen in its place. Nothing is typed. A solubility table, a table of the ions of the bench with their names and charges, and a calculator can be opened over the page.

**Two modes.** Guided shows each station's hints from the start, and offers what a correct observation holds in a list that is closed until the learner opens it. Challenge shows neither at a station until the learner has recorded an observation of their own there, right or not. The mode can be changed at any time, and no entry is labelled with a mode.

**How answers are checked.**

- An observation is correct when every choice is the one the lab's record expects for that station. The feedback names the questions to look at again and not the answers.
- An equation is correct when the substances on each side are those of the equation stored for that station and form, each state is one the substance accepts (ammonia may be a gas or dissolved), and the coefficients are in the stored proportion, so a doubled equation passes. The feedback says what kind of thing is wrong, the first of these that applies: an equation where nothing reacts, or "No reaction" where something does; an ion in a molecular equation; a dissolved compound not split into its ions in an ionic equation; a spectator ion left in a net ionic equation; the substances or their sides; a state; the balance. It never shows the stored equation.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. A station is done when its observation and its three equations are correct, and the lab is complete when all ten are. The Record tab lists each entry as it was last checked and whether it is correct.

**The model.** For each station the lab works out, from the fixed amounts, how much of each ion and solid is in the tube, in whole billionths of a mole, and applies five rules in one pass: an insoluble pair of ions forms a solid; a hydrogen ion with carbonate gives carbon dioxide; ammonium with hydroxide gives ammonia; a hydrogen ion with hydroxide gives water and heat; and magnesium with a hydrogen ion gives hydrogen and heat. It respects the limiting reagent and leaves the coloured ions that are not used up in the liquid. Automated tests hold that its result at every station agrees with the record a correct observation is checked against, that every stored equation balances in atoms and in charge, and that no net ionic equation has a substance unchanged on both sides.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately:

- Every reaction goes to completion at once, and a precipitate takes every ion it can: the liquid over the blue-green and the pale green solids of stations 2 and 3 is colourless.
- At stations 2 and 3 the equation that is checked is the simple carbonate's. The solid that really forms is a basic carbonate; the station's note says so once the station is done.
- The temperature rise (about 40 °C at station 8, about 11 °C at station 10) is worked out as if no heat left the tube and the liquid were water. The thermometer's sentence and the primer say that it is idealised and that a real tube warms less. Heat is worked out for those two rules alone; the other three are given none.
- Ammonia is not shown bubbling out at station 6, where most of it stays dissolved.
- "Warm" and "hot" are the lab's own thresholds, a rise of 5 °C and of 25 °C, stored with the reason for each.
- The solubility table holds only the 23 pairs of ions that the ten stations can bring together. It says of any other pair that it is not listed. It lists ammonium with hydroxide as soluble, as its source's row for ammonium does; in the lab those two ions react by another rule, and that one cell is marked with a footnote's sign, with a line under the table that says the two make no solid but do react, giving ammonia, and names the primer's card on gases.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The solubility of each pair, the forms of an equation and the place of magnesium above hydrogen are read from OpenStax Chemistry 2e; the enthalpies of formation from the NBS tables of 1982, read as a scan by eye; the colours, textures and odours from a set of pages on the reactions of metal ions, the same book, one page of Chemguide, a university's instruction sheet and four Wikipedia pages; the molar masses from PubChem; and the hazards from a supplier's safety data sheets (see Lab safety). Each station's Sources control lists what its values rest on. The research record, `docs/research/phase-4/sources/README.md`, says how each kind of value was read and what could not be sourced: among these, the hex value each colour is drawn in, the name "orange" for the mixed liquid of station 5, and the colours of the fresh precipitates at stations 2 and 3, which are read from descriptions of the dried solids.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 8.4): each station's result against its expected record; the limiting reagent at stations 1, 7, 9 and 10; the two temperature rises; every stored equation's balance; the equation check with doubled coefficients and with a substance that does not belong; and the description of what is seen at every station. Also: that every state a learner can reach in 60 scripted random walks leaves the lab able to be completed and passes the stored format; that every equation of every station can be built with the page's builder; and the page's markup and wording.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The aqueous reactions lab: accessibility" above.
- **By one reviewer that did not build it, which was not a person.** The lab was written and checked by AI coding assistants. On 2026-10-07, at the close of the phase, another AI coding assistant that had not built the lab reviewed its screens, its wording, its primer and its code, and ran it in a browser as described under accessibility, above. It changed twelve things, among them: a station's notes were shown as soon as the observation was correct, and three of them give an equation that was still to be entered, the control station's outright, so they are now shown when the station is done; the primer's card on the case where nothing reacts said that with no acid there is no gas, which is not so at station 6; the primer described the solubility table as a list when the page draws a grid; the question about a solid could be read as asking what went in; an open panel was left outside a window made smaller; and a completed lab said the same sentence four times on a station. The record of the build lists every finding, what was done, and what was left. No person has reviewed the lab: no chemistry instructor, no chemist, and no reader of any kind from outside the project.

**Known limits of the aqueous reactions lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the primer, the stations' hints and notes, the feedback, the model or the hazard text.
- The record of sources says what each page was read to print. Only one of those pages is kept, so the build cannot check the rest again.
- The primer's first card says what an acid and a base are, from the passage of OpenStax Chemistry 2e that the titration lab's primer has them from. That a solution of a base is called basic it says with no source read for it.
- The primer's card of worked steps, the two paragraphs added to its first card and the table of the bench's ions were written on 2026-10-07 by an AI coding assistant, which read the book's sections for them again. Nobody else has read them.
- At stations 2 and 3 the palette offers the metal's hydroxide as a formula that does not belong, and the solubility table has no row for that pair, so the table cannot rule it out; the station's own note, shown when the station is done, says that the solid that really forms does hold hydroxide.
- The palette at each station holds a few formulas that do not belong; how good they are as wrong answers has been judged by nobody.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The flame test lab

**What it teaches.** Six stations, one for each of six 0.1 M solutions, each the chloride of a different metal: barium, calcium, copper, potassium, sodium and strontium. At a station the learner cleans a wire loop, dips it in the solution, holds it in a burner's flame, looks at the flame, looks at the flame's light through a hand spectroscope with a scale from 400 to 780 nm, and records three things from fixed choices: the colour of the flame, where on the scale the brightest thing is (one of six ranges), and whether that thing is a sharp line or a broad band. There is no unknown to identify. When the six are done, one closing question asks what it is that gives each flame its colour, all six bottles being chlorides. The point the lab is built to make is that a flame's colour is not simply the colour of the atom's strongest lines: for calcium, strontium, barium and copper most of the light is a molecule's, formed in the flame, and shows as bands.

**The primer.** Six cards, shown one at a time: what a flame test is; light from atoms; molecules in a flame; the loop; the spectroscope and its scale; safety. Three are marked to read first. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**The technique that is checked is a clean loop.** A loop carries what it was dipped in until it is cleaned, and the loop the lab starts with has been handled and carries a trace of sodium. Whatever is on the loop shows in the flame and on the strip. An observation made with a loop that carried anything but the station's own solution does not count, whatever was recorded: the feedback says so and says what the loop carried, and the station is done again. In Guided a warning comes before such a loop is dipped, with a way back and a way on.

**Two modes.** Guided has hints at each station and what a correct observation holds, each in a list that is closed until the learner opens it, and gives the warning. Challenge gives no warning, and shows neither the hints nor the expected observation at a station until the learner has recorded an observation there that counts. The mode can be changed at any time.

**How answers are checked.**

- An observation that counts is correct when its three choices are the ones the lab's record gives for that salt: the flame's stored colour, the range that the salt's highest-ranked line or band lies in, and which of the two that is. The feedback names the questions to look at again and not the answers.
- The closing question has one right choice of five. A wrong one is told why it cannot be the answer, from what was seen at the bench, and not what the answer is.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. A station is done when its observation was made with a clean loop and is correct, and the lab is complete when all six are done and the closing question is answered rightly. The Record tab lists what was last recorded at each station and whether it counted and is correct.

**Which line or band is "the brightest" follows what instruments recorded, not the eye.** No table of measured intensities of these flames was found. Each salt's lines and bands are ranked from what its sources say or draw: for calcium and strontium, the taller peak in emission spectra drawn in a paper of 1975, which are not corrected for the instrument or for the eye; for barium, what two sources say of its green band; for copper, what a dissertation's reprinted paper says of firework mixtures that hold a source of chlorine; for sodium and potassium, the relative intensity that the NIST database lists, which NIST calls qualitative. An eye at a real hand spectroscope may well disagree: it is far more sensitive to orange than to deep red, so it may find strontium's orange band the brighter where the lab says the red, and potassium's deep red pair near 766 nm, which the lab ranks first, lies at the edge of what an eye sees at all. The primer's card on the spectroscope says that brightness here is the instruments', and each line and band says in words how bright it is beside the others, so no answer depends on judging brightness from the screen.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately and stored with its reason:

- The trace of sodium on a handled loop shows as fully as a dip in the sodium chloride solution. A real trace is far smaller and often gives a brief, faint yellow.
- The loop is cleaned in one step, and a loop that is not clean does not spoil the solution it is dipped in.
- With sodium on the loop the flame is sodium's yellow alone, which has a source. With several salts and no sodium the flame is called by the colour of each and drawn in each side by side, and on the strip every salt's lines and bands are shown as that salt alone gives them: the model does not weigh one salt's light against another's.
- Two lines of one salt closer than 5 nm are shown as one line, as a hand spectroscope would show them. The 5 nm is the lab's figure, not a measurement of any instrument.
- A band whose sources give only where it is brightest is drawn 5 nm wider on each side, and says that its limits are not given.
- The strip shows the lines and bands that the sources place on the scale, and no others. Where a source names more light and gives no place for it (the bands of SrCl and of copper oxide), or where bands are spread over most of the scale or lie inside another (BaO and BaCl), the station says so in a sentence once it is done.
- The colours on the screen are approximate. The flame is drawn in a colour chosen by the project for the colour its source names. A line or a band is drawn in an approximation of the colour of its wavelength, and beyond 740 nm in the colour of 740 nm, so that potassium's deep red line can be found on a screen. A violet, a blue or a deep red that would be too dim against the strip's black is drawn paler, so that it can be found. How light a line or a band looks on a screen follows its colour as well as its rank: calcium's green band, ranked second, is lighter on a screen than its orange-red band, ranked first. Which is the brightest is in the words of each callout.
- A sharp line is drawn the strip's whole height and a broad band a little shorter, so that the two are told apart. That is the picture's way and not a spectroscope's, and the primer says so.
- The burner is no particular burner. The sources' flames were of hydrogen and oxygen in argon, of air and acetylene, of a gas burner in 1860, and of burning firework mixtures.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The wavelengths of the atomic lines are the digits of the NIST Atomic Spectra Database, from tables saved in the repository; the build holds every stored line to the table of its own metal. The bands of CaOH, SrOH and BaOH are from a paper of 1975, read as a scanned copy in a university's repository, pages and figures by eye, and from the abstracts in English of two papers of 1979 whose text was not read; those of CuCl and CuOH from a doctoral dissertation on firework flames. The colours of the flames are from a page of Chemguide for five of the six and from an open textbook on qualitative analysis for sodium's, with that textbook and a paper as second sources. That a handled loop carries sodium, that sodium's yellow hides other colours, and the answer to the closing question rest on a university's page, a college's pre-lab page and the paper of Kirchhoff and Bunsen of 1860, read in an abridged translation. Each station's Sources control lists what its values rest on, once the station is done. The research record, `docs/research/flame/README.md`, says how each kind of value was read, what could not be sourced, and what a chemist should look at first: the ranks; copper, where a green band may in truth outshine the blue; barium, whose broad green band is given to BaOH though the solution is a chloride; and ranges taken from abstracts that may be the windows of a measurement and not the edges of a band.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 15.5): that a clean loop gives each salt its stored flame and its stored lines and bands and nothing else; that a loop that was not cleaned gives those of everything on it, with sodium's yellow where sodium is on it, that an observation made so does not count and names its cause, and that Guided warns before the dip; that every stored line or band has a source and every atomic line the saved table's digits; that from four hundred actions taken as they come the lab can still be completed, and the stored record survives a round trip; that Challenge is handed nothing of what is expected before an observation that counts; and the descriptions in words of the flame and of the strip for every salt. Also the page's markup and wording, and where each line's and band's button lies.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The flame test lab: accessibility" above.
- **By a review, on 2026-10-09,** of the whole lab by an AI coding assistant that had not built it: in a browser as said above, and by reading every sentence a learner is shown against the lab's record and the research record's account of its sources. It rewrote what the lab said of the periodic table's cards (next paragraph) and five sentences of the primer.
- **By nobody else.** The record, the engine, the primer, the wording and the screens were written, checked and reviewed by AI coding assistants.

**What the lab says of the periodic table's cards.** As first written, the notes of the calcium, strontium, barium and copper stations, the primer and the closing question's feedback said that those flames are not the colour of the lines on the element's card. The cards do not bear that out: six of the nine lines on calcium's card are orange or red and its flame is orange-red, and three of the six on strontium's are red and its flame is red. Since the review each says only that the card shows lines of the atom alone, and that what colours the flame is mostly the molecule's light, which the card does not show.

**Known limits of the flame test lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the record, the ranks, the notes, the primer, the feedback, the closing question or the hazard text. The scanned paper was read by one reader, by eye.
- Every source but one saved table was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again. It holds the record to the research record's account of what each page prints.
- The lab's ranking can differ from what a learner sees at a real spectroscope, as said above; a learner who has done the real experiment may have been taught another answer for strontium or potassium.
- The hints are the same five at every station, behind a list that is closed until it is opened.
- **The primer gives one of the three answers.** In both modes its cards say which two of the six flames show lines and which four are mostly bands, and that sodium's flame is yellow. A learner who has read them has, before the bench, the answer to "a sharp line or a broad band" at every station and one of the six colours. That is the design's choice, and it is why the search for what Challenge keeps back left the primer out. A station that is done also lists, among its sources, a paper whose title holds the formulas of two of the molecules.
- The answer to the closing question is the metal, and a learner who chooses the chloride is told that what is the same in all six bottles cannot be what makes the flames differ. At the copper station the brightest band is given to a molecule that holds chlorine; the lab does not say that the chloride takes part in that flame's light.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The calorimetry lab

**What it teaches.** How heat is measured with a thermometer and a cup: a foam-cup calorimeter, drawn, with a lid, a stirrer and a temperature probe whose logger takes a reading every 15 seconds and draws them against time. There are three parts, each with its own run of the cup and its own worksheet. In part 1, 50.0 mL of 1.00 M hydrochloric acid and 50.0 mL of 1.00 M sodium hydroxide solution are mixed, and the learner finds the heat given out for each mole of water formed. In part 2 a weighed portion of solid ammonium nitrate, about 4 g, dissolves in 50.0 mL of water, and the learner finds the heat taken in for each mole dissolved. In part 3 a weighed piece of an unknown grey metal, about 50 g, is heated in boiling water whose temperature is read and put into 50.0 mL of water in the cup; the learner finds the metal's specific heat and, from a table of four, which metal it is. The starting temperatures, the masses and the temperature of the boiling water differ from run to run, and the metal from lab to lab.

**The primer.** Eight cards, shown one at a time: temperature and heat; giving out heat and taking it in; specific heat; the calorimeter and what it assumes; reading the graph; from heat to heat for each mole; a metal's specific heat; safety. Four are marked to read first. Each kind of calculation is worked once, on numbers that no run of the lab can have. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A run.** The learner puts the lid on, starts the stirrer and the logger, starts the change (pours in the second solution, adds the solid, adds the hot metal) and lets time pass, 30 seconds at a press; no clock runs. The graph grows by two readings at each press. Its curve rises or falls to a highest or lowest reading and then drifts slowly back toward the room's temperature. Once it has turned back, the worksheet opens. Its first two entries, the starting temperature and the highest or lowest, each wait until the learner has looked at a reading of the graph that shows it: a reading says its time and its temperature when it is looked at, and nothing beside the graph lists them.

**The technique that is checked is the lid and the stirrer.** A run counts only if the lid was on and the stirrer going from the moment the change was started. Without the lid the cup loses heat to the room faster, and without stirring the change is slow and the cup trades heat with the room all the while; either puts the result outside what the lab accepts, whatever the arithmetic. In Guided a warning comes before the change is started with either skipped, with a way back and a way on. The lid cannot be taken off again, nor the stirrer stopped.

**Two modes.** Guided gives the warning, checks each entry of the worksheet as it is made, refuses one that does not follow, and has the relation each entry uses ready in a disclosure that is closed until the learner opens it. Challenge gives no warning and no relation, takes whatever is typed, and checks the worksheet all together when the learner asks; that check is final for the run. The sentence under a part's title that says what the part finds is the same in both modes and does not say which way the heat goes, which is an entry of the worksheets of parts 1 and 2: "the heat for each mole of water formed when an acid and a base are mixed", "the heat for each mole of ammonium nitrate that dissolves in water". (As first built those two sentences said "given out" and "taken in" and were kept from Challenge until the part was done; changed at the review of 2026-10-09.) The mode can be changed at any time.

**How entries are checked.**

- A temperature is typed to 0.1 °C and must be the reading the logger shows, to the tenth of a degree: the level reading before the change, and the reading furthest from it afterwards. A look at a reading says it to 0.1 °C, so nothing is estimated. In Guided a temperature a tenth of a degree off is refused at once; in Challenge it is taken, as everything is, and marked when the worksheet is checked. (Until 2026-10-10 a tenth either side was taken. Over seeds 1 to 100, with the cup worked carefully and every entry then worked correctly from the temperatures typed, one temperature a tenth off left 2 runs of part 3 in 100 outside the 5%, and both a tenth off, toward each other, left 68 runs of part 2 and 42 of part 3 outside it, each with every entry told in Guided that it follows. The project's owner decided that a lab which ticks every entry and then fails the run is worse than one that refuses a neighbouring reading at once.)
- Every other number is typed to three significant figures and must follow from the learner's own entries above it and from what the bench gives (the volumes, the balance's reading, the molar mass, the specific heat of water, the temperature of the boiling water). It must be a correct rounding, with either neighbour taken at an exact tie; the entries above may be used as they were typed, rounded, or as the calculator had them before rounding. So a learner whose temperatures are a tenth of a degree off is not told that every later entry is wrong.
- Two entries are choices: whether the reaction gave heat out or took it in, in parts 1 and 2, and which of the four metals has the specific heat nearest the learner's own, in part 3.
- Where an entry that does not follow looks like one of five slips, the feedback names the slip: a number cut off where it should be rounded, a wrong sign, the salt left out of the mass that cooled, the water's change in temperature used for the metal's, and joules given for kilojoules. Feedback never gives the value.
- A run counts when its two temperatures are the logger's, every entry follows, the lid was on and the cup stirred, and its result is within 5% of the figure the lab holds: the stored heat for each mole in parts 1 and 2, and in part 3 the specific heat of the metal the piece is, with that metal chosen. A run that does not count says why, and the part is run again, with new numbers. How far off a result was is not said until a run of the part counts. With the lid on, the cup stirred and every entry following, a run does count: the automated tests below hold that, at seeds 1 to 100, for every way the rounding rule lets the entries be worked.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. One run that counts completes a part, and the lab is complete when all three are. The Record tab lists every run of each part, with what was entered and whether it counted.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately. The model's own values are stored with their reasons, and the primer says what the lab takes as simpler than it is.

- Every solution is given water's specific heat, 4.18 J/(g·°C), and each millilitre of it is taken to weigh 1.00 g. The model and the learner's arithmetic make the same assumption, so a run is judged fairly; the temperatures drawn are a little off a real cup's.
- The cup, the lid, the stirrer and the probe take up no heat. A careful bench measures how much they take; the primer says so, and the lab does not ask for it.
- The hot piece of metal reaches the cup at once, as hot as the boiling water, and dry.
- How fast the cup's contents come to their new temperature, and how fast the cup trades heat with the room, are the model's own figures and not measurements of any cup. They were chosen so that a careful run is within the 5% at every seed tried and each technique error alone is outside it; the two for a careless cup are larger than a real cup's are likely to be. The record says of a careful run that it reads between 0.4% and 3.1% low.
- The learner takes the highest or the lowest reading as the logger shows it. The lab does not ask for the cooling line to be carried back to the moment of mixing, which a careful bench does; the primer says that too.
- The piece is one of four metals: aluminium, iron, zinc or tin.
- Part 1 has the same volumes and strengths at every run, so its change in temperature is nearly the same every time; only its starting temperature differs.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The two heats for each mole are worked out by the build from enthalpies of formation in the NBS tables of chemical thermodynamic properties of 1982, read as a scan, pages by eye. They use the tables' rows for solutions of about the strength on the bench, and so the lab holds −57.7 kJ for each mole of water formed and +24.4 kJ for each mole of ammonium nitrate dissolved, where the same tables give −55.8 and +25.7 for solutions so dilute that more water would change nothing; each part's notes, shown once the part is done, give both. The four metals' specific heats are the tables' heat capacities for a mole divided by the standard atomic weights of the IUPAC commission that publishes them. The molar mass of ammonium nitrate is PubChem's, and the specific heat and density of water are from one page of the NIST Chemistry WebBook. The primer rests on fourteen pages: of two universities and of a college, of a university's laboratory manual, of Chemguide, of NASA and of a set of revision notes. Each part's Sources control lists what its values rest on. The research record, `docs/research/calorimetry/README.md`, says how each kind of value was read, what could not be sourced, and what a chemist should look at, among them: that the tables' row for the nitrate ion does not agree with their rows for the dissolved acid and salt, and the lab uses the rows for the salt; that the stored heats are for the strength on the bench and not for the standard state, 3% and 5% away, which is as large as the lab's tolerance; that the solutions are given water's specific heat and density; that a metal's specific heat at 25 °C is used from near 100 °C down; and that the piece of tin is taken to be white tin.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 16.6): that for seeds 1 to 100 a careful run of each part counts and is within the tolerance of the stored value, and that part 3's picks the right metal out of the four; that each technique error alone makes a run not count at every one of those seeds, and that its cause is named; that an entry that does not follow from the learner's own numbers is refused in Guided and marked in Challenge, a result with the wrong sign and one cut off where it should be rounded among them; that the same seed and the same actions give the same run; that the stored record survives a round trip; that from four hundred actions taken as they come the lab can still be completed; that Challenge is handed nothing of what is expected before a run counts; and the descriptions in words of the cup and of the graph. And, since 2026-10-10: that for those seeds a temperature a tenth of a degree off the logger's is refused in Guided, and that from a careful cup every chain of entries the rounding rule allows (each worked from the entries above as typed or as they stood unrounded, with either neighbour at a tie) gives a run that counts, the least favourable 1.57% low in part 1, 2.89% in part 2 and 3.08% in part 3. A search made once and not kept, over every starting temperature, mass and bath temperature the draw can give and each of the four metals, found none that does not count, the least favourable 3.52% low, for a tin piece. Also every sentence the lab can say, the primer's examples against their own arithmetic, the page's markup, and where each reading's point and button lie on the graph.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The calorimetry lab: accessibility" above.
- **By a review, on 2026-10-09,** of the whole lab, by an AI coding assistant that had built none of it, working by script in headless Chromium and by reading. Besides the browser checks listed above it read every sentence of the lab's own that a learner can meet (the primer, the page's wording, the record's notes and the notes under the tiles); worked the primer's five examples and its case of rounding again by hand; worked three seeded runs again by hand from the model's own relations and from what the page showed, and the arithmetic of the record's stored figures; ran the model over seeds 1 to 100 to hold the documents' figures to it (a careful run 1.6% low in part 1, 0.8% to 2.9% in part 2 and 0.4% to 3.1% in part 3; the lid off 8.2% to 11.6% low and the stirrer still 9.4% to 13.2%; the slowest curve turned back 8.5 minutes after the change); and asked six of the pages the lab cites what they say of the claims that rest on them, through a tool that fetches a page and answers questions about it: the NIST page for water and five of the fourteen pages the primer and the notes rest on (two of Chemguide's, the page of revision notes, one of the college's and the laboratory manual's). Each bore out what the lab's text rests on it for. It did not read the scanned tables, the other nine of the fourteen pages, or any safety data sheet, and it read the six through that tool's answers and not as pages. What it found and changed is the last section of `docs/research/calorimetry/README.md`.
- **By nobody else.** The record, the engine, the primer, the wording and the screens were written, checked and reviewed by AI coding assistants. No person has run the lab or read its text.

**Known limits of the calorimetry lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the record, the stored figures, the notes, the primer, the feedback or the hazard text. The scanned tables were read by one reader, by eye.
- **It was not built from any course's manual.** Its three parts are the usual ones of a first-year foam-cup calorimetry lab and were chosen by the project. A course's own lab may use other amounts, another salt or another set of metals.
- **The figure a result is held to is the one for the strength on the bench.** The lab holds −57.7 and +24.4 kJ/mol, and the same tables' figures for endlessly dilute solutions are −55.8 and +25.7. A learner who was taught another figure than the lab's will find a careful result set against the lab's. Whether the lab should hold the standard-state figures is a question for a chemist, and the research record says what changing them would take: two stored numbers and two notes.
- Every source was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again. It holds the record to the research record's account of what each page prints, the molar masses to the copies other parts of the project keep, and the worked-out values to the cited ones.
- **Which way the heat goes is not kept from a learner who reads the primer.** Its card on giving out heat and taking it in is about that, and its safety card says that the cup of part 2 gets colder than the room. The graph shows it too, which is the lab's intended way to it. The Lab tab itself does not say it in either mode before the entry that asks it is made: the sentence under a part's title was reworded at the review so that it does not.
- **A careful run is already up to 3.5% low, and the lab accepts 5%.** That is the lab's own model: the heat the cup loses before its peak, and the logger showing a temperature to 0.1 °C. The typed temperatures are held to the logger's exactly so that nothing is added to it; the 5% was not widened.
- **The graph described in words gives the two temperatures.** It is in the page for a learner who cannot read the picture, behind a disclosure that is closed, in both modes, and it says the starting temperature and the highest or lowest outright. The two entries still wait for a look at the graph.
- The metal is the same for a whole lab: a part 3 that is run again has a new mass and new temperatures and the same metal. "Restart lab" draws a new one, and may draw the same.
- The file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the lab's seed, from which which metal the piece is follows for anyone who reads the source code.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The Group I cations lab

**What it teaches.** Qualitative analysis: finding which ions a solution holds by what they do, and not how much of each there is. The three ions are silver, lead and mercury(I), the ones whose chlorides do not dissolve in cold dilute acid. The scheme has five steps, each worked on a tube the step before it left. Hydrochloric acid brings the three down together as a white solid. Hot water takes the lead chloride out of that solid. Acetic acid and potassium chromate give a yellow solid in the water if lead is there. Ammonia dissolves the silver chloride and turns mercury(I) chloride grey to black. Nitric acid, added until litmus paper shows the liquid acid, brings silver chloride back. There are two parts: a known that holds all three ions, so that each result is seen once, and an unknown that holds one, two or all three, which the learner works through the same steps and then names. The seven possible unknowns come in an order the lab's seed fixes, one for each run of part 2, and then round again.

**The primer.** Ten cards, shown one at a time: what qualitative analysis is; solids from solutions, and what a solubility product says; the three chlorides; parting ions by a difference; complex ions; mercury(I) and ammonia; confirming tests, with a yellow liquid against a yellow solid; centrifuging and pouring off; reading the flow chart; safety. Four are marked to read first. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A step.** The learner adds the step's reagent, in an amount the bench fixes, heats the tube at step 2, tests with litmus paper at step 5, and may centrifuge wherever a solid is spread through the liquid. The tube is drawn, and what it shows is found by looking at it: a pointer over the liquid or the bottom of the tube, keyboard focus on it or a tap says what is seen there, and never what it is. Both looks open two questions, the colour of the solid or that there is none, and the colour of the liquid. In part 1 the learner then fills the step's places on a flow chart of the scheme from nine tiles: which of the three ions is in the solid, which in the liquid, and as what. The step is checked, and at steps 1, 2 and 4 the liquid is poured off, away or into the next tube. A step's notes and its net ionic equations, which say what each solid and each dissolved thing is, are shown once the step is done and not before.

**The technique that is checked** is three slips, each real at a bench. Pouring off before the solid has been centrifuged: the solid goes over with the liquid. Pouring off the water of step 2 without heating it: part of any lead chloride stays with the solid and is still there, white, after the ammonia. Recording step 5 before litmus paper has shown the liquid acid: while ammonia is left no solid can form there, whether silver is in the tube or not. A run in which any of them is made does not count. In Guided a warning comes before each, with a way back and a way on.

**Two modes.** Guided gives the warnings, takes only an observation that is what the tube shows and only a tile that belongs in its place, says how many tiles belong in a place, checks a step by itself when there is nothing left to record in it, and has a hint for each step in a disclosure that is closed. Challenge gives no warning, no hint and no count, takes whatever is recorded or placed, and checks a step when the learner asks; a step that has been checked takes no change. What a step asks the learner to do is said in both modes, and names no colour and nothing of what will be seen. The mode can be changed at any time.

**How entries are checked.**

- What is recorded at a step is held to what the step's tube showed when it was recorded: both answers must be the tube's. Feedback says which part of the tube to look at again and never what it should have been.
- In part 1, each place of the flow chart must hold the tiles that belong in it, all of them and no other. A place that has been checked says whether it does. A run of part 1 counts only with every place right, since the finished chart is the map part 2 is given.
- In part 2, the ions named must be the ones the sample holds, all of them and no other. The naming is said once for each run, and a naming of none of the three is refused and asked again, since no sample holds none. Once it is said, the page says what the sample held.
- A run counts when no slip of technique was made in it, every observation is what its tube showed, and the chart is right or the ions named are the sample's. A run that does not count says why, with each reason, and the part is run again: with a new unknown in part 2.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. One run that counts completes a part, and the lab is complete when both are. Part 2 can still be run after that, for practice on another unknown. The Record tab lists every run of each part, with what was recorded at each step and whether the run counted.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately. The model's own values are stored with their reasons, and the primer and the notes say what the lab takes as simpler than it is.

- The amounts are fixed by the bench: a count of drops of each reagent, and the same amount of each ion in every sample. The learner chooses what to do and when, and not how much.
- Each chloride comes down whole and at once at step 1. On a real bench lead chloride is slow to form and some lead stays dissolved, and far too much acid begins to dissolve the chlorides again; the note of step 1 says so.
- A pouring off takes all the liquid and leaves none with the solid. A careful bench washes each solid, and washes the solid of step 2 with hot water two or three times more; the note of step 2 says so.
- The water from the bath is taken as 100 °C, and the second tube is taken to stay hot until step 3. In a real tube lead chloride can come back out of cooling water; the note of step 3 says so.
- Step 5 is all or nothing: while ammonia is left no silver chloride comes back, and once the acid is past it all of it does. In a real tube the solid begins to come back as the ammonia runs out.
- A slip of technique makes a run not count wherever it is made, also where the sample holds no ion that the slip would change the look of. A warning or a verdict that turned on what the sample holds would say what an unknown holds.
- A solid carried into the wrong tube by a pouring off without the centrifuge stays there as it is, whatever is added after it, and of two solids of different colours in one tube one colour is seen: white is hidden by yellow, and both by grey to black. No source was read for either; both matter only after a slip.
- The samples are plain solutions. A real bench makes each nitrate solution up in dilute nitric acid to keep it clear.
- Every liquid but one with chromate in it is drawn as colourless.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The solubility products of the three chlorides and the formation constant of the silver-ammonia ion are worked out by the build from Gibbs energies of formation in the NBS tables of chemical thermodynamic properties of 1982, read as a scan, pages by eye: 1.77 × 10⁻¹⁰, 1.71 × 10⁻⁵, 1.46 × 10⁻¹⁸ and 1.67 × 10⁷. The solubility of lead chloride, 10 g/L at 20 °C and 33.5 g/L at 100 °C, is from a page on the reactions of the lead ion. The solubility product of lead chromate is a university handout's figure, which gives no source for it; the tables have no Gibbs energy for it. The scheme, the eight rules the model's actions follow and what each solid and liquid looks like are from twelve sections of an open textbook of qualitative analysis, four pages on the reactions of metal ions, a college's laboratory experiment that is this scheme step for step, a university's handout for the same scheme, and a page of a college's pre-lab notes. Wherever a stored number can check a rule at the bench's amounts, the build makes the check and stops on a disagreement: that each chloride comes down and leaves under a hundredth dissolved, that the water holds all the lead chloride hot and about half of it cold, that lead chromate comes down with chromate left over, that the ammonia holds all the silver chloride, and that one addition of nitric acid leaves ammonia over and two make the liquid acid whatever the sample holds. Each step's Sources control, under the step, lists what the step rests on. The research record, `docs/research/cations/README.md`, says how each kind of value was read, where the lab is other than its design, what could not be sourced, and eleven things a chemist should look at, among them: that the solubility products are worked out and not read, and are not quite a textbook's; that lead chloride's solubility product does not give its measured solubility, so the lab uses the one for step 1 and the other for step 2; that the white solid lead chloride leaves with ammonia is named lead hydroxide as one handout names it, where a basic chloride is likely too; and that one figure's caption in the textbook's section on litmus says the opposite of the section's text, which the lab follows.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 17.7): that a careful run of the known and of each of the seven unknowns shows at every step what its ions give and nothing else, and counts, in both modes, and for the unknown a seed deals first at every seed from 1 to 60; that each slip of technique alone makes a run not count wherever it is made, is named, and is warned of first in Guided whether or not the sample holds the ion it matters for; that an observation or a tile that is not right is refused in Guided and does not count a run in Challenge, and that a naming that is not right does not count a run in either mode; that the same seed and the same actions give the same run; that the stored record survives a round trip after every action; that from four hundred actions taken as they come each part can still be finished; that Challenge is handed nothing of whether an observation is right before its step is checked; that two unknowns that have shown the same things are the same to the page until their ions are named; and the description in words of every tube a careful or a careless run can show. Also every sentence the lab can say, the build's checks of rules against numbers, the page's markup at each stage of a step in both parts and both modes, what the page draws in a tube, and the flow chart's tree.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The Group I cations lab: accessibility" above. In them a careful run of each part was made in Guided by mouse, and a run with each kind of slip and with a wrong observation and a wrong tile, and each said that it counted, or that it did not and why.
- **By one review,** on 2026-10-10, by an AI coding assistant that had built none of the lab, working by script in headless Chromium and by reading. It worked again by hand the four constants from their Gibbs energies and the build's checks of rules against numbers, among them the lead chloride in hot and in cold water and the acid against the ammonia; read every sentence a learner can be shown; read eleven of the documents the lab cites, in the copies the builders had saved; ran all seven unknowns in both modes; and set the pages of unknowns that had shown the same things side by side, 234 times, with none different. It changed no stored value, rule or model parameter. It made the refusal of a naming of none the engine's, put the centrifuge before the record in three steps' tasks, put right one sentence of the primer and the end of one hazard note, and corrected what the documents said of the grain's contrast, of the size of every control and of how much room one of the build's checks has. What it found, changed and left is the last section of `docs/research/cations/README.md`.
- **By nobody else.** The record, the engine, the primer, the wording and the screens were written, checked and reviewed by AI coding assistants. No person has run the lab or read its text.

**Known limits of the Group I cations lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the record, the stored figures, the rules, the notes, the primer, the feedback or the hazard text. The scanned tables were read by one reader, by eye.
- **The procedure is the usual scheme for these three ions,** as two of the procedures read have it, and each choice in it was the project's. A course's own lab may use other amounts or strengths, wash its solids, or confirm an ion another way.
- **A real bench of this lab holds lead, mercury, silver and chromate.** That is one reason for a virtual one, and the primer's safety card says so; see Lab safety below.
- **The lab does not teach the handling it leaves out:** how many drops to add and how to tell when enough has gone in, washing a solid, balancing a centrifuge, or keeping a tube hot. The primer's cards on centrifuging and on safety say what a real bench does.
- **The task of each step, which both modes show, says how the step is worked,** in order: add, heat, centrifuge, test with litmus until the paper shows acid, record, pour. A learner who follows it makes no slip, in Challenge too, which has the task and no hint and no warning.
- **Acid added twice at step 5 and recorded before litmus paper is tried does not count,** though the liquid is acid by then and any silver chloride has come back: the slip is recording before the paper has shown it. The reason such a run is given says that no solid can form until the liquid is acid, which is true and is not what that tube showed. The review left the rule and the sentence as they are.
- **The unknowns come round in an order, none twice in seven.** A learner who has named six of them can know the seventh before a tube shows anything.
- **The primer says what each result means,** and is not kept back in either mode: a learner who has read its cards on the three chlorides and on confirming tests knows which colour of solid confirms which ion. The Lab tab does not say it before a step is done, and never says which ions an unknown holds before they are named.
- **The flow chart's tiles name every solid and every ion of the scheme,** in both modes, from the first step: that is what filling the chart is. A learner in Challenge sees those nine names before the tubes show anything.
- **The tube described in words gives both answers of a step.** It is in the page for a learner who cannot read the picture, behind a disclosure that is closed, in both modes. The two questions still wait for the two looks.
- Every source was read and is not kept, but for the table of atomic weights the periodic table's record keeps, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again. It holds the record to the research record's account of what each page prints, and the worked-out values to the cited ones.
- The seven unknowns of a lab come in an order the seed fixes, so a learner who runs part 2 seven times meets each once, and the eighth is the first again. "Restart lab" draws a new seed, and with it an order.
- The file that "Export my progress" downloads holds the lab's seed, from which which ions each unknown holds follows for anyone who reads the source code.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The Le Chatelier's principle lab

**What it teaches.** Le Chatelier's principle: a system at equilibrium that is disturbed shifts in the direction that undoes part of the disturbance. The lab disturbs four equilibria, each in a test tube, seventeen times between them. Lead chloride with its ions: hydrochloric acid is added to lead nitrate solution in three portions, and a white solid comes with the second; the tube is heated, and the solid dissolves; cooled, and it comes back; water is added, and it dissolves. The two forms of methyl violet indicator: acid in two portions takes the violet through a colour between to yellow, and base in two takes it back. The pink and the blue ion of cobalt: water turns the blue tube violet, heat turns it blue, and ice water turns it pink. The hydroxides of zinc and of magnesium: a drop of sodium hydroxide brings each down as a white solid, which is shared among three tubes; acid dissolves both, and more sodium hydroxide and ammonia dissolve zinc's and not magnesium's. Each equilibrium is written one way and kept that way, so that "left" and "right" mean something. Nothing in the lab is drawn by chance: the same actions give the same tubes.

**The primer.** Ten cards, shown one at a time: what equilibrium is; Le Chatelier's principle; adding and taking away; heating and cooling; diluting; a solid and its ions; indicators; complex ions and colour; a second equilibrium; safety. Four are marked to read first. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A disturbance.** The learner is shown the equilibrium as written, with what stands on each side and the colour that tells it, and what is about to be done. They first say which way the equilibrium will shift: toward the left side, toward the right, or not at all. Then they make the change with one button: a count of drops at each press, or the tube stood in the bath of boiling water or of ice water. The tube is drawn, and what it shows is found by looking at it: a pointer over the liquid or the bottom of the tube, keyboard focus on it or a tap says what is seen there, and never what it is. Once the change is complete, both looks open two questions, whether there is a solid and the colour of the liquid. When those are recorded the disturbance asks its one question: which way the equilibrium went, or, at the two tubes of each metal that get more sodium hydroxide or ammonia, what took the metal ion away: hydroxide ions, ammonia, or nothing. Answering ends the disturbance, and its notes then say what happened and why, with the learner's prediction and answer over them.

**The prediction is not marked.** It is asked before the change, can be changed until the change begins, and is fixed after it. It is never said to be right or wrong, on the page or in the record, and does not bear on whether a system counts: it is there to be set beside what happened. No change can be made without one.

**Two modes.** Guided takes only an observation that is what the tube shows and only an answer that is right, checks a system by itself once its last disturbance is done, and has a hint for each disturbance in a disclosure that is closed, which says how to think about the question and not what the tube shows or which answer is right. Challenge gives no hint, takes whatever is recorded, takes an answer once, and checks a system when the learner asks. What is about to be done is said in both modes. The mode can be changed at any time; changed to Guided in the middle of a disturbance, Guided takes no answer until an observation that Challenge recorded there is what the tube shows.

**How entries are checked.**

- What is recorded at a disturbance is held to what its tube showed once the change was complete: both answers must be the tube's. Feedback says which part of the tube to look at again and never what it should have been.
- The answer to the disturbance's question is held to the one the lab's record stores for it. In Guided an answer that is not right is not taken, and the feedback does not say which is.
- A system counts when each observation is what its tube showed and each answer is right. A run that does not count says at which disturbance, for each reason, and the system is worked again from new tubes.
- There is no slip of technique that spoils a run in this lab: every change in it can be taken back, which is the point of it.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. One run that counts completes a system, and the lab is complete when all four are. A system can still be worked after that. The Record tab lists every run of each system, with what was predicted, recorded and answered at each disturbance and whether the run counted.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately. The model's own values are stored with their reasons, and the notes say what the lab takes as simpler than it is.

- The amounts are fixed: a count of drops at each press and a count of portions that is said beforehand. An addition that stopped when the solid came would say what the tube shows before it is looked at.
- Lead chloride comes down at once. On a real bench it may take minutes to show, and a real tube probably needs more acid than the lab's; the note of that disturbance says so.
- A tube in the bath of boiling water is taken to be at 100 °C, and one in ice water at 20 °C, the temperature the cold solubility of lead chloride is given for. Colder water holds less of it, so the solid that is there by that figure is there in ice water too.
- The cobalt tube is shown back at room temperature after its water is added. A real tube warms as water goes into concentrated acid, which pushes toward blue while the water pushes toward pink; the note of that disturbance says so.
- The indicator goes back to violet by acid and base that cancel exactly, which no bench does.
- A liquid with nothing coloured in it is drawn and named as colourless after every addition, with no source read for what it looks like then.
- The picture's level is not to scale, every solid is drawn at one height, and the colours on the screen are the drawing's own.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** Six constants are worked out by the build from Gibbs energies of formation in the NBS tables of chemical thermodynamic properties of 1982, read as a scan, pages by eye: the solubility products of zinc hydroxide and of magnesium hydroxide, 4.13 × 10⁻¹⁷ and 5.61 × 10⁻¹²; the formation constants of the two complex ions of zinc, with hydroxide and with ammonia, 2.82 × 10¹⁴ and 3.60 × 10⁸; the constant of ammonia as a base, 1.81 × 10⁻⁵; and the ion product of water. The heat that dissolving lead chloride takes in, about 23 kJ for each mole, is worked out from the same tables' enthalpies. The solubility of lead chloride, 10 g/L at 20 °C and 33.5 g/L at 100 °C, is from a page on the reactions of the lead ion, and methyl violet's range, pH 0.0 to 1.6, from a reference table of indicators. The procedure and the bottles are those of a college's handout for a lab of this kind, which leaves the results to the learner. The colours of the two cobalt ions and of the violet liquid hot and cold, and that the change toward blue takes in heat, are from a demonstration of the Nuffield Foundation and the Royal Society of Chemistry and a page of Chemguide; the other rules are from three pages on the reactions of metal ions and a section of an open textbook of qualitative analysis. Wherever a stored number can decide what a tube shows at the lab's amounts, the build works it out and stops on a disagreement, and on a number too near its edge to decide: whether lead chloride is there after each portion, hot, cold and diluted; the indicator's colour after each portion of acid and of base; and whether each hydroxide is there after the drop, the acid, the excess of base and the ammonia. The cobalt system is held by stored observations and not by number, since the tables have no row for the ion with chloride. Each disturbance's Sources lists what it rests on, and the research record (`docs/research/lechatelier/README.md`) gives every figure, every check with its margin, what could not be sourced, and eleven things a chemist should look at.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 18.7): that working each system's disturbances in order shows at every press what the record stores and nothing else, and that a system counts when each observation and each answer is right and only then, in both modes; that no press is taken without a prediction, that a prediction can be neither changed nor withdrawn once the change has begun, and that with every prediction a careful run counts and a wrong run is no more wrong for it; that the same actions give the same run; that the stored record survives a round trip after every action; that from four hundred actions taken as they come each system can still be finished; that Challenge is handed nothing of whether an entry is right before its system is checked, and no view holds what a later press will show; that a disturbance's notes are open exactly when its question has been answered; and the description in words of each of the thirty-eight tubes the lab shows. Also every sentence the lab can say, the build's checks of what a tube shows against its numbers, the page's markup at each stage of a disturbance in both modes, the colours the tubes are drawn in, and where the page opens.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The Le Chatelier's principle lab: accessibility" above. In them a careful run of the whole lab was made in each mode by mouse, a run with a wrong observation and a wrong answer in Challenge, and Guided was given a wrong observation and a wrong answer and refused each.
