- **By one review,** on 2026-10-11, by an AI coding assistant that had built none of the lab, working by script in headless Chromium and by reading. It used the page as a learner would, in both modes, by keyboard and by tap, in both themes; read every sentence a learner can be shown; worked again by hand the six constants from their Gibbs energies and the build's checks of lead chloride, of one drop of base with each metal and of zinc hydroxide in excess base and in ammonia, and found each as the record has it; read six of the documents the chemistry rests on and four of the safety data sheets, in the copies the builders saved; and made the changes that the accessibility section above and the research record give. It is not a chemist's reading.
- **By nobody else.** The record, the engine, the primer, the wording and the screens were written and checked by AI coding assistants. No person has run the lab or read its text.

**Known limits of the Le Chatelier's principle lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the record, the stored figures, the rules, the notes, the primer, the feedback or the hazard text. The scanned tables were read by one reader, by eye. The research record lists what a chemist should look at, among them: that a real bench probably needs more hydrochloric acid than the lab's to bring lead chloride down, since the lab decides by the solubility in plain water; that zinc hydroxide brought down by one drop of sodium hydroxide needs a large excess of ammonia by the tables' constants, and a real one may thin and not go; that the margins of those two checks, 5.4 and 3.8 times, use concentrations where the constants are for activities; and that methyl violet's equation is the handout's, one hydrogen ion apart, where the dye takes up more than one in strong acid.
- **The colour in the middle of methyl violet's range has no name here.** The table read gives the change as yellow to blue, and no source read names what lies between. The record stores "between blue and yellow", a look at the tube says those words, and the picture draws a colour half way between its blue and its yellow, which a learner may call green. A real tube may pass through more than one colour.
- **The primer gives answers the lab asks for,** and is not kept back in either mode: which way each kind of disturbance pushes, which changes of this bench take in heat, and that a large excess of hydroxide or of ammonia holds the zinc ion in a complex ion while magnesium has none. That is the design's choice (section 18.6 of the design spec). The Lab tab says none of it before the answer it belongs to is recorded.
- **The card of the equilibrium names the colour of each side,** in both modes, from the start: it is what an answer is read from. A learner who reads it knows before the change which colour would mean a shift to the left and which to the right, and at cobalt what a liquid that holds both ions looks like.
- **In Challenge a disturbance's notes open when its question is answered,** before the system is checked. They say which way the equilibrium went, so a learner then knows whether the answer just given was right, though the page puts no mark on it until the check and the answer cannot be changed. The notes of one disturbance can also bear on the next, as heating does on cooling.
- **The tube described in words gives both answers of what is seen.** It is in the page for a learner who cannot read the picture, behind a disclosure that is closed, in both modes. The two questions still wait for the change and the two looks.
- **The procedure is the usual one for these bottles,** as the handout read has it, with amounts that are the project's: a course's own lab may use other amounts or strengths, or other systems.
- **The lab does not teach the handling it leaves out:** counting drops, stirring, sharing a mixture among tubes, heating a tube in a bath, or working with concentrated acid. The primer's safety card says what a real bench of it holds.
- Every source was read and is not kept, but for the table of atomic weights the periodic table's record keeps, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again. It holds the record to the research record's account of what each page prints, and the worked-out values to the cited ones.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The clock reaction lab

**What it teaches.** How fast a reaction goes, and what that depends on. Bromate ions, iodide ions and acid make iodine slowly; a small, known amount of thiosulfate takes the iodine up as it forms, and starch shows the first iodine that is left over, so the liquid stays clear and colourless and then turns dark blue all at once. The time that takes says how fast the reaction went. Part 1 times five mixtures at room temperature, each different from the first in the amount of one reactant, or of all three. Part 2 finds from the five times the rate of each run, the order in each of the three reactants, and the rate constant. Part 3 times the first mixture again in ice water, cool water and warm water, and finds the activation energy from a graph of the logarithm of the rate constant against one over the temperature.

**The primer.** Eight cards, shown one at a time: how fast; what a rate depends on; the clock; finding an order; the rate constant; temperature and activation energy; reading the graph; safety. Four are marked to read first. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A run.** The learner sees the mixture as a table, adds starch to one flask, and for a run of part 3 stands both flasks in the bath, lets them stand and reads the thermometer. One press pours one flask into the other and starts the watch. The flask is drawn, and nothing changes in it until the liquid turns dark blue between one moment and the next. The learner stops the watch. The time that is recorded is the time the model gives with the learner's own delay added, in true seconds. A learner can instead have the watch stopped at the change; that run counts as any other, and the table of times and the Record tab say which way each run was timed.

**Two modes.** Guided warns before a pour that will not count (no starch; flasks that have not stood in their bath), checks each entry of a notebook as it is made, and has the relation each entry uses in a disclosure that is closed. Challenge gives no warning and no hint, takes whatever is entered, and checks a notebook when the learner asks. The mode can be changed at any time.

**How entries are checked.**

- A run counts when its flask held starch, when its flasks had stood in the bath where it has one, and when its watch was stopped at the change or by hand within 2.0 seconds after it. A run that does not count says why and is made again; a run that counts is not made again, since the notebook is worked from its time.
- Each entry of a notebook must follow from the learner's own earlier entries and the figures on the page, rounded to three significant figures as the primer says. Feedback says what kind of slip an entry looks like where the engine knows one (a figure cut off where it should be rounded, a concentration left as the bottle's, the six-to-one count left out, a sign, joules given for kilojoules) and never the value.
- The learner works one row by hand and the notebook works the rest, and says that it has: the other four runs' concentrations and rates once the first mixture's row is worked, their rate constants and the mean once the first is entered with its unit, and in part 3 each temperature's rate constant, its logarithm and one over the temperature.
- A notebook counts when every entry follows and its result is within 5% of what the lab's model gives for those runs: the three orders must be the model's, the mean rate constant within 5% of the model's at the room's temperature, and the activation energy within 5% of the model's.
- Attempts are unlimited and nothing is scored. The lab is complete when all three parts count. The Record tab lists every attempt at each run, with its time, how its watch was stopped and whether it counted, and every working of each notebook.

**Rules that are not true to a bench.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately, and the notes shown when a part is done say what the lab takes as simpler than it is.

- **The page plays the time faster than a clock.** The five mixtures of part 1 are played at one speed, 14 times a clock's, so that a run takes on the screen what it takes on the watch, at one scale: over seeds 1 to 100 the first mixture changes 7.9 to 10.5 true seconds after its pour, the second and third 3.9 to 5.2, the fourth, with twice the acid, 2.0 to 2.6, and the fifth 8.8 to 11.7. As first built each mixture had a speed of its own and all five took about as long, which hid what the lab is about; the review changed that. Each bath of part 3 has a speed of its own, 5, 30 and 55 times a clock's, so that a run of forty seconds and one of ten minutes each take 5 to 12 true seconds, and the page says beside the watch that each bath is played at its own pace. From the change the watch adds true time. The watch reads the lab's time throughout.
- **The rate is taken as steady** from the mixing to the change, with every concentration as it was at mixing, which is what the learner's own arithmetic assumes. Summed properly, a run would last about 1% longer.
- **A flask with no starch shows nothing at all.** A real one would slowly take on the colour of iodine.
- **Letting the flasks stand is one step,** ten minutes at a press with no clock run, after which they are at the bath's temperature and stay there. A run mixed before it is timed at a temperature half way between the room's and the bath's, which is the model's own device for a time that belongs to no one temperature.
- **Iodine is taken to meet thiosulfate in no time at all,** in ice water as in warm.
- **The watch stops itself at 30 minutes,** and a watch stopped by hand more than 2.0 seconds late does not count. The second is a limit of the lab's own: without it a lab whose every run counted could fail a part for the watch alone.
- The room's temperature, the baths' and a scatter of up to about 1% in each time are drawn by the seed, the scatter no larger than a bench's.

**Where the chemistry comes from, and which figures are the lab's own.** The orders, 1 in iodide, 1 in bromate and 2 in hydrogen ion, are cited to the summary of a kinetic study of this reaction and to the summary of a thesis. The rate constant, 50.26 L³/(mol³·s) at 25 °C, is cited to that thesis's summary, which measured it in a far saltier solution than these mixtures. **The activation energy, 50 kJ/mol, is the lab's own figure and not a measured one:** no source read gives it for a mixture like this bench's. It is stored as a model parameter with its reason, cites no source, and the note shown when part 3 is done, the sentence that says what the learner's result was held to, and the list of sources under each part all say that it is the lab's own. It is the size at which a rate about doubles for a rise of ten degrees near room temperature; the one paper read that measures it, in sulfuric acid with a salt added, reports far smaller apparent figures, and the note says that a real bench may well give a figure far from the lab's. The five mixtures are those of a school's handout for this experiment, with the lab's own bottles; the gas constant and the kelvin of 0 °C are NIST's. The research record, linked from the README, lists every figure with whose it is, what could not be sourced, and ten things a chemist should look at.

**How the lab was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 19.7): that for seeds 1 to 100 careful runs of the five mixtures and the three baths all count, that the orders that follow from them are the stored ones, and that the rate constant and the activation energy worked from them are within tolerance; that each error of technique alone makes a run not count, with its cause named, and that Guided warns first; that an entry that does not follow is refused or marked; that the same seed and actions give the same lab and the stored record round-trips; and that Challenge is shown nothing of what is expected before a part is done. The playing of a run's time is tested with no clock: that the five mixtures of part 1 are played at one speed, at which, at every seed from 1 to 100, the slowest changes within 12 true seconds of its pour, the fastest in two to three, and the fourth in a quarter of the first's true time; that each bath's run changes between 5 and 12 true seconds after its pour; that a press within half a second of a pour is taken for the pour come twice; that the watch never goes back; and that what a stopped watch reads is what the running one read.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The clock reaction lab: accessibility" above.
- **By one review.** Held on 2026-10-11, when the screens were whole, by an AI coding assistant that had built none of the lab, by script in headless Chromium and by reading. It read every sentence a learner can be shown, worked the primer's examples and one whole lab's figures again by hand, from its eight times to its rate constant and its activation energy, and they agreed with the page; it used the lab in both modes, by keyboard and by emulated touch; and it made the changes the owner had decided on and put right what it found. The last section of the lab's research record lists all of it, with what was left and what nobody checked.
- **By nobody else.** The record, the engine, the primer, the wording and the screens were written and checked by AI coding assistants. No person has run the lab or read its text.

**Known limits of the clock reaction lab.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the lab:** not the record, the stored figures, the rules, the notes, the primer, the feedback or the hazard text. Two of the sources behind the rate law were read only as summaries.
- **The activation energy is not a measured value,** as above, and the rate constant was measured in a different medium. A real bench need not give either, and the lab's times are the model's.
- **A learner's result is held to the lab's model, not to nature.** A careful learner is always within tolerance; a real class's figures would be judged by an instructor against what a real bench gives.
- **The lab does not teach the handling it leaves out:** measuring volumes, rinsing, swirling, reading a thermometer, holding a bath at its temperature, or working with potassium bromate. The primer's safety card says what a real bench of it holds.
- **The primer gives what the lab asks for,** and is not kept back in either mode: how an order is read from two runs, and the unit of a rate constant for each sum of orders. The Lab tab says none of this reaction's orders before part 2 is done.
- **Timing by hand on a screen is not timing at a bench:** what is added to a time is how late the press was, with whatever the device adds.
- The strength of the starch solution is not stored, and since the review no sentence of the lab gives one, where the bottle's note said about 2%: the documents read differ on it, and which is on a real bench is the laboratory's to say.
- Every source was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again. It holds the record to the research record's account of what each page prints.
- No learner has used the lab.

### The iodination of acetone lab

**What it teaches.** How fast a reaction goes and what that depends on, with a result that surprises: the rate does not depend on one of the reactants at all. Acetone and iodine react in acid, and the yellow of the iodine fades until none is left. Part 1 times four mixtures at room temperature: a first, and three that double the acetone, the acid or the iodine. Part 2 finds from the four times the rate of each run, the order in acetone, in hydrogen ion and in iodine, and the rate constant. There is no part on temperature.

**The primer.** Seven cards, shown one at a time, with those to read first marked. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A run.** The learner sees the mixture as a table. One press pours the iodine in and starts the watch. The flask is drawn beside a flask of water, on white; the yellow fades steadily, and the learner stops the watch when the two can no longer be told apart. The time that is recorded is the time the model gives with the learner's own delay added, in true seconds. A watch stopped while there is still colour, or more than five seconds after the last of it went, does not count, and the run is made again. A learner can instead have the watch stopped when the colour has gone; that run counts as any other, and the table of times and the Record tab say which way each run was timed.

**The notebook.** As the clock reaction lab's part 2: the learner works the first mixture's three concentrations and its rate by hand and the notebook fills the other three rows and says so; gives each order from the two runs its question names; then the rate constant of the first run and its unit, and the notebook fills the others and their mean. Guided checks each entry as it is made and has the relation ready to open; Challenge takes whatever is entered and checks the notebook all together.

**Whose figures.** The orders, the rate constant and the activation energy the model runs on are cited; nothing of the rate record is the lab's own figure. The rate constant and the activation energy were read from page images of a scanned table of 1951, by the lab's builder alone. The room's temperature, the small scatter in a time, the five seconds, the tolerance and the steps between the words for the colour are the lab's own. The lab's research record, `docs/research/acetone/README.md`, says which is which, what could not be sourced, and what a chemist should look at.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **The page plays the time faster than a clock,** 60 times, a minute on the watch to a second, at one speed for all four mixtures, and says so beside the watch: the mixture with twice the iodine is watched for twice as long as the first, 8.0 to 13.0 seconds against 4.0 to 6.5, and the two with twice the acetone or the acid for half as long, 2.0 to 3.3.
- **The end is drawn sharper than a bench shows it.** The model has the iodine go at one steady pace to none, and the drawing shows the last of it and then none. A real fade ends in a tail, and an eye loses the colour before that.
- **One rate constant for every mixture.** On a real bench the mixture with twice the acid would be a few percent faster than the model has it.
- **Timing by hand on a screen is not timing at a bench:** what is added to a time is how late the press was, with whatever the device adds.
- **The primer gives what the lab asks for,** and is not kept back in either mode. The Lab tab says none of this reaction's orders before part 2 is done.
- Every source was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again.
- No learner has used the lab, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read its record, its notes, its primer or its page. An AI reviewer read every sentence a learner is shown on 2026-10-11 and worked one lab's figures again by hand, from its four times to its rate constant; it changed one sentence of the primer, which said of a reactant of order nought that it is used up at a steady pace and now says that this is so if nothing else changes.

### The red cabbage pH lab

**What it teaches.** That pH is a scale of acidity, that an indicator shows it by colour, and that a unit of pH is ten times. The juice of red cabbage is an indicator with many colours. Part 1 puts it into thirteen buffers, pH 1 to pH 13, and the rack of them is a scale of colours. Part 2 places fourteen household things on that scale by eye and checks each with a pH meter. Part 3 asks, from the learner's own readings, which of two things holds more hydrogen ion and how many times more.

**The primer.** Short cards, shown one at a time, with those to read first marked. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A sample.** The learner adds the indicator to a thing's tube, looks at it, presses the tube of the scale it is nearest, which stays beside the sample, says whether the thing is acid, neutral or basic, and then rinses the meter's probe and reads it. A sample counts when the estimate is within one unit of the meter and acid, neutral or basic agrees with it. Bleach takes the colour out of the indicator, and there the right answer by eye is that the indicator cannot say. A reading taken with a probe that was not rinsed is pulled toward the last sample; it does not count, and says why; Guided warns first. Eight things of the fourteen make the part.

**Whose figures.** The colours against pH are a supplier's figure for red cabbage indicator, for pH 2 to 12, and a table for anthocyanins in general for pH 1 and 13. Each thing's range of pH is a document's, or is worked out from a cited strength and constant and said to be. Where one colour gives way to the next, what an unrinsed probe reads, what each thing looks like before the indicator goes in and the values the colours are drawn in are the lab's own. The research record, `docs/research/cabbage/README.md`, lists each.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **Nobody saw these colours or measured these pH values.** No bench was run. A real extract of red cabbage varies with the cabbage and how it was made, and the colour of a real sample will not match a row of buffers this neatly.
- **A sample shows exactly the colour of a buffer.** The model has twelve colours with sharp edges between them; a real indicator shades from one to the next, and a real thing's own colour or cloudiness changes what is seen. Apple juice and lemon juice are pale yellow before the indicator goes in, and the model shows the indicator's colour all the same.
- **Neutral means the colour of the pH 7 tube,** a reading from 6.6 to 7.5; the page says so beside the question.
- **An unrinsed probe reads a fifth of the way toward the last sample, to a tenth.** Between two things two tenths apart or less that is no way at all, and the meter reads what a rinsed probe reads; the reading does not count all the same, and the page says that the meter may have been pulled.
- **pH 11 and pH 12 share a colour,** as the supplier's figure has them, and everything above pH 12.0 is drawn yellow. A drain cleaner at 12.1 is therefore yellow beside a greenish-yellow pH 12 tube.
- **The drain cleaner is taken as sodium hydroxide in water alone.** The maker's sheet that gives its strength also lists bleach, which would take the colour out; its note says so. Whether it belongs on this bench as a plain base is a teacher's to decide.
- **Several figures stand in for the bench's own dilution:** the toiletries' sheets give the product as sold, and the model takes one part in twenty to read the same; the aspirin's figure is for a weaker suspension; the bleach is worked out for hypochlorite alone. The research record lists these for a chemist.
- **Buffered aspirin,** which the bench's directions list, is left off: no document that states its pH could be read.
- **In Guided an estimate that is not near enough is refused,** so a learner can find a thing's place by trying tubes. Challenge records what it is given.
- **The primer gives what the lab asks for,** and is not kept back in either mode.
- Every source was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again.
- No learner has used the lab, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read its record, its notes, its primer or its page.

### The measurement and density lab

**What it teaches.** That every instrument is read to one figure beyond its finest mark, that this last figure is an estimate, and that a number worked out from measurements can be no better than the worst of them. Part 1 has a paper note measured with a ruler marked in centimetres and with one marked in millimetres, a volume of water in a 50 mL graduated cylinder, a thermometer in ice and water, and a mass on a balance; then the note's area from each ruler, and how far the thermometer is off. Part 2 finds the density of an unknown liquid from two weighings and a volume, and part 3 that of an unknown metal from a weighing and the water its pieces push aside; each unknown is then named from a table. It is two first-week labs built as one in three parts.

**The primer.** Eight short cards, shown one at a time, with those to read first marked: what a measurement is; reading a scale between its marks; a liquid's level; which zeros count; the two rounding rules, each worked once on numbers that are no run's; density; volume by the water pushed aside; safety. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A reading.** Each instrument is drawn, and a closer look of its scale is opened by a button. The learner types what they read. A reading must have the figures its instrument gives, and one with too few or too many is refused with the reason: 13.8 on the centimetre ruler, 13.84 on the millimetre ruler, a final zero included. It counts within two of its last figure of where the edge, the level or the thread stands. Beside the cylinder the learner moves their eye above, level with or below the liquid's level, and the level is drawn as it looks from there; the eye begins above. The page never prints a reading of a scale: only the balance, which is read by copying its display, shows figures.

**The notebook.** A difference is given to the decimal place of its least exact term, and a product or a quotient to as many significant figures as its factor with the fewest. Each worked-out entry is held to the learner's own readings by its rule, and one with the right arithmetic and the wrong count is told which rule it broke. A whole number that ends in a zero, such as an area of 70 cm², is taken with the zero counted where its rule asks for two figures. Guided checks each reading and entry as it is made, says how many significant figures each reading has, keeps the relation and the rule in a closed hint, and warns before a level is read with the eye off it, before the pieces go in unweighed or are dropped in, and before wet pieces are weighed. Challenge takes what is typed and says nothing until the notebook is checked. A part counts when its readings are right, its entries follow, its technique was right and, for an unknown, the density is within 3% of the stored figure and the right row is named. A part can be run again with new numbers.

**Whose figures.** The densities of the ethanol and of the salt solutions are two handbooks', each read through an encyclopedia's data page that transcribes its table, and water's is a government institute's, all at 20 °C; the metals' are a handbook's, read the same way, for "near room temperature". Neither handbook was itself read. Each is stored to three significant figures with the figure its source prints beside it. How near a reading must be, how a level looks from above or below, how much water clings to wet pieces or splashes out, and every amount set out are the model's own, and the lab's research record lists them.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **Nobody read these scales at a bench.** The drawings are clean: a real meniscus is harder to see, a real ruler's edge has thickness, and a real balance drifts.
- **Which way a level looks from above or below is the model's own.** No source read says. The model has it half a millilitre higher on the scale from above and lower from below; the primer and the page say only that such a reading is wrong.
- **The ethanol is 95% by mass,** as its source defines the strength. A bottle whose 95% is by volume holds a weaker and denser liquid, and a denatured alcohol, whose safety data sheet the hazards rest on, is neither. The research record says what that changes.
- **The metals' densities have no temperature in degrees,** and iron is 7.87 g/mL where another handbook has 7.86.
- **The pieces of metal are drawn alike.** Real copper is red-brown and would be named without a measurement.
- **The balance alone tells a learner who knows how much metal is set out.** The pieces are 15 to 20 mL of metal whichever it is, so they weigh 40 to 54 g as aluminium, 107 to 143 g as zinc and 134 to 179 g as copper. No page says so. As first built each metal's hazard note gave its own range and could be opened before the unknown was named; the review took the amounts out.
- **An area that comes to a whole number of tens is typed as it is.** The centimetre ruler's area has two figures, and at about one seed in ten it comes to 50, 60, 70, 80 or 90 cm². An entry takes no power of ten, so the notebook counts the zero of "70" where the rule asks for two figures, and the primer says so. As first built it refused "70" as one figure, and part 1 could not be finished there.
- **Pieces weighed wet come out inside the tolerance** (0.4% to 3.0% high): that run does not count because of the technique, which is named, and not because of its number. Pieces dropped in come out 4% to 11% high.
- **In Guided a reading more than two of its last figure from the scale is refused,** so a learner can find a reading by trying. Challenge records what it is given.
- **The thermometer is a few tenths of a degree off in ice and water,** by the seed, and part 1 asks by how much. Nothing is then corrected with it.
- **The liquid is taken to be at 20 °C,** and nothing evaporates from an open cylinder of ethanol.
- **Every density is judged at three significant figures,** so the 1.1478 a source prints for the salt solution is 1.15.
- Every source but the periodic table's copy was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again.
- No learner has used the lab, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read its record, its notes, its primer or its page.

### The freezing point depression lab

**What it teaches.** That something dissolved in water lowers the temperature at which it freezes, by an amount that depends on how many dissolved particles there are for each kilogram of water and not on what they are; so that a fall in the freezing point counts particles. Part 1 finds what the lab's probe reads in stirred ice and water. Part 2 finds the molar mass of an unknown liquid from the fall a weighed amount of it gives, and names it from a table of three: acetone, ethanol and 2-propanol. Part 3 finds how many dissolved particles each formula unit gives, for urea and for one of three salts of the learner's choosing: close to one for urea, and close to two for a salt.

**The primer.** Nine short cards, shown one at a time, with five marked to read first: freezing and melting; what dissolving does; molality; the freezing point constant; from a fall to a molar mass; salts, ions and the count of particles; reading the graph; straining and weighing; safety. A molar mass and a count of particles are each worked once on numbers that are no run's. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**A run.** Ice and water go into a foam cup; the lid goes on, the stirrer and the logger are started, and "Run on" lets a minute pass at each press. No clock runs. When the readings are level the weighed solute goes in; the temperature falls and levels again; the cup is poured through a strainer and the solution is weighed. The learner finds the two temperatures by looking at points of the logger's graph: the page does not print them. A run counts when its technique was right, its temperatures are the logger's, its entries follow, and its result is within 1.5% of the liquid's molar mass, with the liquid named rightly, or within 3% of the count of particles the lab holds for the solute. A run that does not count says why, and the part is run again with new numbers. Part 3 is done when a run with urea counts and a run with a salt does, and it stays open after the lab is complete, so that the other salts can be tried.

**The notebook.** The two temperatures are typed to a hundredth of a degree and must be the logger's own: the reading the solute went in after, and the last reading, taken as the cup is strained. The mass of water is the solution's less the solute's, in kilograms with all five decimals the balance gives. Every other number is typed to three significant figures and must be a right rounding of its relation worked on the learner's own entries above it. Guided checks each entry as it is made, says what kind of slip a wrong one looks like and never the value, and has each relation in a closed hint; Challenge takes what is typed and checks the notebook when asked.

**Three errors of technique,** each with its own cause and each named when a run does not count: a temperature read before it had levelled off; the ice all melted, so that what was read was no freezing point; and the solution weighed with the ice in it. A lid left off and a stirrer left still are how the first two come about. Guided warns before each.

**Whose figures.** The freezing point constant of water, 1.86 °C·kg/mol, is from the table of an encyclopedia's article, which gives it from a book of chemical data that was not read. The molar masses are worked out from the standard atomic weights the periodic table keeps, with the working shown once a part is done. Sodium chloride's count of particles, 1.82, is worked out from a handbook's freezing point of a 3% solution, read in an encyclopedia's transcription. The lab's research record, `docs/research/freezing/README.md`, gives each value with its source and the model's twenty-four parameters with the reason for each.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **Two of the three salts are held to two particles, the ideal.** No measured freezing point of a solution of potassium chloride or of sodium bromide was found in a source that could be read. A learner who picks sodium chloride is held to 1.82 and one who picks either of the others to 2.00, though real solutions of those two give about as much under two as sodium chloride does. The notes say so.
- **The three liquids and urea are held to one particle,** the ideal; one handbook row for ethanol agrees to the one figure it prints.
- **The constant is used as it stands** for solutions of 0.4 to 1.0 mol/kg, where a real solution is no longer dilute, and each count of particles is used at every strength of the bench.
- **A real bench would not tell acetone from 2-propanol this way.** Their molar masses are 3.5% apart; the lab can because its cup is ideal, and its tolerance, 1.5%, is fixed by those two. The notes say that the fall has to be good to about 1%.
- **The cup is ideal.** The solute comes at the cup's temperature and gives or takes no heat on dissolving; the liquids are pure and do not evaporate, acetone among them; the strainer holds back all the ice and none of the solution; and the probe is off by one amount at every temperature, which is what part 1 finds.
- **A cup with its lid off loses all its ice in about seven minutes.** That is faster than a real cup, and is there for the lesson.
- **The second temperature is the last reading, not any reading of the level stretch.** The ice goes on melting slowly after the fall, so the solution that is weighed is the one in the cup when it is strained. A learner who takes an earlier level reading is refused in Guided and marked in Challenge.
- **The mass of water is typed with five decimals.** To three figures it would be half a percent out, a third of what parts acetone from 2-propanol.
- **In Guided a wrong entry is refused,** so a learner can find an entry by trying. Challenge takes whatever is typed.
- Every source was read and is not kept, so the build cannot check the record against the pages again; it does work every molar mass and every measured row out again.
- No learner has used the lab, and no chemist or chemistry instructor has read its record, its notes, its primer or its page.

### The types of reactions lab

**What it teaches.** That most reactions a first course meets are one of four kinds, and how to tell which from what went in and what came out: two things join into one (combination), one thing comes apart (decomposition), an element takes the place of another in a compound (single replacement), two compounds trade partners (double replacement). Five stations, each with one reaction to set off: zinc in copper(II) sulfate solution; magnesium held in a flame; magnesium, zinc and copper each in hydrochloric acid; calcium chloride and sodium carbonate solutions; hydrogen peroxide with a few drops of iron(III) chloride. At each the learner records what is seen, says which kind it is, and builds its balanced equation from tiles. The station of three metals also asks for their order, the most active first, and has a tube in which nothing reacts.

**The primer.** Six short cards, shown one at a time, with four marked to read first: what a reaction is, and the signs of one; the four kinds, each with its pattern in letters; an equation, what balancing means, and one equation worked by counting atoms; metals in order; a catalyst; safety. It is under 800 words. It is not kept back in either mode, so it says nothing of what the stations show: its examples and its worked equation are none of the bench's, and it gives the rules by which metals are put in order and not the order of the bench's three. Each card lists its sources, and the primer quotes none of them.

**How entries are checked.** What is seen is recorded from fixed choices, which open once the station's action is done and every part of the picture has been looked at. The kind is one choice of five, the same five at every tube. An equation is right when each side holds the things of the stored equation and no other, each in a state it takes, and the two sides hold the same atoms, which the lab counts: so a whole multiple of an equation is right, and hydrogen peroxide may be written as dissolved or as a liquid. What is wrong is named by its kind (something that does not belong, something missing, a state, the balance) and never by what would put it right. A learner who says that nothing reacted is asked for no equation, at any tube. Guided refuses an answer that is not right and has a hint for each thing asked, the same at every station; Challenge takes what it is given and checks a station when asked. A station counts when what was seen, the kind, the equation and, where it is asked, the order are right; no slip of technique spoils a run, and a station can be worked again at any time. The notes of a station, which say what each thing was and why, are shown once it counts and not before.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **The fifth station is this lab's reading of the directions,** which name iron(III) chloride and nothing to go with it. Hydrogen peroxide was chosen because it gives the one kind of reaction the other four stations lack. If the directions mean another reaction, the station is wrong as a whole.
- **What is seen is what a source describes, and a real tube may differ.** Zinc in 1 M hydrochloric acid is shown bubbling slowly; a clean piece may hardly bubble at first. One piece of zinc in 1 M copper(II) sulfate is shown with a red-brown coating and a paler blue; the fading takes long, and the copper often comes down dark and loose. The white solid of calcium carbonate is drawn spread through the liquid, and in a real tube it settles. The peroxide tube is drawn pale yellow, which is this lab's own reading and is asked of nobody; it may well be darker while it reacts.
- **That the peroxide tube warms enough to feel** is not from a measurement: the sources give the heat of the reaction and no rise in temperature for a tube this small.
- **Burning magnesium** leaves an ash that holds some nitride with the oxide; the note says so, and the equation is for the oxide alone. On a real bench it is often a demonstration, and a supervisor decides.
- **The order of the three metals** is stored from a cited reactivity series, and not worked out from potentials.
- **No everyday case of a single replacement** is in the primer: none was found in a source that was read.
- **Three stations ask for something no sentence gives before the station counts:** that burning in air is joining with the air's oxygen; which of two products is the solid; and that the iron(III) chloride is a catalyst and so is in no equation. The review found them and left them, since each would put most of an answer on the page.
- No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes, the primer or the page. The lab's review, by an AI coding assistant, checked the six equations by hand and read every sentence a learner sees, some of them against the pages they rest on. The lab's research record lists ten things a chemist should look at.

### The polarity and mixing lab

**What it teaches.** That some liquids mix and some stay as two layers, that some solids dissolve in a liquid and some do not, and that one rule covers most of it: like dissolves like. Five liquids are on the bench, each a household thing (distilled water, rubbing alcohol, vinegar, vegetable oil, mineral oil), and two solids (table salt, sugar). In part 1 two liquids go into a tube, more of one than of the other; the tube is shaken and left to stand; and the learner says whether there is one layer or two and, of two, which liquid is on top. In part 2 a little salt or sugar goes into a liquid, and the learner says whether it is gone. Each part is done when six tubes count, of nine pairs and of eight solids in liquids. In part 3 the learner sorts the five liquids into two families, from what the tubes showed, and answers two questions about things that are not on the bench.

**The primer.** Six short cards, shown one at a time, with four marked to read first: mixing and not mixing, with the four words for it; a molecule with two ends and one with none; like dissolves like; which layer is on top; why salt comes apart in water; safety. It is under 700 words, and each card lists its sources.

**How entries are checked.** Everything is recorded from fixed choices, which open once the tube has stood and has been looked at. A pair's tube counts when the number of layers is the one stored and, of two, the liquid named as on top is the less dense by the stored densities; a solid's when what is said of it is what is stored. Guided refuses an answer that is not right and has a hint at each question; Challenge takes what it is given and checks a tube, or part 3, when asked, and shows no mark, note or family before that. No slip of technique spoils a tube, and nothing is drawn by chance.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **No tube of this bench was set up.** Every outcome is from a document, and ten of the seventeen rest on a rule for a family or on what a bottle holds, not on a source that names the two things. The lab's research record says which.
- **Three things are left off the bench,** because nothing that was read settles what they show: rubbing alcohol with mineral oil, and salt and sugar in rubbing alcohol. The page says so where the tubes are chosen.
- **The rubbing alcohol is taken as about 70% 2-propanol in water.** With the vegetable oil it is given as two layers with the alcohol on top, from the stored densities, which are 0.053 g/mL apart; the one source read is for alcohol of 75% or more. A bench with the 91% kind may see something else.
- **Rubbing alcohol sorts with water,** and the note of part 3 says that its molecule has an oily part too. There is no third family.
- **A tube is drawn cloudy until it has stood,** whatever it holds, and settled at once when it stands. Real pairs can stay cloudy for some time.
- **Two of the five densities are specific gravities printed with no temperature,** and the record says so.
- **Mixed liquids are drawn at the sum of their volumes,** and pale yellow where one is the oil. Both are the lab's own.
- **Part 3's second question says that Epsom salt is a salt, made of charged particles.** No source cited under part 3 says so. The lab's review found it in two pages read for other parts of the project (PubChem's page for magnesium, and a page on metals with dilute acids that the types of reactions lab cites); they are named in the record and are not cited on the page.
- **The primer gives the rule before the bench,** and says that oil floats on water. The page of parts 1 and 2 says of no tube what it shows before it is looked at, and gives no liquid's family.
- No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes, the primer or the page. The lab's review, by an AI reviewer on 2026-10-12, worked again by hand which liquid is on top in the five pairs that make layers and read every sentence a learner sees. The lab's research record lists seven things a chemist should look at, and what the review looked at and left.

### The endothermic and exothermic lab

**What it teaches.** That a change can give heat out or take heat in, that the water a salt dissolves in shows which, and the two words: exothermic and endothermic. It is the calorimetry lab made small, for an introductory course: the same foam cup, logger and graph, with no joules and no moles. In part 1 a weighed portion of Epsom salt, about 10 g, is stirred into 50.0 mL of water, and in part 2 about 4 g of dry calcium chloride; the two can be done in either order. For each the learner reads the starting temperature and the highest or the lowest from the graph, works out the change, and says whether the water got warmer or colder, whether the dissolving gave heat out or took heat in, and which word that is. Part 3 asks which of the two salts would suit a pack that warms and which a pack that cools.

**The primer.** Six short cards, shown one at a time, with four marked to read first: temperature and heat; giving heat out and taking heat in, with the two words; where the heat goes; reading the graph; hot packs and cold packs; safety. It is about 650 words, and each card lists its sources. It says of neither salt which way its temperature goes.

**How entries are checked.** A typed temperature must be the reading the logger shows, to the tenth of a degree; the change must be the difference of the two temperatures entered; and each of the three choices must follow from the learner's own entries above it. Guided warns before the salt goes in with the lid off or the stirrer still, refuses an entry that does not follow and has a hint at each entry; Challenge takes what it is given and checks the notebook when asked. A run counts when the lid was on and the cup stirred from the moment the salt went in, its two temperatures are the logger's and its entries follow. No result is held to a tolerance, since no figure is worked out.

**What is idealised, and what a teacher should know:**

- **No cup of this bench was run.** The heat of each salt dissolving is worked from enthalpies of formation in the NBS tables, for one mole of salt in 75 moles of water, the row nearest the strength on the bench: +17.56 kJ/mol for Epsom salt and −77.689 kJ/mol for calcium chloride. Each figure of the scanned tables was read by eye, twice, by the AI coding assistant that built the lab.
- **The hydrate matters.** Epsom salt is magnesium sulfate with seven waters in its crystal, and takes heat in as it dissolves. By the same tables, magnesium sulfate with no water in its crystal gives out about 85 kJ for each mole. A bottle filled with the dry salt would turn part 1 round, and the part's notes say so.
- **The size of the two changes.** The model has Epsom salt's cup fall by 2.7 to 2.9 °C and calcium chloride's rise by 11.6 to 12.8 °C, to 37 °C at the most. Each solution is given water's specific heat, the cup takes up no heat, and each salt is dissolved within a minute of stirring. A real cup will differ, and Epsom salt in large crystals may dissolve more slowly.
- **Epsom salt's two figures are far apart:** +17.56 kJ/mol at the bench's strength and +11.78 at endless dilution, both from the tables. A figure quoted elsewhere may be either.
- **Dry calcium chloride takes up water from the air,** by its sheets, and a bottle that has done so gives out less heat. It is also sold with water in its crystal; the record is for the dry salt alone.
- **Part 3 asks only what the two runs showed.** A page that was read names calcium chloride dissolving as its example of a hot pack that mixes. Another names ammonium nitrate, calcium ammonium nitrate and urea for the cold packs that are sold, and not Epsom salt; the part's note says so. Both pages are encyclopedia articles.
- **A lid left off or a stirrer left still makes a run not count by rule.** In the model such a cup reads 0.2 to 0.3 °C short for Epsom salt and 0.9 to 1.2 °C short for calcium chloride, and would still show which way the heat went.
- **The second entry is named "Highest or lowest temperature reached"** whatever the graph shows, since which of the two it was is the next question.
- No chemist or chemistry instructor has read the record, the notes, the primer or the page, and the lab's one deep review has not yet been held. The lab's research record lists six things a chemist should look at.

### The mechanism timeline

**What it teaches.** What happens, molecule by molecule, when sodium hydrogen carbonate (baking soda) is added to hydrochloric acid and fizzes. The learner moves a timeline through seven chapters: the beaker before; the acid as it is before mixing, as hydronium ions and chloride ions; the solid dissolving into sodium ions and hydrogen carbonate ions; a hydronium ion giving a proton to a hydrogen carbonate ion, which makes carbonic acid; carbonic acid splitting into water and carbon dioxide, with a water molecule passing a proton along; the carbon dioxide leaving as gas; and the beaker afterwards. One unit of the solid and one of the acid stand for all of them: fourteen atoms, followed through the whole change.

**What a learner does.** Plays the timeline, steps through it with Previous and Next, or moves a slider to any point of it. The scene is drawn in 3D and can be turned and zoomed. Pointing at a molecule or an ion, or tapping it, names it, and a list beside the scene gives the same names with which of them take part in the chapter. Each chapter has a short text and a longer one that is closed until it is opened; the texts say what an ion, a proton, a lone pair and a double bond are where each word is first needed. A panel that floats over the page draws free energy and enthalpy along the timeline and says what each step changes.

**Two modes.** Guided asks nothing. Challenge stops before each of the four steps and asks what happens next, with four answers of which one is right. One press answers. The feedback says whether the answer is right and why, and the chapter's text follows. Until a question is answered the page keeps back the name of its step, and until every question is answered the energy panel gives its figures without the notes, the working and the sources that would answer some of them. The first answer to each question is the one kept, until the learner presses "Start again", which asks first. Nothing is scored, and when the end is reached the page says how many of the predictions were right. The timeline is completed when its end has been reached, in either mode, by playing or by moving the slider there.

**How answers are checked.** Each question and its four answers are in the timeline's record, with which one is right and a sentence of feedback for each. The stored progress holds which answer was chosen, and whether it was right is worked out from the record each time. Automated tests hold that each question has exactly one right answer.

**What is drawn, and what is not.**

- The molecules and the polyatomic ions have the shapes of the explorer's library: the experimental structure for water and carbon dioxide, and a computed conformer for the hydronium ion, the hydrogen carbonate ion and carbonic acid.
- Every ball is at one scale. The atoms of a molecule are balls a quarter of their van der Waals radius, as in the explorer's ball and stick view, and the sodium ion and the chloride ion are balls a quarter of their ionic radius, with the symbol and charge written beside them. So a sodium ion is drawn a little smaller than a hydrogen atom's ball, which is what the two radii give. A line under the scene says that every ball is drawn much smaller than the atom or ion it stands for, so that the bonds can be seen.
- **The solid is not drawn before it is added.** "Before mixing" shows the acid alone: a hydronium ion, a chloride ion and one water molecule. The unit of the solid comes into the picture in step 1, and until its two ions have moved apart it has one name, "unit of solid sodium hydrogen carbonate", and no charge written on its sodium, so that the page does not answer the first question.
- **A molecule or an ion is named from its bonds as they are.** While a proton crosses, the hydronium ion is called a hydronium ion until the proton is half-way, and a water molecule from then on.
- The water that fills the beaker is not drawn, nor the water that surrounds each ion. One water molecule is in the scene from the start, because it takes part in step 3. The first chapter's text says so.
- A bond that is forming, breaking or changing order is drawn as dashes, and the scene's description says how far along it is. Curved arrows show where each pair of electrons goes during the two steps in which bonds change: each starts at the edge of the atom a lone pair is on or at the middle of the bond a pair comes from, and ends at the middle of the bond the pair becomes or at the edge of the atom it stays on. They are flat marks on the picture, drawn over the atoms, and all the arrows of a step are shown together, though the pairs they stand for do not move one after another.
- The bubble is not drawn atom by atom, and the two bookends are a drawn beaker.

**The energies, and which of them are cited, derived or drawn.** All are for standard conditions at 25 °C, in kilojoules per mole. The panel says of each value which of these it is.

| Step | Free energy | Enthalpy | Barrier |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 Dissolving | +2.3, from cited formation values | +18.7, from cited formation values | none drawn |
| 2 Proton transfer | −19.9, from the cited acid constant of the carbonic acid molecule | counted with step 3 | no value; drawn, and labelled "not to scale" |
| 3 Decomposition | −16.4, derived: the cited total for steps 2 and 3, less step 2 | −7.6 for steps 2 and 3 together, from cited formation values | about 66, derived from how long a carbonic acid molecule lasts |
| 4 Gas escapes | −8.4, from cited formation values | +20.3, from cited formation values | none drawn |

The four steps sum to +31.4 for the enthalpy and −42.4 for the free energy, which is what the cited tables give for the whole change taken at once; an automated test holds the sums to that, and nothing was adjusted to reach it. The point the panel makes is that the change takes in heat and runs all the same, because the gas that escapes is far more spread out.

**No figure is read off a drawn curve.** Between the level a step starts at and the level it ends at, each curve is a smooth line that was chosen for the drawing, and so is the shape of a barrier. A cursor on the curves shows where the timeline is and gives no number. Every energy the page prints, and every one the scene's description gives, is a step's own whole change or a sum of whole steps. Automated tests hold that at a thousand points of the timeline.

**Rules that are not true to a real solution.** Each is a simplification, chosen deliberately:

- **Where the atoms are between and around the reacting molecules is Valence's own choreography.** It is not a computed or a measured path: how far apart two species are when they meet, where the water that passes the proton sits, the turn of one O–H group of carbonic acid before the proton moves, and the order in which things move were all placed by hand, from the library's shapes. The first chapter's longer text says so, step 3's says it of how the hydrogen crosses, and so do the panel's Sources control and the About page.
- **That one water molecule passes the hydrogen along in step 3 is the route drawn, not a finding.** No source was read for the path by which carbonic acid breaks up. The timeline draws one water molecule taking a hydrogen from one O–H group and giving one of its own to the other, in one move; more water molecules may take part in a real solution, and the step may not be one move.
- **The track is not linear in time.** A step that takes picoseconds has about as much room as one that takes seconds. Each step is labelled with its real timescale, and Play takes one minute for the whole track whatever the steps really take. Step 3 is labelled the slower of the two steps that change bonds; dissolving and the gas leaving, which change none, are labelled in seconds.
- **The acid constant of carbonic acid** was measured at 20.5 °C and is used at 25 °C with no correction. It is the constant of the carbonic acid molecule itself, 3.49, and not the 6.35 of textbooks, which counts dissolved carbon dioxide as the acid; a note in the panel says so.
- **The barrier of step 3** is worked out by the Eyring equation, with a transmission coefficient of one, from a lifetime that the source gives as about 60 milliseconds at a room temperature it does not state. A lifetime twice as long or half as long moves it by 1.7 kJ/mol.
- **The barrier of step 2 has no value.** It is drawn 10 kJ/mol high so that it can be seen, and labelled as not to scale.
- **Steps 1 and 4 are drawn with no barrier.** Neither is one move over a barrier.
- **The enthalpy curve shows steps 2 and 3 as one change,** because no heat of forming the carbonic acid molecule is cited. One has been published and was not read at its source; the research record says where.
- **The proton is drawn leaving a hydronium ion, H₃O⁺.** In the experiment the timescale of step 2 rests on, the proton came from another acid, made by a flash of light.

**Where the chemistry comes from.** The formation values are read from the NBS tables of 1982, as a scan, by eye. The acid constant of carbonic acid, how long it lasts, and the timescales of steps 2 and 3 are from one paper of 2016 in the Journal of Physical Chemistry B, read as the authors' manuscript: its text and tables, not its figures. Three constants are from NIST's listing of the 2022 CODATA values. The two ionic radii are Shannon's of 1976, checked through a transcription and not in the paper. The timescales of steps 1 and 4 ("seconds") have no source and are stored as everyday observations. The research record, `docs/research/phase-5/sources/README.md`, says how each kind of value was read, what is worked out from what, and what could not be sourced.

**How the timeline was checked.**

- **By automated tests.** The acceptance list of the design spec (section 9.3): that the scene is continuous at every chapter boundary; that each step's bonds broken and formed are the difference between the bond lists at its two ends; that the atoms and the total charge are the same at every boundary and every species there has its library record's bonds; that the energies sum as above; and that each chapter's link opens at its start. Also: that the scene at a position is the same whichever way it is reached; that no two atoms sit on or pass through each other; that every energy is worked out by the build from the cited values; the wording; the stored progress; the scene's description; the drawing against the model; and the page's markup.
- **By script in a browser.** The checks listed under "The mechanism timeline: accessibility" above.
- **By one reviewer that did not build it, which was not a person.** The timeline was written and checked by AI coding assistants. On 2026-10-08 another AI coding assistant, which had built none of it, reviewed the phase: it read every sentence a learner sees as a teacher of first-year chemistry would, looked at the scene at every chapter, and ran the checks listed under "The review of the phase" above. It rewrote eight sentences of the chapters, the questions' feedback, the panel's notes and one timescale label (the build record lists each, with why), and changed how the scene is drawn and framed. No chemist, no chemistry instructor and no other person has read the timeline.

**Known limits of the mechanism timeline.**

- **No chemistry instructor and no chemist has reviewed the timeline:** not the chapters' text, the four questions and their answers, the choreography, the energies or what is said of them.
- **The choreography has been looked at by nobody but AI coding assistants:** the ones that made it and drew it, and the one that reviewed it and changed it. As first drawn, the carbon dioxide molecule of step 4 passed in front of a water molecule as the camera saw it; it now leaves where nothing else is, and an automated test holds it clear of every other species in the picture.
- **The last question is largely answered by the first chapter,** whose longer text says that the fizz is carbon dioxide leaving the liquid. What the question still asks is what becomes of the two kinds of ion.
- No source is kept, so the build cannot check a stored value against its page again. It holds the timeline's record to the research record's list of what each page was read to print.
- The paper that the acid constant is from did not itself measure how long carbonic acid lasts; it gives that in one sentence of its introduction, citing older studies that were not read.
- That the powder is the one sold as baking soda is said once, with no source.
- How good the three answers that are not right are, as answers a learner might really give, has been judged by no person. The reviewing assistant found one piece of feedback that gave away the next question's answer, and rewrote it; an automated test now holds each question's words clear of the answers still to be asked.
- No learner has used the timeline.

## Lab safety

Valence makes no claim that what it shows about hazards meets any rule or standard for hazard communication. This section says what is shown, where it comes from, and what nobody has checked.

- **A supplement, not a replacement.** Valence is a simulation. It supplements bench work and does not replace it, and it does not replace safety training; the About page and the first paragraph of the primer's safety card say so. Nothing in it teaches the handling of real glassware or chemicals, and finishing a lab in Valence is no evidence that a learner can work safely at a bench.
- **What each bottle's hazard information classifies.** Each of the titration lab's three bottles has a "Hazards" disclosure: a heading that says whose hazards these are and in what form, under it the signal word and the hazard statements with their GHS codes, a note headed "At the strength used here", and the sources of both. The statements are those of one supplier's safety data sheets, Flinn Scientific's, for the concentrated or the pure substance, stored as the sheets print them: concentrated hydrochloric acid, 6 M to 12 M; solid sodium hydroxide; and solid phenolphthalein. All three give the signal word Danger. They are not the hazards of the dilute solutions on the lab's bench, and the heading over each, and the primer's safety card, say what they are for.
- **What each note rests on.** The note under the statements says what a safety data sheet gives for the solution at the strength on the bench, and has its own source, or two.
  - *Hydrochloric acid.* The label, 0.0850 to 0.1050 M, lies on both sides of the line between two of the same supplier's sheets: one for less than 0.1 M, which gives Warning, and one for 0.1 M to 2.4 M, which gives Danger and severe burns to the skin. The note gives both, says that the acid sits at the line between them, and says to handle it as the stricter sheet says. It does not call the acid mild.
  - *Sodium hydroxide.* The bench's solution, about 0.1 M, is under the same supplier's sheet for solutions of less than 0.3 M, which gives Warning and irritation of the skin and the eyes.
  - *Phenolphthalein.* The supplier's one sheet for a phenolphthalein solution is for a solution in alcohol alone, which is not the 0.5% solution in 50% alcohol the lab takes its bottle to be. So this note rests on another supplier's sheet, Ricca Chemical Company's, for a solution of that kind, and says that its sheet is a different supplier's. That the lab's bottle is such a solution is assumed.
- **The sheets are cited, not kept, so the build cannot re-check them.** Each sheet was read on the date its citation gives (2026-10-04 or 2026-10-05), and no copy is in the repository. The research record lists, row by row, what each sheet was read to print. The build holds the app's record to that list: every statement, in order, and the signal word. It cannot hold either of them to the sheets themselves. Whether the list is what the sheets print can be checked only by a person who reads the sheets, and a sheet that is revised later is not noticed. Two of the sheets, for the two solids, carry revision dates of 2014 and 2015; whether the supplier has later revisions was not looked into. None of the supplier's sheets says which regulation its classification is made under, and Valence does not say either.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the sheets.** The AI coding assistant that entered it, and then one independent AI check with the sheets loaded again. That check found that the first aid for a splash in the eyes was given shorter than the sheets give it, and made five other findings; what was written to fix them has been read by no independent reader. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- **First aid.** The notes and the primer's safety card say what the sheets say is to be worn, and what they say to do after a splash in the eyes or on the skin. The sheets give no length of time for the rinsing. The safety card says so, says that a laboratory sets its own rule and that a common one is 15 minutes at the eyewash, and says to follow the rule of the laboratory one is in. No source was read for the 15 minutes.
- **What is not covered.** The sheets' precautionary statements are not shown as such, and neither are two sentences on cancer listings that two of the sheets print outside their hazard statements. Nothing is said about first aid after breathing in or swallowing a solution, spills, storage, waste disposal, emergency equipment, or the choice of protective equipment for a particular laboratory. A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.
- **The exaggerated technique errors** of the titration lab (see Faculty and curriculum) are about the accuracy of a result. They are not about safety.
- **The mechanism timeline shows no hazard information.** It follows hydrochloric acid and sodium hydrogen carbonate at the scale of molecules and has no bench, no bottles and no amounts. Its first chapter calls the powder baking soda and says that the mixture fizzes; it says nothing of handling the acid.

**The aqueous reactions lab's reagents.**

- **Fifteen bottles.** Each reagent's tile on its station has the same "Hazards" disclosure. The statements are those of the same supplier's safety data sheets for the substance at full strength: for the hydrochloric acid at each of its three strengths, concentrated acid of 6 M to 12 M; for magnesium, the metal; for every other bottle, the solid the solution is made from. The heading over each says so. The note under them says what a sheet gives for the reagent as it is on the bench, and is headed "At the strength used here" for a solution and "As it is used here" for the calcium carbonate powder and the magnesium ribbon.
- **Where no sheet covers the strength, the note says so and rests on a stricter one.** Iron(III) chloride at 0.5 M lies between two of the supplier's sheets and is handled as the stricter says. The nickel chloride and cobalt chloride solutions on the bench are more concentrated than any solution those sheets cover, and are handled as the sheets for the solids say, which include statements that each may cause cancer and may harm fertility or an unborn child. Sodium hydroxide at 6 M is at the top of one sheet's range and is handled as the next says.
- **Calcium carbonate's sheet lists no hazard statement and no signal word.** Its tile says so in a sentence, in place of a signal word over an empty list, and its note gives what the sheet does say.
- **The sheets for cobalt chloride print no first aid for a splash in the eyes.** The note under that bottle says so and gives none. Whether it should give the words the supplier's other sheets carry is for the project's author; nothing was added.
- **First aid, and how long to rinse.** The notes say what each sheet says to wear and to do after a splash. The primer's safety card says that where a sheet gives first aid for the eyes it is rinsing for several minutes, kept up after that, with no number of minutes for the eyes; that the two sheets for iron(III) chloride solution also ask for flushing for 15 minutes or more without saying what is flushed; and that the cobalt chloride sheets print nothing for the eyes. It then says, as the titration lab's card does, that a laboratory sets its own rule, that a common one is 15 minutes at the eyewash, and to follow the rule of the laboratory one is in. No source was read for that rule: it is the author's statement.
- **Two cautions** are shown at the bench: on smelling a tube directly, which rests on a guide to prudent practice in laboratories, and on touching the hot tube of station 8, which rests on the two sheets for the 6 M acid and base.
- **The sheets are cited, not kept.** All 25 were read on 2026-10-05 or 2026-10-06 and no copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each sheet was read to print, statement by statement, and cannot hold either to the sheets themselves. A sheet revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the sheets.** The AI coding assistant that entered it. The repository holds no record of an independent check of it against the sheets, and no safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- What is not covered is as for the titration lab, above.

**The flame test lab's bottles.**

- **Six bottles.** Each solution's tile on its station has the same "Hazards" disclosure. The statements are those of the same supplier's safety data sheet for the solid salt, stored as the sheet prints them, and the heading over them says so: they are not the hazards of the 0.1 M solutions on the bench. Three of the six give the signal word Danger: barium chloride (toxic if swallowed), copper(II) chloride and strontium chloride.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the project's author's, of 2026-10-08. The supplier's sheets are the default reference. Where another official document that was read, a second or a third supplier's safety data sheet or a government record, is stricter on a hazard, a precaution or first aid, and what it says is about the substance, the note under the bottle goes by the stricter one and names it. A line that a supplier's form prints on every sheet whatever the substance, and a statement that the same sheet contradicts, are not carried: the research record keeps a row for each, saying what was read and why it was left out. Valence cannot know whose product is on a learner's shelf, so it sides with the strictest of the documents read; it does not say that its notes meet any rule for hazard communication.
- **What each note holds.** Three short paragraphs: what the supplier's sheet for the solution gives; what is stricter and which kind of document says it; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations, with the laboratory's supervisor to ask. The notes for the barium chloride and copper(II) chloride solutions also say that those two are kept out of drains and waterways. No disposal procedure of any one site is given.
- **First aid.** The notes give what the stricter document asks after a splash in the eyes. The barium chloride note also gives what the second supplier's sheet asks if any of the solution is swallowed, that a doctor or a poison centre be called at once; swallowing is the hazard that bottle's statements name first. That sentence had been left out to keep the note to a length, and was put back on 2026-10-08. Nothing is said about first aid after breathing a solution in, spills, storage, emergency equipment or the choice of protective equipment for a particular laboratory.
- **The burner.** The primer's safety card says that a burner is an open flame, what is tied back and cleared away, that a lit burner is not left alone, that the gas is shut off afterwards and the burner left to cool. It rests on a university's standard operating procedure for burners. That card's first paragraph says that nothing on a screen can burn or poison anyone and that the lab does not take the place of safety training.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Twenty-seven were read, on 2026-10-06 and 2026-10-08: the first supplier's sheets for each solid and each solution, a second supplier's sheets for five of the solutions, a third supplier's sheets for the solids, and records of a United States government database of chemicals. The six bottles' statements and notes cite 25 of them between them, and no copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, statement by statement, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves. A document revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistants that wrote it. The repository holds no record of an independent check of it against the documents, and no safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The calorimetry lab's reagents.**

- **Seven tiles.** The acid, the base and the ammonium nitrate each have a bottle's tile on the bench of their part, and each of the four metals a tile on the bench of part 3, all with the same "Hazards" disclosure. The acid and the base carry the aqueous reactions lab's records unchanged: the statements are those of concentrated hydrochloric acid and of solid sodium hydroxide, under a heading that says so, with a note on the 1 M solution on the bench. The statements for ammonium nitrate are those of the supplier's safety data sheet for the solid, which gives the signal word Warning and five statements. For each of the four metals as a solid piece the supplier's sheet lists no signal word and no hazard statement, and the tile says so in a sentence in their place. The water has no tile.
- **The unknown piece.** Until a run of part 3 counts, the learner does not know which of the four metals the piece is, so the bench shows the hazards of all four, under a heading that says why. Once it is known the bench shows that metal's alone.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above: the supplier's sheets are the default, and where another official document that was read is stricter on a hazard, a precaution or first aid, and what it says is about the substance, the note goes by the stricter one and says which kind of document it is.
- **What each of the five new notes holds.** Three short paragraphs: what the supplier's sheet gives for the form that is on the bench; what is stricter and which kind of document says it; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations, with the laboratory's supervisor to ask. Each metal's note says that the piece is not a powder, a dust or filings, which the documents treat as another matter. The note for zinc also says that it is kept out of drains and waterways and apart from ammonium nitrate, and those for aluminium and tin that the piece is kept from the acid of part 1. No disposal procedure of any one site is given.
- **First aid.** The five notes give what the stricter document asks after a splash in the eyes. Nothing is said of spills, of emergency equipment or of the choice of protective equipment for a particular laboratory.
- **Boiling water and a hot piece of metal.** No tile covers them. The primer's safety card says that the bath of part 3 is boiling water and that boiling water scalds, that on a real bench the piece is lifted with tongs or a holder and never with fingers and lowered into the cup, not dropped, that the bath stands where it cannot be knocked over, and that a hot piece of metal looks no different from a cold one. The lifting and the lowering rest on a page of a university's laboratory manual; the rest is common care at a bench, and no source was read for it, as the primer's own account of what that paragraph rests on says. That card's first paragraph says that nothing on a screen can burn or poison anyone and that the lab does not take the place of the safety training of a laboratory.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Twenty-five were read for the ammonium nitrate and the four metals, two of them first on 2026-10-06 and the rest on 2026-10-09: the first supplier's sheets for the solid, for its solution and for each metal, with its separate sheets for the powder or dust of aluminium, of iron and of zinc; a second and a third supplier's sheets; a metal maker's sheet for iron; and records of a United States government database of chemicals. The research record lists 22 of them as sources. The other three are the first supplier's sheets for a powder or a dust, read to tell the forms apart and named in the rows of the sheets for the pieces. The five tiles' statements and notes cite 21 of the 22 between them, and no copy is in the repository. (This paragraph said that twenty-two were read until the review of 2026-10-09 counted them.) The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves. A document revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistants that wrote it. The repository holds no record of an independent check of it against the documents, and no safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The Group I cations lab's bottles.**

- **Eight tiles, in one place.** The five reagents (hydrochloric acid, acetic acid, potassium chromate solution, ammonia solution and nitric acid) and the three solutions the samples are made from (silver nitrate, lead nitrate and mercury(I) nitrate) each have a bottle's tile with the same "Hazards" disclosure.
