They are not on every step's page: they are in "The bench", a disclosure that is closed, under the ways on of every step of both parts. The three nitrate solutions are all shown whichever of them an unknown holds, under a heading that says so. The water has no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** All eight give the signal word Danger. The hydrochloric acid carries the aqueous reactions lab's record unchanged: the statements of the supplier's sheet for concentrated acid of 6 M to 12 M, which covers the 6 M acid on this bench. For the acetic acid and the nitric acid the statements are those of the supplier's sheets for the concentrated acid; for the ammonia, of its sheet for solutions of 1.1 M to 14.8 M; for the potassium chromate, the silver nitrate and the lead nitrate, of its sheets for the solid. The heading over each says so. Potassium chromate's include that it may cause cancer and genetic defects, and lead nitrate's that it may cause cancer and may damage fertility or the unborn child.
- **Mercury(I) nitrate has no sheet from that supplier.** Its statements are another supplier's, for the solid, and include that it is fatal if swallowed, in contact with skin or if inhaled. No sheet for a solution of the salt could be read, and the note under the tile says that the solution is handled as the solid is.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above: the first supplier's sheets are the default, and where another official document that was read is stricter on a hazard, a precaution or first aid, and what it says is about the substance, the note goes by the stricter one and says which kind of document it is.
- **What each of the seven new notes holds.** Three short paragraphs: what a sheet gives for the solution on the bench, or that none could be read; what is stricter and which kind of document says it; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations, with the laboratory's supervisor to ask. The notes for the potassium chromate, the ammonia, the silver nitrate, the lead nitrate and the mercury(I) nitrate say that it is kept out of drains and waterways. No disposal procedure of any one site is given.
- **Things kept apart.** The notes for the acetic acid and the nitric acid say that the two are kept from each other, and the nitric acid's that it is kept from bases, metals and anything that burns. The notes for the ammonia and for the silver nitrate say that ammonia can form explosive or shock-sensitive compounds with silver and with mercury, by the documents read, and to ask the supervisor before any tube from steps 4 and 5 is set aside or kept. The primer's safety card says what the two documents say and marks as the lab's own caution, which is no source's, that on a real bench those tubes are not set aside or kept: no document read says what is to be done with such a tube. The nitric acid's note says that step 5 adds it to ammonia a little at a time.
- **First aid.** The seven notes give what the stricter document asks after a splash in the eyes, which is flushing for 15 minutes or more, with a doctor at once. Those for the potassium chromate, the silver nitrate and the lead nitrate also give what is asked if any is swallowed, and the mercury(I) nitrate's what is asked after any contact. Nothing is said of spills, of emergency equipment or of the choice of protective equipment for a particular laboratory.
- **The solids the steps make** have no tile: a learner does not know which of them a tube holds until a step is done. The primer's safety card says what the documents read say of them: that lead chloride and lead chromate may cause cancer, that mercury(I) chloride is toxic if swallowed, that mercury and mercury(II) amidochloride are each fatal if inhaled, and what two sheets list for silver chloride. That card's first paragraph says that nothing in the lab can hurt the learner and that a real bench of it holds lead, mercury, silver and chromate. It also says what the sheets call for at a real bench, that a hot tube is held with a holder and pointed away from everybody, and that a centrifuge is balanced and its lid kept shut until it has stopped.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Forty-nine were read, on 2026-10-06 and 2026-10-09: the first supplier's sheets, a second and a third supplier's, one sheet each of two other makers, and records of a United States government database of chemicals. The seven bottles' statements and notes cite 29 of them between them and the primer 14 more; the other six are in the research record as read, and nothing in the app cites them. No copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, statement by statement, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves. A document revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistants that wrote it. The repository holds no record of an independent check of it against the documents, and no safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The Le Chatelier's principle lab's bottles.**

- **Ten tiles, in one place.** Lead nitrate solution, hydrochloric acid at 0.3 M, 6 M and 12 M, methyl violet indicator solution, solid cobalt(II) chloride hexahydrate, zinc nitrate solution, magnesium nitrate solution, sodium hydroxide solution and ammonia solution each have a bottle's tile with the same "Hazards" disclosure. They are not on every disturbance's page: they are in "The bench", a disclosure that is closed, under the ways on of every entry of all four systems, and all ten are there whichever system is shown. The water and the two baths have no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** Nine give the signal word Danger and the indicator Warning. The 6 M hydrochloric acid and the sodium hydroxide carry the aqueous reactions lab's records unchanged. For the other two strengths of hydrochloric acid the statements are those of the supplier's sheet for concentrated acid of 6 M to 12 M; for the ammonia, of its sheet for solutions of 1.1 M to 14.8 M; for the lead nitrate, the zinc nitrate and the magnesium nitrate, of its sheets for the solid; for the indicator, of its sheet for the solid dye; and for the cobalt(II) chloride, which is on the bench as the solid, of its sheet for that. The heading over each says so. Lead nitrate's include that it may cause cancer and may damage fertility or the unborn child, and cobalt(II) chloride's that it may cause cancer, is suspected of causing genetic defects and may damage fertility or the unborn child.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above: the first supplier's sheets are the default, and where another official document that was read is stricter on a hazard, a precaution or first aid, and what it says is about the substance, the note goes by the stricter one and says which kind of document it is.
- **What each of the eight new notes holds.** Three short paragraphs: what a sheet gives for the bottle as it is on the bench; what is stricter and which kind of document says it; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations, with the laboratory's supervisor to ask. The notes for the lead nitrate, the indicator, the cobalt(II) chloride, the zinc nitrate and the ammonia say that it is kept out of drains and waterways. No disposal procedure of any one site is given.
- **Things kept apart.** The note for the 12 M acid says that it fumes and is used in a fume hood, and that by a government record it reacts violently with sodium hydroxide and with ammonia solution, both on this bench, and is kept apart from them; the ammonia's says that it is kept from acids, of which the bench has three strengths. The lab's own procedure adds 6 M acid and 6 M base to one tube, a few drops at a time, and 6 M acid to tubes that hold sodium hydroxide.
- **First aid.** The eight notes give what the stricter document asks after a splash in the eyes, which is flushing for 15 minutes or more, with a doctor; most give what is asked if any is swallowed. Nothing is said of spills, of emergency equipment or of the choice of protective equipment for a particular laboratory.
- **The solids the lab makes, and the acid with water.** Lead chloride, zinc hydroxide and magnesium hydroxide come from no bottle and have no tile. The primer's safety card says what the documents read say of them, among it that lead chloride may cause cancer and may harm fertility or an unborn child. It says that concentrated hydrochloric acid gives off heat when water is added to it, so that a real tube of system 3 warms, and that a sheet for a cobalt salt in weaker acid gives that liquid the hazards of both. Its first paragraph says that nothing in the lab can hurt the learner and that a real bench of it holds salts of lead and of cobalt, concentrated acid, and strong solutions of sodium hydroxide and of ammonia. Nothing is said of the bath of boiling water.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Forty-two were read for this lab, on 2026-10-05, 2026-10-06, 2026-10-09 and 2026-10-10: the first supplier's sheets, a second and a third supplier's, one sheet of another maker, and records of a United States government database of chemicals. The eight bottles' statements and notes cite 28 of them between them and the primer's safety card 8 more; the other six are in the research record as read, and nothing in the app cites them. No copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, statement by statement, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves. A document revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistants that wrote it; and, at the review of 2026-10-11, the reviewing assistant read the statements of four of the ten bottles (lead nitrate, cobalt(II) chloride, methyl violet and ammonia) against the first supplier's sheets, in the copies saved when they were read, and found each as stored. It read no note against its documents, and thirty-eight of the forty-two documents not at all. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.

**The clock reaction lab's bottles.**

- **Five tiles, in one place.** Potassium iodide solution, sodium thiosulfate solution, potassium bromate solution, hydrochloric acid and starch solution each have a bottle's tile with the same "Hazards" disclosure. They are in "The bench", a disclosure that is closed, under the ways on of every run and of both notebooks, and all five are there whichever part is shown. The water and the three baths have no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** Each bottle is a dilute solution, and the statements are those of the first supplier's sheet for the solid or, for the acid, for concentrated acid of 6 M to 12 M; the heading over each says so. Potassium bromate's give the signal word Danger: it may cause fire or explosion as a strong oxidizer, is toxic if swallowed, and may cause cancer. Hydrochloric acid's give Danger, and potassium iodide's Warning. The sheets read for sodium thiosulfate and for starch list no hazard statement, and the tile says so in place of a signal word.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above: the first supplier's sheets are the default, and where another official document that was read is stricter on a hazard, a precaution or first aid, and what it says is about the substance, the note goes by the stricter one and says which kind of document it is.
- **What each note holds.** What a sheet gives for the bottle as it is on the bench; what is stricter and which kind of document says it; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations, with the laboratory's supervisor to ask. No disposal procedure of any one site is given.
- **The primer's safety card** says that nothing in the lab can hurt the learner and what a real bench of it holds, potassium bromate above all, and what the documents read say of the iodine the reaction makes.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Thirty-two are listed in the lab's hazard record as read, on the dates their citations give: sheets of three suppliers, a fact sheet of a state government and records of a United States government database of chemicals. No copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves. A document revised later is not noticed.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistants that wrote it. The review of 2026-10-11 read the five notes as sentences a learner sees and did not read them against the documents, but for the strength of the starch solution: no document read gives the about 2% that the note had, and the note now gives none. The repository holds no record of an independent check of the hazard text against the documents, and no safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The iodination of acetone lab's bottles.**

- **Three tiles, in one place.** Acetone solution, hydrochloric acid and iodine solution each have a bottle's tile with the same "Hazards" disclosure. They are in "The bench", a disclosure that is closed, under the ways on of every run and of the notebook. The water has no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** Each bottle is a solution in water, and the statements are those of the first supplier's sheet for pure acetone, for concentrated hydrochloric acid of 6 M to 12 M, and for solid iodine; the heading over each says so. Acetone's give the signal word Danger: a highly flammable liquid and vapour. Hydrochloric acid's give Danger, and iodine's Warning. The note under each says what a sheet gives for the solution as it is on the bench, what is stricter and which kind of document says it, and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations.
- **The primer's safety card** says that nothing in the lab can hurt the learner and what a real bench of it holds, the acetone above all, which burns, and that no official document that could be read says what the product, iodoacetone, does to a person.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it, apart from the rest of the lab. The screens' builder drew the tiles from the record and read none of the notes against their documents. The review of 2026-10-11 read the notes and the primer's safety card as sentences, and not against their documents; it noted that the acetone's note reasons from a sheet for a 50% solution to the bench's own, which is about 29% by volume. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The measurement and density lab's unknowns.**

- **Five tiles, on the bench's page.** Part 2's bench has a tile for each of the two liquids its unknown may be, ethanol of 95% and sodium chloride solution of 20%, and part 3's one for each of the three metals, aluminium, zinc and copper, each with the same "Hazards" disclosure, under a heading that says the unknown is to be handled as all of them say until it is named. Once a run of the part counts, the heading names what the unknown was and one tile is left. The water, the paper note and the ice have no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** The ethanol's are those of the first supplier's sheet for its denatured ethyl alcohol, which is ethanol with other alcohols added, and the heading over them says so: the signal word Danger, a highly flammable liquid and vapour, harmful if swallowed, irritation of the skin, the eyes and the airways, and damage to organs; one of the five is for the methanol the product holds. The sodium chloride's is the one statement of that supplier's sheet for the solid, with Warning; its sheet for the solution prints none, and the note says so. The sheets read for the three metals as pieces print no signal word and no statement, and each tile says that in place of a list.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above: the first supplier's sheets are the default, and where another official document that was read is stricter, and what it says is about the substance, the note goes by the stricter one and says which kind of document it is. Each note is three short paragraphs: what the thing on this bench is and what the sheet says of it; what is stricter; and that disposal is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations.
- **The notes can be read before the unknown is named, and say nothing the bench shows that differs with what the unknown is.** As first written the metals' notes said how many grams of each the bench holds, three ranges that differ, so a learner who opened them after weighing the pieces could tell the metal without measuring a volume. The lab's review of 2026-10-12 took the amounts out, which the owner had decided; each note now says dry pieces of its metal, slid into cold water. The two liquids' notes give the same 20 to 45 mL. An automated test holds that no note carries a figure in a unit the bench reads unless every other note of that unknown carries the same one.
- **The documents are cited, not kept.** Fourteen are behind these five records: sheets of three suppliers and records of a United States government database of chemicals. Ten of them were read by the flame test and calorimetry labs for the same substances in another form, and four were read new, for the ethanol and for copper; their records are not shared, since a piece of metal heated in boiling water and a 0.1 mol/L solution are not what is on this bench. No copy is in the repository. The build holds the app's record to the research record's list of what each was read to print, and cannot hold either to the documents themselves.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it. The screens' builder drew the tiles from the record and read none of the notes against their documents. The lab's reviewer, an AI too, read the five notes, changed the first sentence of the three metals' as said above, and did not read any note again against its documents. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has reviewed any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The freezing point depression lab's bench.**

- **Seven tiles, on the bench's card.** Part 2 has a tile for each of the three liquids its unknown may be, acetone, ethanol and 2-propanol, each with the same "Hazards" disclosure, under a heading that says the unknown is to be treated as all three until it is named. Once a run of the part counts, the heading names the liquid and one tile is left. Part 3 has a tile for each of urea, sodium chloride, potassium chloride and sodium bromide until one is chosen for the run, and then that one's. The ice and the water have no tile.
- **What the statements classify.** Each is the first supplier's sheet for the pure substance, and the heading over it says so; the ethanol's is that supplier's sheet for denatured ethyl alcohol, which is ethanol with other alcohols added, since it has none for pure ethanol. The three liquids' give the signal word Danger: each a highly flammable liquid and vapour, with irritation of the eyes; the denatured alcohol's add that it is harmful if swallowed and damages organs. The sheet for urea prints no statement, and the tile says so. The two chlorides' give Warning, with one statement each, and sodium bromide's Warning with two.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above. Each note is three short paragraphs and gives no method of disposal, which is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations. The three liquids' notes are in the same words and give no amount, so that a note read before the unknown is named tells nothing of which it is; an automated test holds that the page of part 2 does not differ with the liquid.
- **Sodium bromide's note rests on one sheet.** A United States government database of chemicals has no record of it, and no second supplier's sheet was looked for.
- **The primer's safety card** says that the lab is a simulation, that nothing in it can hurt the learner and that it does not take the place of safety training; and that until the unknown is named it is to be treated as each of the three liquids it may be, all of which burn readily.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it. The screens' builder drew the tiles from the record and read none of the notes against their documents. The lab's review, by an AI reviewer on 2026-10-12, read every note as a learner sees it and looked for anything in a note or a tile that tells the unknown liquid, and found nothing; it held the statements and the notes that rest on the three sheets read new for this lab (2-propanol, urea, sodium bromide) and on two of the government database's records to the saved copies of those documents, and they agreed. It did not read again the documents the other four notes share with earlier labs. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has read any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The types of reactions lab's bench.**

- **Nine tiles, in one place.** The bench is at the foot of every station's page, behind a disclosure: what the station shown uses, that eye protection and gloves are worn at every station, and a tile for each of the lab's nine things, each with the same "Hazards" disclosure: zinc, magnesium and copper as metal; 1 M copper(II) sulfate, hydrochloric acid, calcium chloride and sodium carbonate; 0.5 M iron(III) chloride; and 3% hydrogen peroxide.
- **What the statements classify.** Each is the first supplier's sheet, and the heading over it says for what: for five of the six solutions it is the sheet for the solid or for the concentrated acid, and the peroxide's is the sheet for a solution of 2% to 6%. The hydrochloric acid and the iron(III) chloride carry the aqueous reactions lab's records unchanged.
- **The rule the notes are written to** is the one given for the flame test lab's bottles, above. Each note of this lab's own is three short paragraphs and gives no method of disposal, which is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations.
- **Burning magnesium.** The primer's safety card says that its light is bright enough to hurt the eyes and that the strip is held in tongs; the card and the metal's note say that a magnesium fire is never put out with water, and that dry sand is used. The page's task for that station says not to look straight at the strip while it burns.
- **Hydrogen from the metals in acid.** The shared note of the 1 M hydrochloric acid says nothing of the hydrogen it gives with metals. The primer's safety card says that the gas is hydrogen, that it burns, and to keep flames from those tubes; the notes of zinc and of magnesium say that each gives hydrogen with acids.
- **Three notes rest on few documents:** the peroxide's on the first supplier's sheet alone, the sodium carbonate's on that supplier's two sheets, and the copper(II) sulfate's on that supplier's two sheets and a government record of the solid. No document that was read classes copper(II) sulfate as a hazard to water life, so its note says nothing of drains; other suppliers' sheets, which were not read, may.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it. The screens' builder drew the tiles from the record and read none of the notes against their documents. The lab's review read the nine notes and, against its saved sheet, the peroxide's signal word and statements; it reworded two notes that said what a station shows (the copper's, and the peroxide's) and kept what each sheet asks. The notes of zinc and of magnesium say that each gives hydrogen with acids, which is also what one station asks the learner to find; that was left, as a hazard of the metal. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has read any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The polarity and mixing lab's bench.**

- **One tile.** The rubbing alcohol is the one bottle of this bench that is a laboratory chemical. Its tile is on the bench, which is behind a disclosure at the foot of every part and every tube, with the same "Hazards" disclosure as every other lab's bottle.
- **What the statements classify.** The signal word, Danger, and the four statements are those of the first supplier's sheet for 2-propanol of 40% to 90% in water, and the heading over them says so. That sheet has one statement more, for the skin, than the same supplier's sheet for the alcohol alone, on which the freezing point depression lab's record rests; so this lab's record is its own.
- **The note** gives that sheet's precautions and, where a government record of the alcohol is stricter (the vapour, splashed skin, the eyes), that record's. The sheet lists acids among what the alcohol is kept from, and this bench puts it in a tube with vinegar: the note says so and gives no opinion. It gives no method of disposal, which is decided by the laboratory's own rules and by local regulations.
- **Everything else on the bench** (the water, the vinegar, the two oils, the salt and the sugar) shares one note, the red cabbage pH lab's for foods on a bench, word for word: nothing on a laboratory bench is tasted, the maker's label is read, and eye protection is worn. A test holds the two labs' notes to each other.
- **The primer's safety card** is one of the four cards marked to read first, on the tab the page opens on before the lab is started. It says that rubbing alcohol and its vapour catch fire easily.
- **The mineral oil has no hazards of its own on this bench.** The first supplier's sheet classes it as not hazardous. A government record of it, which the lab cites for its density, calls it combustible and a mild irritant of the eyes and warns of breathing it in. The note it shares has eye protection worn and nothing tasted, and the primer has no flame on the bench. The lab's review found this and left it for the owner.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it; and, at the review of 2026-10-12, the reviewing assistant read the rubbing alcohol's signal word, its four statements and its note against the saved text of the supplier's sheet, and did not read the note's lines from the government record against that record. The screens' builder drew the tile from the record and read the note against no document. No safety officer, chemistry instructor or chemist has read any of it.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.

**The endothermic and exothermic lab's bench.**

- **Two tiles,** one for each salt, each on the bench's card of its own part, with the same "Hazards" disclosure as every other lab's bottle. Both records are this lab's own: the solids as they are weighed out here.
- **What the statements classify.** Epsom salt's tile has no signal word and no statement: the first supplier's sheet for magnesium sulfate heptahydrate classes it as not hazardous, and the heading over the tile says whose hazards they are. Calcium chloride's has the signal word Warning and the two statements of the first supplier's sheet for the anhydrous solid: harmful if swallowed, and causes serious eye irritation.
- **The notes** give each sheet's precautions and, where another document that was read is stricter, that document's. For Epsom salt, a second supplier's sheet: no dust raised or breathed, and 15 minutes of rinsing for the eyes; the note also says that the first supplier's sheet calls the salt an irritant in its section on toxicology. For calcium chloride, a government record: the dust and the eyes, the solid and the skin, 15 minutes of washing for the eyes and an eye doctor; and a second supplier's sheet, for the same 15 minutes for the skin. Each note is three short paragraphs of about 140 words. Neither gives a method of disposal.
- **One thing the documents say is kept out of the tile.** The first supplier's sheet says what calcium chloride does to the temperature of water, and the government record tells of the salt added to hot water and of violent boiling. A tile is shown before the run, and which way this salt's temperature goes is what the learner is there to find, so neither is carried into it; the hazard manifest records both as read and not carried, with why. The primer's safety card, which is marked to read first, says of no salt by name that only the portion weighed out is used and only water at room temperature, since a salt that gives heat out can make hot water boil up; and the part's notes tell of the case once the run counts. Whether a safety statement should be held back from a tile for this reason is the owner's to decide.
- **No document was fetched for this lab's hazards.** Four of the five are documents that the red cabbage pH lab and the flame test lab list; each saved page was checked against its recorded hash and read again. The fifth, the first supplier's sheet for Epsom salt, had been saved on 2026-10-12 by the builder of the red cabbage pH lab and not cited; it was checked against the hash in the log of that fetch.
- **Who has read the hazard text against the documents.** The AI coding assistant that wrote it, and nobody else.
- A laboratory's own safety data sheets, for the products it actually stocks, and its own rules take precedence over anything shown in Valence.
